Summary

Based on the book 'The love hypothesis' by Ali Hazelwood.

When Lena finds herself caught in a lie to help her friend get with her ex, she does what any normal person would, she continues to lie.

✓♥︎

The Love Hypothesis

by Elle_08

https://archiveofourown.org/works/38204932

    The Girl

    hy·poth·e·sis (noun)

    A supposition or proposed explanation made on the basis of limited evidence, as a starting point for further investigation.

    Example: “Based on the available information and the data hitherto collected, my hypothesis is that the farther away I stay from love, the better off I will be.”

    Honestly, Lena was a bit on the fence about this whole grad school thing. Not because she didn’t like science. (She did. She loved science. Science was her thing.) It wasn’t the enormous amount of work she would have to put in, the time she would have to sacrifice, the underappreciation she would have to accept, no those things she could learn to deal with. it was something else that held her back from surrendering herself to the hell that is a Ph.D. program. A question that has held her back from making a decision, that is until she came across The Girl.

    The Girl whose name she never really got. The Girl she met after stumbling blindly into the first bathroom she could find. The Girl who asked her, “Out of curiosity, is there a specific reason you’re crying in the restroom?”

    Lena jumped. She tried to open her eyes through the tears and only barely managed to. Her entire line of sight was blurry. All she could see was a watery outline-someone tall, light-haired, probably blonde, and…yeah. That was all she could see.

    “I…is this the ladies’ room?” Lena asked.

    There was a moment of silence before this mystery person answered with a single word, ‘no’. her voice was…dreamy.

    “Are you sure?” Lena asked.

    “Yes.”

    “Really?”

    “Certain since this is my lab’s bathroom.”

    Oh.

    “I’m so sorry. Do you need to…” she gestured toward the stall, or where she assumed the stalls to be. Her eyes burned, even when closed and she had to scrunch them to dull the burn.

    “I just need to pour this reagent down the drain. We don’t use this as a restroom, just to dispose of waste and wash equipment.”

    “Oh, sorry I thought…” honestly, she didn’t really think, not with her eyes burning like the bush God used to speak to Moses.

    “Are you okay?”

    “Yes, why do you ask?”

    “Because you are crying. In my bathroom.”

    “Oh, I’m not really crying. Well. I sort of am, but it’s just tears, you know?”

    “I do not.”

    Lena sighed and rested against the bathroom wall. “It’s my contacts. They expired some time ago, and they were never that great to begin with. they messed up my eyes. I’ve taken them off, but it takes a while to get better,” she explained.

    “You put in expired contacts?”

    “Just a little expired,” Lena tried to reason.

    “How long is ‘a little’?”

    “I don’t know. A few years,” Lena shrugged.

    “What?”

    “Only just a couple I think.”

    “Just a couple of ‘years’?”

    “It’s okay. Expiration dates are for the weak.”

    “I’m pretty sure they are so I don’t find you weeping in the corner of my bathroom.”

    “It’s fine,” Lena waved her hand. She would have rolled her eyes if they weren’t on fire. “The burning only lasts a few minutes.”

    “You’ve done this before?”

    Lena frowned, “Done what?”

    “Put in expired contacts.”

    “Of course. Contacts aren’t cheap,” Lena answered.

    “Neither are eyes.”

    Good point.

    “Have we met before? Maybe last night, at the recruitment dinner with prospective Ph.D. students?” Lena asked.

    “No.”

    “You weren’t there?” Lena asked.

    “Not really my scene.”

    “But the free food?”

    “Not worth the small talk. Are you interviewing for a spot in the program?”

    “Yes. For next year’s Biology cohort. What about you?”

    “Me?”

    “How long have you been here?”

    There was another moment of silence.

    “Six years.”

    “Oh, are you graduating soon then?” Lena asked. Before receiving an answer she face palmed herself. “Don’t answer that, first rule of grad school- don’t ask about other grads’ dissertation timeline.”

    Another moment of silence.

    “I’m Lena by the way,” she stretched out her hand, hoping it wasn’t to a trashcan.

    The hand that closed her around hers was warm, large and so, so soft.

    Lena broke the silence once more. “If you happen to talk with anyone who’s on the admissions committee would you mind not mentioning my contacts mishap? It might make me look like a less-than-stellar applicant.”

    “You think so?”

    She would have glared at her if she could.

    “Are you planning to enroll?”

    Lena shrugged. “I might not get an offer.”

    She was selling herself short, her GRE scores and GPA were almost perfect. Not having a life had to account for something.

    “Are you planning to enroll if you do get an offer?”

    She’d be stupid not to. The was Stanford.

    “I…maybe. I just…”

    “You just?”

    Lena bit her lip. “What if I’m not good enough?” There she said it. The question that’s been holding her back. God only knows why she just blurted out her deepest insecurity to a complete stranger. Must have something to do with not knowing what said stranger looks like. Everyone in her life keeps telling her how brilliant she is. That she will go far in life of she puts her mind to it. Surely this person would say the same.

    “Why do you want to do it?”

    Okay, not what Lena expected. “Do what?”

    “Get a Ph.D.?”

    Lena cleared her throat. “I’ve always had an inquisitive mind, and graduate school is an ideal environment to foster that. It will give me important transferable skills-”

    “Not the line you found in an interview prep book. Why do you want a Ph.D.?”

    “It’s true,” she insisted weakly. “I want to sharpen my research abilities-”

    “Is it because you don’t know what else to do?”

    “No.”

    “Because you didn’t get an industry position?”

    “No, I didn’t even apply for industry.”

    “Ah.”

    A single word and yet it infuriated Lena.

    “I need more freedom than industry can offer,” she said.

    "You won’t have much freedom in academia. You’ll have to fund your work through ludicrously competitive research grants. You’d make better money in a 9-5 job that actually allows you to entertain the concept of weekends.”

    Lena pouted. “Are you trying to get me to decline my offer?”

    “No.”

    For some reason Lena believed her.

    “So why do you want a Ph.D. Lena?”

    Lena bit her lip and squeezed her eyes tighter. “I-I have a question. A specific research question. Something that I want to find out. Something I’m afraid no one else will discover if I don’t.”

    “A question?”

    “Yes,” she nodded her head. “A question that is important to me. And- I don’t trust anyone else to do it. Because they haven’t so far. Because…” because something bad happened. Because I want to do my part, so it doesn’t happen again. “It’s important to me,” she repeated. Lena cleared her throat and looked at up to where she assumed the other person was standing, “Is mine a good enough reason to go to grad school?”

    “It’s the best one. Good luck with your interview Lena.”

    “Thanks. Maybe I’ll see you next year. You know if I get in and you haven’t graduated.”

    “Maybe.”

    And with that, she heard the door open and close. The Girl was gone. Lena never got her name.

    A few weeks later, when the Stanford department of Biology extended her an offer, she accepted it. without hesitating.

    The Night

    Chapter Notes

    HYPOTHESIS: When given a choice between A (a slightly inconveniencing situation) and B (a colossal shitshow with devastating consequences), I will inevitably end up selecting B.

    Two years, eleven months later

    In Lena’s defense, the woman didn’t seem to mind the kiss too much. It did take the woman a moment to adjust—perfectly understandable, given the sudden circumstances. It was an awkward, uncomfortable, somewhat painful minute, in which Lena was simultaneously smashing her lips against someone else’s and pushing herself as high as her toes would extend to keep her mouth at the same level as that person’s face. Did they have to be so tall? The kiss must have looked like some clumsy headbutt, and she grew anxious that she was not going to be able to pull the whole thing off. Her friend Andrea, whom Lena had spotted coming her way a few seconds ago, was going to take one look at this and know at once that Lena and Kiss Woman couldn’t possibly be two people in the middle of a date.

    Then that agonizingly slow moment went by, and the kiss became . . . different. The woman inhaled sharply and inclined her head a tiny bit, making Lena feel less like a squirrel monkey climbing a baobab tree, and their hands—which were large and pleasantly warm in the AC of the hallway—closed around her waist. It slid up a few inches, coming to wrap around Lena’s rib cage and holding her closer. Not too close, and not too far.

    It was more of a prolonged peck than anything, but it was quite nice, and for the life span of a few seconds Lena forgot a large number of things, including the fact that she was pressed against a random, unknown woman. That she’d barely had the time to whisper, “Can I please kiss you?” before locking lips with her. That what had originally driven her to put on this entire show was the hope of fooling Andrea, one of her best friends in the whole world.

    But a good kiss will do that: make a girl forget herself for a while. Lena found herself melting into a broad, solid chest that showed absolutely no give. Her hands travelled from a defined jaw into surprisingly thick and soft hair, and then— then she heard herself sigh, as if already out of breath, and that’s when it hit her like a brick on the head, the realization that— No. No. Nope, nope, no

    She should not be enjoying this. Random woman, and all that. Lena gasped and pushed herself away from her, frantically looking for Andrea. In the 11:00 p.m. bluish glow of the biology labs’ hallway, her friend was nowhere to be seen. Weird. Lena was sure she had spotted her a few seconds earlier. Kiss Woman, on the other hand, was standing right in front of her, lips parted, chest rising and a weird light flickering in her eyes, which was exactly when it dawned on her, the enormity of what she had just done. Of who she had just— Fuck her life.
    Fuck.
    Her.
    Life.

    Because Dr. Kara Zor-El was a known ass.

    This fact was not remarkable in and of itself, as in academia every position above the graduate student level (Lena’s level, sadly) required some degree of assness in order to be held for any length of time, with tenured faculty at the very peak of the ass pyramid. Dr. Zor-El, though—she was exceptional. At least if the rumours were anything to go by.

    Dr. Zor-El might have been a young academic rock star and biology’s wunderkind, but she was also mean and hypercritical, and it was obvious in the way she spoke, in the way she carried herself, that she thought herself the only person doing decent science within the Stanford biology department. Within the entire world, probably. She was a notoriously moody, obnoxious, terrifying ass.

    And Lena just kissed her.

    She wasn’t sure how long the silence lasted—only that Dr Zor-El was the one to break it. She stood in front of Lena, ridiculously intimidating for anyone with blue eyes and blonde hair, staring down from who knows how many inches above six feet—she must have been over half a foot taller than Lena.

    Kara Zor-El. Destroyer of research careers, Lena had once overheard her adviser say.

    “Did you…did you just kiss me?” Dr Zor-El asked puzzled. Her lips were red and puffy and… no not going there.

    “Nope,” Lena answered.

    “Ah. Okay, then,” Dr Zor-El nodded and turned around, looking vaguely disoriented. She took a couple of steps down the hallway, reached the water fountain—maybe where she’d been headed in the first place.

    Lena thought she was off the hook until Dr Zor-El turned around and asked, “Are you sure?”

    Fuck.

    “I-” Lena wasn’t exactly sure what to say. “It’s not what it looks like.”

    “Okay. I . . . Okay,” Dr Zor-El repeated slowly. Her voice was deep and low and sounded a lot like she was on her way to getting mad. Like maybe she was already mad. “What’s going on here?”

    Lena let out a sigh. There really was no way of getting out of this. Well, she could try couldn’t she?

    “Look, not to be rude but it’s not your business.”

    Dr Zor-El stared at her for a moment then nodded. “Okay. I’ll just go back to my officer and file my Title IX complaint.”

    Lena exhaled in relief. “Yeah. That would be great, since — Wait. Your what?”

    “Title IX is a federal law that protects against sexual misconduct within academic settings—”

    “I know what Title IX is,” Lena said, cutting her off.

    “I see. So you wilfully chose to disregard it.” Dr Zor-El raised an eyebrow.

    Lena looked at her baffled. “I— What? No. No, I didn’t!”

    Dr Zor-El shrugged. “I must be mistaken, then. Someone else must have assaulted me.”

    “I did not assault you.”

    “You did kiss me.”

    “But not really.”

    “Without first securing my consent.”

    “I asked if I could kiss you!”

    “And then did so without waiting for my response.”

    “What? You said yes.”

    “Excuse me?”

    Lena frowned. “I asked if I could kiss you, and you said yes.”

    “Incorrect. You asked if you could kiss me, and I snorted.”

    “I’m pretty sure I heard you said yes.” Lena realised she could have been mistaken in the rush of the moment. “I’m really sorry. It was a weird situation. Can we just forget that this happened?”

    “Is there something wrong?” Dr Zor-El sounded almost concerned.

    “What? No. No, there isn’t.”

    “Because,” she continued calmly, “kissing a stranger at midnight in a science lab might be a sign that there is.”

    “There isn’t.”

    Dr Zor-El nodded, thoughtful. “Very well. Expect mail in the next few days, then.” she began to walk past Lena, and Lena turned to yell after her.

    “You didn’t even ask my name!”

    “I’m sure anyone could figure it out, since you must have swiped your badge to get in the labs area after hours. Have a good night.”

    “Wait!” Lena leaned forward and stopped her with a hand on her wrist. Dr Zor-El paused immediately, even though it was obvious that it would take her no effort to free herself. She let go of Dr Zor-El at once and took one step back. “Sorry, I didn’t mean to—”

    “The kiss. Explain.”

    Lena bit into her lower lip. She had truly screwed herself over. She had to tell her, now. “Andrea Rojas.” She looked around to make sure Andrea was really gone. “The girl who was passing by. She’s a graduate student in the biology department.”

    Dr Zor-El gave no indication of knowing who Andrea was.

    “Andrea has . . .” Lena pushed a strand of hair behind her ear. This was where the story became embarrassing. Complicated, and a little juvenile sounding. “I was seeing this guy in the department. Jack Spheer, he works with Dr. . . . Anyway, we went out just a couple of times, and then I brought him to Andrea’s birthday party, and they just sort of hit it off and—”

    Lena shut her eyes. Which was probably a bad idea, because now she could see it painted on her lids, how her best friend and her date had bantered in that bowling alley, as if they’d known each other their whole lives; the never exhausted topics of conversation, the laughter, and then, at the end of the night, Jack was following Andrea’s every move with his gaze. It had been painfully clear who he was interested in. Lena waved a hand and tried for a smile. “Long story short, after Jack and I ended things, he asked Andrea out. She said no because of . . . girl code and all that, but I can tell that she really likes him. She’s afraid to hurt my feelings, and no matter how many times I told her it was fine she wouldn’t believe me.”

    Lena left out the part where she overheard Andrea confess to their friend Sam that she thought Jack was awesome, but she could never betray Lena by going out with him, and she sounded so dejected. Disappointed and insecure, not at all like the spunky, larger-than-life person Andrea was.

    “So I just lied and told her that I was already dating someone else. Because she’s one of my closest friends and I’d never seen her like a guy this much and I want her to have the good things she deserves and I’m positive that she would do the same for me and—” Lena realized that she was rambling and that Dr Zor-El couldn’t have cared less. She stopped and swallowed, even though her mouth felt dry. “Tonight. I told her I’d be on a date tonight.”

    “Ah.” Dr Zor-El’s expression was unreadable.

    “But I’m not. So I decided to come in to work on an experiment, but Andrea showed up, too. She wasn’t supposed to be here. But she was. Coming this way. And I panicked— well.” Lena wiped a hand down her face. “I didn’t really think. I just needed her to believe that I was on a date.”

    Dr Zor-El nodded. “So you kissed the first person you saw in the hallway. Perfectly logical.”

    Lena winced. “It sounds bad when you put it like that. Not my best moment”

    “You think?”

    “But it wasn’t my worst, either! I’m pretty sure Andrea saw us. Now she’ll think that I was on a date with you, and she’ll hopefully feel free to go out with Jack and—” Lena shook her head. “Listen. I’m so, so sorry about the kiss.”

    “Are you?”

    “Please, don’t report me. I really thought I heard you say yes. I promise I didn’t mean to . . .” Suddenly it all hit her. She kissed someone without their consent. “Actually, you’re absolutely right. And I am so sorry. If you felt in any way harassed by me, you really should report me, because it’s only fair. It was a horrible thing to do, though I really didn’t want to . . . Not that my intentions matter; it’s more like your perception of . . .” Fuck, she really messed up, didn’t she? “I’m going to leave now, okay? Thank you, and . . . I am so, so, so sorry.” Lena turned around and ran away down the hallway.

    “Lena,” she heard Dr Zor-El call after her. “Lena, wait—”

    She didn’t stop. She sprinted down the stairs to the first floor and then out the building and across the pathways of the sparsely lit Stanford campus, running past a girl walking her dog and a group of students laughing in front of the library. She continued until she was standing in front of her apartment’s door, stopping only to unlock it, making a beeline for her room in the hope of avoiding her roommate and whoever she might have brought home tonight.

    Lena threw herself on her bed and stared up at the glow-in-the-dark stars stuck on her ceiling and let herself cry.

    What an absolute shit of a day.

    It didn’t occur to Lena that Dr Zor-El had called her by her name.

    Chapter End Notes

    Okay I know that calling Kara 'Zor-El' is weird since it's technically her father's name in the show, but it will all make sense eventually.

    The Confrontation

    Chapter Notes

    HYPOTHESIS: In a situation where I can come clean or continue to lie, I will choose the latter.

    Lena was a rising third-year Ph.D. student in one of the best biology departments in the country, one that housed more than one hundred grads and what often felt like several million majoring undergrads. She had no idea what the exact number of faculty was but judging from the mailboxes in the copy room she’d say that a safe guess was: too many. Therefore, she reasoned that if she’d never had the misfortune of interacting with Kara Zor-El in the two years before ‘The Night’ (it had been only a handful of days since the kissing incident, but Lena already knew that she’d think of last Friday as The Night for the rest of her life), it was entirely possible that she might be able to finish grad school without crossing paths with Kara Zor-El ever again. In fact, she was fairly sure that not only did Kara Zor-El have no idea who she was, but she also had no desire to learn—and had probably already forgotten all about what happened.

    Unless, of course, she was catastrophically wrong and Kara Zor-El did end up filing a Title IX lawsuit. In which case she supposed that she would see her again, when she pleaded guilty in federal court.

    Lena figured that she could waste her time fretting about legal fees, or she could focus on what were more pressing issues. Like the approximately five hundred slides she had to prepare for the neurobiology class that she was slated to TA in the fall semester, which was starting in less than two weeks. Or the note Sam had left this morning, telling her she’d seen a cockroach scurry under the credenza even though their apartment was already full of traps. Or the most crucial one: the fact that her research project had reached a critical point and she desperately needed to find a bigger, significantly richer lab to carry out her experiment. Otherwise, what could very well become a ground-breaking, clinically relevant study might end up languishing on a handful of petri dishes stacked in the crisper drawer of her fridge.

    Lena opened her laptop with half a mind to google ‘Organs one can live without’ and ‘How much cash for them’ but got side-tracked by the twenty new emails she’d received while busy with her lab animals. They were almost exclusively from predatory journals, Nigerian prince wannabes, and one glitter company whose newsletter she’d signed up for six years ago to get a free tube of lipstick. Lena quickly marked them as read, eager to go back to her experiments, and then noticed that one message was actually a reply to something she had sent. A reply from . . . Holy crap. Holy crap.

    She clicked on it so hard she almost sprained her pointer finger.

    Today, 3:15 p.m.
    FROM: Michael-Matthew@harvard.edu
    TO: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu SUBJECT:
    Re: Pancreatic Cancer Screening Project
    Lena,
    Your project sounds good. I’ll be visiting Stanford in about two weeks. Why don’t we chat then?
    Cheers,
    MM
    Michael Matthew, Ph.D.
    Associate Professor
    Department of Biological Sciences, Harvard University

    Her heart skipped a beat. Then it started galloping. Then it slowed down to a crawl. And then she felt her blood pulsate in her eyelids, which couldn’t be healthy, but— Yes. Yes! She had a taker. Almost. Probably? Maybe. Definitely maybe. Michael Matthew had said “good.” He had said that it sounded “good.” It had to be a “good” sign, right?

    She frowned, scrolling down to reread the email she’d sent him several weeks earlier.

    July 7, 8:19 a.m.
    FROM: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu
    TO: Michael-Matthew@harvard.edu
    SUBJECT: Pancreatic Cancer Screening Project
    Dr. Matthew,
    My name is Lena Luthor, and I am a Ph.D. student in the biology department of Stanford University. My research focuses on pancreatic cancer, in particular on finding non-invasive, affordable detection tools that could lead to early treatment and increase survival rates. I have been working on blood biomarkers, with promising results. (You can read about my preliminary work in the peer-reviewed paper I have attached. I have also submitted more recent, unpublished findings to this year’s Society for Biological Discovery conference; acceptance is pending but see the attached abstract.) The next step would be to carry out additional studies to determine the feasibility of my test kit.

    Unfortunately, my current lab (Dr. Rhea Daxam, who is retiring in two years) does not have the funding or the equipment to allow me to proceed. She is encouraging me to find a larger cancer research lab where I could spend the next academic year collecting the data I need. Then I would return to Stanford to analyse and write up the data. I am a huge fan of the work you have published on pancreatic cancer, and I was wondering whether there might be a possibility to carry out my work in your lab at Harvard.

    I am happy to talk more in detail about my project if you are interested.

    Sincerely,
    Lena
    Lena Luthor
    Ph.D. Candidate
    Biology Department, Stanford University

    If Michael Matthew, cancer researcher extraordinaire, came to Stanford and gave Lena ten minutes of his time, she could convince him to help her out with her research predicament!

    Well…maybe.

    Lena was much better at actually doing research than at selling its importance to others. Science communication and public speaking of any sort were definitely her big weaknesses. But she had a chance to show Matthew how promising her results were. She could list the clinical benefits of her work, and she could explain how little she required to turn her project into a huge success. All she needed was a quiet bench in a corner of his lab, a couple hundred of his lab mice, and unlimited access to his twenty-million-dollar electron microscope. Matthew wouldn’t even notice her.

    Lena headed for the break room, mentally writing an impassioned speech on how she was willing to use his facilities only at night and limit her oxygen consumption to less than five breaths per minute. She poured herself a cup of stale coffee and turned around to find someone scowling right behind her.

    She startled so hard that she almost burned herself.

    “Jesus!” She clutched her chest, took a deep breath, and held tighter onto her unicorn mug. “Andy. You scared the shit out of me.”

    “Lena.”

    It was a bad sign. Andrea never called her Lena—never, unless she was reprimanding her for biting her nails to the quick or for having vitamin gummies for dinner.

    “Hey! How was your—”

    “The other night.”

    Dammit. “—weekend?”

    “Dr. Zor-El.”

    Dammit, dammit, dammit. “What about her?”

    “I saw the two of you together.”

    “Oh. Really?” Lena’s surprise sounded painfully playacted, even to her own ears. Maybe she should have signed up for drama club in high school instead of playing every single sport available.

    “Yes. Here, in the department,” Andrea answered.

    “Oh. Cool. Um, I didn’t see you, or I’d have said hi.”

    Andrea frowned. “Lee, I saw you. I saw you with Zor-El. You know that I saw you, and I know that you know that I saw you, because you’ve been avoiding me.”

    “I have not.”

    Andrea gave her one of her formidable no-bullshit looks. It was probably the one she used as president of the student senate, as head of the Stanford Women in Science Association, as director of outreach for the Organization of BIPOC Scientists. There was no fight Andrea couldn’t win. She was fearsome and indomitable, and Lena loved this about her—but not right now.

    “You haven’t answered any of my messages for the past two days. We usually text every hour,” Andrea pointed out.

    Lena switched the mug to her left hand, for no reason other than to buy some time. “I’ve been . . . busy?”

    “Busy?” Andrea’s eyebrow shot up. “Busy kissing Zor-El?”

    “Oh. Oh, that. That was just . . .”

    Andrea nodded as if to encourage her to finish the sentence. When it became obvious that Lena couldn’t, Andrea continued for her.

    “That was—no offense, Lee—but that was the most bizarre kiss I have ever seen.”

    Calm. Stay calm. She doesn’t know. She cannot know.

    “I doubt that” Lena retorted weakly. “Take that upside-down Spider-Man kiss. That was way more bizarre than—”

    “Lee, you said you were on a date that night. You’re not dating Zor-El, are you?” She twisted her face in a grimace.

    It would have been so easy to confess the truth. Since starting grad school Andrea and Lena had done heaps of moronic things, together and separately; the time Lena panicked and kissed none other than Kara Zor-El could become one of them, one they laughed about during their weekly beer-and-s’mores nights.

    Or not. There was a chance that if Lena admitted to lying now, Andrea might never trust her again. Or that she’d never go out with Jack. And as much as the idea of her best friend dating her ex had Lena wanting to puke just a bit, the thought of said best friend being anything but happy had her wanting to puke a lot more.

    The situation was depressingly simple: Lena was alone in the world. She had been for a long time, ever since high school. She had trained herself not to make a big deal out of it —she was sure many people were alone in the world and found themselves having to write down made-up names and phone numbers on their emergency contact forms. During college and her master’s, focusing on science and research had been her way of coping, and she had been perfectly ready to spend the rest of her life holed up in a lab with little more than a beaker and a handful of pipettes as her faithful companions —until . . . Andrea.

    In a way, it had been platonic love at first sight. First day of grad school. Biology cohort orientation. Lena entered the conference room, looked around, and sat in the first free seat she could find, petrified. She was the only woman in the room, virtually alone in a sea of white men who were already talking about boats, whatever sports ball was on TV the night before, and the best routes to drive places. I have made a terrible mistake, she’d thought. The Girl in the bathroom was wrong. I should never have come here. I am never going to fit in.

    And then a girl with wavy brown hair and a pretty face plopped in the chair next to hers and muttered, “So much for the STEM programs’ commitment to inclusivity, am I right?” That was the moment everything changed.

    They could have just been allies. As the only two non-cis-white-male students in their year, they could have found solace together when some bitching was needed and ignored each other otherwise. Lena had lots of friends like that—all of them, actually, circumstantial acquaintances whom she thought of fondly but not very often. Andrea, though, had been different from the start. Maybe because they’d soon found out that they loved spending their Saturday nights eating junk food and falling asleep to romcoms. Maybe it was the way she’d insisted on dragging Lena to every single “women in STEM” support group on campus and had wowed everyone with her bull’s-eye comments. Maybe it was that she’d opened up to Lena and explained how hard it had been for her to get where she was today. The way her father refused to support her decision to not take over the family business. That time a physics professor asked her if she was in the wrong class on the first day of the semester. The fact that despite her grades and research experience, even her academic adviser had seemed sceptical when she’d decided to pursue STEM higher education.

    Lena, whose path to grad school had been rough but not nearly as rough, was befuddled. Then enraged. And then in absolute awe when she understood the self-doubt that Andrea had been able to harness into sheer fierceness.

    And for some unimaginable reason, Andrea seemed to like Lena just as much. When Lena’s stipend hadn’t quite stretched to the end of the month, Andrea had shared her instant ramen. When Lena’s computer had crashed without backups, Andrea had stayed up all night to help her rewrite her crystallography paper. When Lena had nowhere to go over the holidays, Andrea would bring her friend home to Argentina and let her large family ply Lena with delicious food. When Lena had felt too stupid for the program and had considered dropping out, Andrea had talked her out of it.

    The day Lena met Andrea’s rolling eyes; a life-changing friendship was born. Slowly, they’d begun to include Samantha and become a bit of a trio, but Andrea . . . Andrea was her person. Family. Lena hadn’t even thought that was possible for someone like her.

    Andrea rarely asked anything for herself, and even though they’d been friends for more than two years, Lena had never seen her show interest in dating anyone—until Jack. Pretending that she’d been on a date with Dr. Zor-El was the least Lena could do to ensure her friend’s happiness.

    So Lena bucked up, smiled, and tried to keep her tone reasonably even while asking, “What do you mean?”

    “I mean that we talk every minute of every day, and you never mentioned Zor-El before. My closest friend is supposedly seeing the superstar professor of the department, and somehow, I’ve never heard of it? You know her reputation, right? Is it some kind of joke? Do you have a brain tumour? Do I have a brain tumour?”

    This was what happened whenever Lena lied: she ended up having to tell even more lies to cover her first, and she was horrible at it, which meant that each lie got worse and less convincing than the previous. There was no way she could fool Andrea. There was no way she could fool anybody. Andrea was going to get mad, then Jack was going to get mad, then Sam, too, and then Lena was going to find herself utterly alone. The heartbreak was going to make her flunk out of grad school. She was going to lose her visa and her only source of income and move back to Canada, where it snowed all the time and people ate moose heart and—

    “Hey.”

    The voice, deep and even, came from somewhere behind Lena, but she didn’t need to turn to know that it was Dr. Zor-El’s. Just like she didn’t need to turn to know that the large, warm weight suddenly steadying her, a firm but barely there pressure applied to the center of her lower back, was Dr. Zor-El’s hand. About two inches above her ass.

    Holy crap.

    Lena twisted her neck and looked up. And up. And up. And a bit more up. She was not a short woman, Dr. Zor-El was just tall. “Oh. Um, hey.”

    “Is everything okay?” Dr. Zor-El asked, looking into her eyes, in a low, intimate tone. Like they were alone. Like Andrea was not there. She said it in a way that should have made Lena uncomfortable but didn’t. For some inexplicable reason her presence in the room soothed Lena, even though until a second ago she had been freaking out. Perhaps two different types of unease neutralized each other? It sounded like a fascinating research topic. Worth pursuing. Maybe Lena should abandon biology and switch to psychology. Maybe she should excuse herself and go run a literature search. Maybe she should expire on the spot to avoid facing this crapfest of a situation she’d put herself in.

    “Yes. Yes. Everything is great. Andy and I were just . . . chatting. About our weekends,” Lena answered.

    Dr.Zor-El looked at Andrea, as though realizing for the first time that she was in the room. She acknowledged Andrea’s existence with one of those brief nods dudes used to greet others. Her hand slid lower on Lena’s spine just as Andrea’s eyes widened.

    “Nice to meet you, Andrea. I’ve heard a lot about you,” Dr. Zor-El said, and she was good at this, Lena had to admit. Because she was sure that from Andrea’s angle it looked like Dr. Zor-El was groping her, but in fact she was . . . not. Lena could barely feel her hand.

    Just a little, maybe. The warmth, and the slight pressure, and—

    “Nice to meet you, too.” Andrea looked thunderstruck. Like she might pass out. “Um, I was just about to leave. Lee, I’m going to text you when . . . yeah.”

    She was out of the room before Lena could answer. Which was good, because Lena didn’t need to come up with more lies. But also, slightly less good, because now it was just her and Dr. Zor-El. Standing way too close. Lena would have paid good money to say that she was the one to put some distance between them, but the embarrassing truth was that it was Dr. Zor-El who stepped away first. Enough to give her the space she needed, and then some.

    “Is everything okay?” Dr. Zor-El asked again. Her tone was still soft. Not something Lena would have expected from her.

    “Yes. Yes, I just . . .” Lena waved her hand. “Thank you.”

    “You’re welcome.”

    “Did you hear what she said? About Friday and . . .”

    “I did. That’s why I . . .” Dr. Zor-El looked at her, and then at her hand—the one that had been warming Lena’s back a few seconds ago—and Lena immediately understood.

    “Thank you,” she repeated. Because Kara Zor-El might have been a known ass, but Lena was feeling pretty damn grateful right at the moment. “Also, uh, I couldn’t help noticing that no agents from the Federal Bureau of Investigation have knocked on my door to arrest me in the past seventy-two hours.”

    The corner of Dr. Zor-El’s mouth twitched. Minimally. “Is that so?”

    Lena nodded. “Which makes me think that maybe you haven’t filed that complaint. Even though it would have been totally within your rights. So, thank you. For that. And . . . and for stepping in, right now. You saved me a lot of trouble.”

    “You shouldn’t need someone to step in.”

    Lena stiffened. Right. Known ass. “Well, it’s not as if I asked you to do anything. I was going to handle it by myse—”

    “And you shouldn’t have to lie about your relationship status,” Dr. Zor-El continued. “Especially not so that your friend and your boyfriend can get together guilt-free. That’s not how friendship works, last I checked.”

    Oh. So, she’d actually been listening when Lena vomited her life story at her, well she did greet Andy by her actual name. “It’s not like that. Jack wasn’t really my boyfriend. And Andrea didn’t ask me for anything. I’m not some sort of victim, I just . . . want my friend to be happy.”

    “By lying to her.”

    “Well, yeah, but . . . She thinks we’re dating, you and I,” Lena blurted out. God, the implications were too ridiculous to bear.

    “Wasn’t that the point?”

    “Yeah.” Lena nodded and then remembered the coffee in her hand and took a sip from her mug. It was still warm. The conversation with Andrea couldn’t have lasted more than five minutes. “Yeah. I guess it was. By the way—I’m Lena Luthor. In case you’re still interested in filing that complaint. I’m a Ph.D. student in Dr. Daxam’s lab—”

    “I know who you are.”

    “Oh…okay.”

    “I’m Kara. Zor-El. I’m faculty in—”

    Lena burst out laughing in her face. And then regretted it immediately as she noticed her confused expression, as though she’d seriously thought Lena might not know who she was. As though she was unaware of being one of the most prominent scholars in the field. The modesty was not at all like Kara Zor-El.

    Lena cleared her throat. “Right. Um, I know who you are, too, Dr. Zor-El.”

    “You should probably call me Kara.”

    “Oh. Oh, no.” That would be way too . . . No. The department was not like that. Grads didn’t call faculty by their first names. “I could never—”

    “If Andrea happens to be around.”

    “Oh. Yeah.” It made sense. “Thank you. I hadn’t thought of that. If that’s o-okay with you. I’m going to go home because this whole thing was kind of stressful and . . .” I need a hot shower and a good cry to get over this day.

    Kara nodded. “I’ll walk you to your car.”

    “I’m not that distraught.”

    “In case Andrea is still around.”

    “Oh. Thanks, but no need.”

    “I’d feel better if you’d let me walk you to your car.”

    “I don’t have a car.”

    “Do you take the bus?”

    “I bike. And my bike is right at the entrance of the building.”

    Kara opened her mouth, and then closed it. And then opened it again. “There are no bike lanes around here.”

    Lena shrugged. “I like to live dangerously.” Cheaply, she meant. “And I have a helmet. I’ll see you around, okay?”

    Kara’s chest rose as she inhaled deeply. “Yeah. Okay.”

    Lena got out of the room as fast as she could.

    Chapter End Notes

    Michael Matthew is based on Mon-El but he is human in this. Rhea Daxam is not related to Michael. Yes, she is an alien, as is Kara. Future chapters will go into more detail about aliens.

    The Rumour

    HYPOTHESIS: Any rumour regarding my love life will spread with a speed that is directly proportional to my desire to keep said rumour a secret.

    “Is it a prank? It must be a prank. Am I on national TV? Where are the hidden cameras? How do I look?”

    “It’s not a prank. There are no cameras.” Lena adjusted the strap of her backpack on her shoulder and stepped to the side to avoid being run over by an undergrad on an electric scooter. “But now that you mention it—you look great. Especially for seven-thirty in the morning.”

    Lena could ask her why she’d gone the extra mile to look nice on a run-of-the-mill Tuesday morning, but she already knew the answer: Jack’s and Andrea’s labs were on the same floor, and while the biology department was large, chance encounters were very much a possibility.
    She hid a smile. As weird as the idea of a best friend dating an ex might sound, she was glad that Andrea was starting to allow herself to consider Jack romantically.

    “Thanks.” Andrea chewed on her lower lip, deep in concentration. “So, it’s not a prank. Which means that there must be another explanation. Let me find it.”

    “There is no explanation to be found. We just—”

    “Oh my God, are you trying to get citizenship? Are they deporting you back to Canada because we’ve been sharing Sam’s Netflix password? Tell them we didn’t know it was a federal crime. No, wait, don’t tell them anything until we get you a lawyer. And, Lee, I will marry you. I’ll get you a green card and you won’t have to—”

    “Andy.” Lena squeezed her friend’s hand tighter to get her to shut up for a second. “I promise you, I’m not getting deported. I just went on a single date with Dr. Zor-El.”

    Andrea scrunched her face and dragged Lena to a bench on the side of the path, forcing her to sit down. Andrea took one of Lena’s hands in hers and gave it a gentle squeeze.

    “You know I am here for you, right Lee? And always will be, no matter what. We’re a team, you and me. And Sam, when she’s not busy screwing her way through the Stanford population. So, if Zor-El is forcing you into anything you don’t want to do you can tell me.”

    “For God’s sake”—Lena had to laugh— “it was just a date!”

    Andrea looked pained. “I just don’t understand.” Because it doesn’t make sense.

    “I know, but there is nothing to understand. It’s just . . . we went on a date.”

    “But . . . why? Lee, you’re beautiful and smart and funny and have excellent taste in knee socks, why would you go out with Kara Zor-El?”

    “Is this because she is an alie-”

    “God no Lena,” Andrea cut her off. “You know that I have no problem whatsoever about aliens or any group of people for that matter. But come on Lee, she is Kara Zor-El, the biggest asshole in Stanford, probably even in the entirety of America. You deserve so much better than her.”

    Lena scratched her nose. It pained her to say the words that were coming out of her mouth. “She’s not that bad Andy.”

    “Not that bad?” Andrea’s eyebrows shot up so high they almost merged with her hairline. “Kara Ass Zor-El?”

    “She is…okay.”

    “You deserve so much better than just ‘Okay’ Lee. How can you go from dating someone as cool as Jack to dating Kara Zor-El?”

    Perfect. This is the opening Lena needed. “Simple, I never cared that much about Jack. It wasn’t hard to move on honestly. Which is why you should put that poor boy out of his misery already. He deserves it and most importantly, you deserve it.”

    Andrea blushed, “I don’t know. Maybe. I mean if you really think that-”

    “I do. Don’t you have a Diversity in STEM meeting to get to?”

    Andrea looked at her watch, “Oh God yes, thanks for reminding me.” She stood and picked up her backpack. “Want to get lunch today?”

    “Can’t. I have a TA meeting, maybe Jack is free?” Lena raised an eyebrow and smirked making Andrea roll her eyes and flip her off.

    --
    Jack was acting weird. Which didn’t mean much, since Jack had always been a bit awkward, and having recently split from Lena to date her best friend was not going to make him any less so—but today he seemed even weirder than usual. He came into the campus coffee shop, a few hours after Lena’s conversation with Andrea, and proceeded to stare at her for two good minutes. Then three. Then five. It was more attention than he’d ever paid to Lena—yes, including their dates. When it got borderline ridiculous, she lifted her eyes from her laptop and waved at him. Jack flushed, grabbed his latte from the counter, and found a table for himself. Lena went back to rereading her two-line email for the seventieth time.

    Today, 10:12 a.m.
    FROM: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu
    TO: Michael-Matthew@harvard.edu
    SUBJECT: Re: Pancreatic Cancer Screening Project

    Dr. Matthew,
    Thank you for your response. Chatting in person would be fantastic. What day will you be at Stanford? Let me know when it’s most convenient for you to meet.

    Sincerely,
    Lena

    Not twenty minutes later, a fourth-year who worked with Dr. Mercy Graves over in pharmacology came in and took a seat next to Jack. They immediately started whispering to each other and pointing at Lena. Any other day she would have been concerned and a little upset, but Dr. Matthew had already answered her email, which took priority over . . . anything else, really.

    Today, 10:26 a.m.
    FROM: Michael-Matthew@harvard.edu
    TO: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu
    SUBJECT: Re: Pancreatic Cancer Screening Project

    Lena,
    I’m on sabbatical from Harvard this semester, so I’ll be staying for several days. A Stanford collaborator and I were just awarded a large grant, and we’ll be meeting to talk about setup, etc. Okay if we play it by ear once I’m there?

    Cheers,
    MM
    Sent from my iPhone

     

    Yes! She had several days to convince him to take on her project, which was much better than the ten minutes she’d originally anticipated. Lena fist-pumped—which led to Jack and his friend staring at her even more weirdly. What was up with them, anyway? Did she have toothpaste on her face or something? Who cared? She was going to meet Michael Matthew and convince him to take her on. Pancreatic cancer, I’m coming for you.

    She was in an excellent mood until two hours later, when she entered the biology TA meeting and a sudden silence dropped in the room. About fifteen pairs of eyes fixed on her —not a reaction she was accustomed to receiving.

    “Uh—hi?”

    A couple of people said hi back. Most averted their gazes. Lena told herself that she was just imagining things. Must be low blood sugar. Or high. One of the two.

    After that, things became even weirder. Dr. Daxam stopped by the lab just to ask Lena if there was anything she’d like to talk about; Chase, a grad in her lab, let her use the PCR machine first, even though he usually hoarded it like a third-grader with his last piece of Halloween candy; the lab manager winked at Lena as he handed her a stack of blank paper for the printer. And then she met Sam in the restroom, completely by chance, and suddenly everything made sense.

    “You sneaky monster,” she hissed. Her eyes were almost comically narrow. “I’ve been texting you all day.”

    “Oh.” Lena patted the back pocket of her jeans, and then the front one, trying to remember the last time she had seen her phone. “I think I might have left my phone at home.”

    “I cannot believe it.”

    “Believe what?”

    “I cannot believe you.”

    “I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

    “I thought we were friends.”

    “We are.”

    “Good friends.”

    “We are. You and Andy are my best friends. What—”

    “Clearly not, if I had to hear it from Stella, who heard it from Jess, who heard it from Jack, who heard it from Andy —”

    “Hear what?”

    “—who heard it from I don’t even know who. And I thought we were friends.”

    Something icy crawled its way up Lena’s back. Could it be . . . No. No, it couldn’t be.

    “Hear what?”

    “I’m done. I’m letting the cockroaches eat you. And I’m changing my Netflix password.” Oh no.

    “Sam. Hear what?”

    “That you are dating Dr. Zor-El.”

    --
    Lena had never been in Dr. Zor-El’s lab, but she knew where to find it. It was the biggest, most functional research space in the whole department, coveted by all and a never-ending source of resentment toward Dr. Zor-El. She had to swipe her badge once and then once more to access it (she rolled her eyes both times). The second door opened directly onto the lab space, and maybe it was because she was as tall as Mount Everest and her shoulders were just as large, but Dr. Zor-El was the very first thing she noticed. She was peering at a Southern blot next to Anderson, a grad who was one year ahead of Lena, but she turned toward the entrance the moment Lena came in.

    Lena smiled weakly at her—mainly out of relief at having found her.

    It was going to be all right. She was going to explain to her what Sam had told her, and without a doubt she was going to find the situation categorically unacceptable and fix it for the both of them, because Lena could not spend her next three years surrounded by people who thought that she was dating Kara freaking Zor-El.

    The problem was, Dr. Zor-El wasn’t the only one to notice Lena. There were over a dozen benches in the lab, and at least ten people working at them. Most of them—all of them—were staring at Lena. Probably because most of them—all of them —had heard that Lena was dating their boss.

    Fuck her life.

    “Can I talk to you for a minute, Dr. Zor-El?” Rationally, Lena knew that the lab was not furnished in a way that made echoing possible. Still, she felt as though her words bounced off the walls and repeated about four times.

    Dr. Zor-El nodded and handed the Southern blot to Anderson before heading in her direction. She appeared either unaware or uncaring that approximately two-thirds of her lab members were gaping at her. The remaining ones seemed to be on the verge of a haemorrhagic stroke.

    She led Lena to a meeting room just outside the main lab space, and she followed her silently, trying not to dwell on the fact that a lab full of people who thought that she and their professor were dating had just seen them enter a private room. Alone

    This was the worst. The absolute worst.

    “Everyone knows,” Lena blurted out as soon as the door closed behind her.

    Dr. Zor-El studied her for a moment, looking puzzled. “Are you okay?”

    “Everyone knows. About us.”

    “Us?”

    “Us.”

    She seemed confused, so Lena elaborated.

    “Us, dating—not that we’re dating, but Andrea clearly thought so, and she told . . .” She realized that the words were tumbling out and forced herself to slow down. “Jack. And he told everyone, and now everyone knows. Or they think they know, even though there’s absolutely nothing to know. As you and I know.”

    “And when you say everyone . . .?”

    “I mean everyone.” She pointed in the direction of the lab. “Those people? They know. The other grads? They know. Cherie, the department secretary? She totally knows. Gossip in this department is the worst. And they all think that I am dating a professor.”

    “I see.”

    “I am sorry this happened. So sorry. This is all my fault.” She wiped a hand down her face. “But I didn’t think that . . . I understand why Andrea would tell Jac—I mean, getting those two together was the whole point of this charade—but . . . Why would Jack tell anyone?”

    Dr. Zor-El shrugged. “Why wouldn’t he?”

    Now Lena was the one that looked confused. “What do you mean?”

    “A grad student dating a faculty member seems like an interesting piece of information to share.”

    Lena shook her head. “It’s not that interesting. Why would people be interested?”

    Dr. Zor-El lifted one eyebrow. “Someone once told me that ‘Gossip in this department is the wor—’ ”

    “Okay, okay. Point taken.” Lena took a deep breath and started pacing, trying to ignore the way Dr. Zor-El was studying her. “This is— We need to do something, of course. We need to tell people that this is not true and that we made it all up. Except that they’ll think that I’m crazy, and maybe that you are, too, so we have to come up with some other story. Yes, okay, we need to tell people we’re not together anymore—”

    “And what will Andrea and what’s-his-face do?”

    Lena stopped pacing. “Uh?”

    “Won’t your friends feel bad about dating if they think we’re not together? Or that you lied to them?”

    She hadn’t thought of that. “I— Maybe. Maybe, but—” Okay she was stumped.

    “Are you going to tell her the truth?”

    She let out a panicked sound. “I can’t. Not now.” God, why did Lena ever agree to date Jack? She wasn’t even into him. Yes, he was handsome, but not worth any of this. “Maybe we can tell people that I broke up with you?”

    “That’s very flattering,” Dr. Zor-El deadpanned.

    “Fine. We can say that you broke up with me.”

    “Because that sounds credible,” Dr. Zor-El said drily, almost below her breath.

    What was that even supposed to mean? No time to dwell on that, there’s a bigger problem at hand.

    “What if we tell people that it was a mutual breakup?”

    Zor-EL nodded. “Sounds good.”

    Lena perked up. “Really? Great, then! We’ll—”

    “We could ask Cherie to add it to the departmental newsletter.”

    “What?”

    “Or do you think a public announcement before seminar would be better?”

    “No. No, it’s—”

    “Maybe we should ask IT to put it on the Stanford home page. That way people would know—”

    “Okay, okay, fine! I get it.”

    “If what bothers you is that people are talking about you dating a professor, the damage is done, I’m afraid. Telling everyone that we broke up is not going to undo the fact that they think we dated.”

    Lena sighed. “Okay, then. If you have any ideas on how to fix this mess, by all means I am open to—”

    “You could let them go on thinking it.”

    For a moment, she thought she hadn’t heard correctly. “W-What?”

    “You can let people go on thinking that we’re dating. It solves your problem with your friend and what’s-his-face, and you don’t have much to lose, since it sounds like from a . . . reputation standpoint”—she said the word ‘reputation’ rolling her eyes a little, as if the concept of caring about what others thought were the dumbest thing since homeopathic antibiotics —“things cannot get any worse for you.”

    This was . . . Out of everything . . . In her life, Lena had never, she had never . . . “What?” she asked again, feebly.

    Dr. Zor-El shrugged. “Seems like a win-win to me.”

    It so did not, to Lena. It seemed like a lose-lose, and then lose again, and then lose some more, type of situation. It seemed insane.

    “You mean . . . forever?” She thought her voice came out whiny, but it was possible that it was just an effect of the blood pounding in her head.

    “That sounds excessive. Maybe until your friends are not dating anymore? Or until they’re more settled? I don’t know. Whatever works best, I guess.”

    “Are you not . . .” Lena had no idea how to even ask it. “Married, or something?”

    Dr. Zor-El shrugged. “My wife and the twins won’t mind.”

    Oh, shit.

    Lena felt like she was about to die of shame. She had forced herself on a married woman, a mother. And now everyone thinks that they are having an affair. She was a horrible, horrible person.

    “I . . . Oh my God, I didn’t— I am so sorry—”

    “Just kidding.”

    “I really had no idea that you—”

    “Lena, I was joking. I’m not married. No kids.”

    A wave of relief crashed into her. Followed by just as much anger.

    “Dr. Zor-El, this is not something you should joke—”

    “You really need to start calling me Kara. Since we’ve reportedly been dating for a while.”

    Lena exhaled slowly, pinching the bridge of her nose. “Why would you even— What would you even get out of this?”

    “Out of what?”

    “Pretending to date me. Why do you care? What’s in it for you?”

    Dr. Zor-El—Kara—opened her mouth, and for a moment Lena had the impression that she was going to say something important. But then she averted her gaze, and all that came out was “It would help you out.” She hesitated for a moment. “And I have my own reasons.”

    Lena narrowed her eyes. “What reasons?”

    “Reasons.”

    “If it’s criminal, I’d rather not be involved.”

    Kara smiled a bit. “It’s not.”

    “If you don’t tell me, I have no choice but to assume that it entails kidnapping. Or arson. Or embezzlement.”

    “If I tell you, it cannot leave this room.”

    “I think we can both agree that nothing that has happened in this room should ever leave it.”

    “Good point,” Kara conceded. She paused. Sighed. Chewed on the inside of her cheek for a second. Sighed again. “Okay,” she finally said, sounding like a woman who knew that she was going to regret speaking the second she opened her mouth. “I’m considered a flight risk.”

    “Flight risk? So, it is something criminal.”

    “What? No. The department suspects that I’m making plans to leave Stanford and move to another institution. Normally it wouldn’t bother me, but Stanford has decided to freeze my research funds.”

    “Oh.” Not what she’d thought. Not at all. “Can they?”

    “Yes. Well, up to one-third of them. The reasoning is that they don’t want to fund the research and further the career of someone who—they believe—is going to leave anyway.”

    “But if it’s only one-third—”

    “It’s millions of dollars,” Kara said levelly. “That I had earmarked for projects that I planned to finish within the next year. Here, at Stanford. Which means that I need those funds soon.”

    “Oh.” Come to think of it, Lena had been hearing scuttlebutt about Dr. Zor-El being recruited by other universities since her first year. A few months earlier there had even been a rumour that she might go work for NASA. “Why do they think that? And why now?”

    “A number of reasons. The most relevant is that a few weeks ago I was awarded a grant—a very large grant—with a scientist at another institution. That institution had tried to recruit me in the past, and Stanford sees the collaboration as an indication that I am planning to accept.” Kara hesitated before continuing. “More generally, I have been made aware that the . . . optics are that I have not put down roots because I want to be able to flee Stanford at the drop of a hat.”

    “Roots?”

    “Most of my grads will be done within the year. I have no extended family in the area. No spouse, no children. I’m currently renting—I’d have to buy a house just to convince the department that I’m committed to staying,” she said, clearly irritated. “If I was in a relationship . . . that would really help.”

    Okay. That made sense. But.

    “Have you considered getting a real girlfriend? boyfriend? Partner?”

    Kara’s eyebrow lifted. “Have you considered getting a real date?”

    “Touché.” A moment of silence fell between them then Lena found herself saying, “It would be complicated.”

    “What would be?”

    “To pretend that we’re dating.”

    “Really? It would be complicated to make people think that we’re dating? Is it because I’m an al-”

    “What? Of course not. I do attend a school that openly supports alien rights, half of my professors are from different planets, as is one of my close friends.”

    “Oh, okay.”

    God, she was insufferable.

    “It would be hard to do so convincingly for a prolonged period,” Lena elaborated.

    Kara shrugged. “We’ll be fine, as long as we say hi to each other in the hallways and you don’t call me Dr. Zor-El.”

    “I don’t think people who are dating just . . . say hi to each other.”

    “What do people who are dating do?”

    Honestly? Lena wasn’t exactly sure. She had gone on maybe five dates in her life, including the ones with Jack, and they had ranged from moderately boring to anxiety-inducing to horrifying (mostly when a guy had monologued about his grandmother’s hip replacement in frightening detail). She would have loved to have someone in her life, but she doubted it was in store for her. Maybe she was unlovable. Maybe spending so many years alone had warped her in some fundamental way and that was why she seemed to be unable to develop a true romantic connection, or even the type of attraction she often heard others talk about. In the end, it didn’t really matter. Grad school and dating went poorly together, anyway, which was probably why Dr. Kara Zor-El, MacArthur Fellow and genius extraordinaire, was standing here at thirtysomething years old, asking Lena what people did on dates. Academics, ladies and gentlemen.

    “Um . . . things. Stuff.” Lena racked her brain. “People go out and do activities together. Like apple picking, or those Paint and Sip things.” Which are idiotic, Lena thought.

    “Which are idiotic,” Kara said. “You could just go to Andrea and tell her that we went out and painted a Monet. Sounds like she’d take care of letting everyone else know.”

    “Okay, first of all, it was Jack. Let’s agree to blame Jack. And it’s more than that,” Lena insisted. “People who date, they—they talk. A lot. More than just greetings in the hallway. They know each other’s favourite colours, and where they were born, and they . . . they hold hands. They kiss.”

    Kara pressed her lips together as if to suppress a smile. “We could do that. I mean to make it more believable and if I have your consent obviously.”

    Lena winced. “I am sorry about the kiss. I really didn’t think, and—”

    Kara shook her head. “It’s fine.” She looked amused.

    Lena cocked her head. “Are you enjoying this?”

    “‘Enjoying’ is probably not the right word, but you have to admit that it’s quite entertaining.”

    She had no idea what Kara was talking about. There was nothing entertaining about the fact that she had randomly kissed a faculty member because she was the only person in the hallway and that, as a consequence of that spectacularly idiotic action, everyone thought she was dating someone she’d met exactly twice before today— She burst into laughter and folded into herself before her train of thought was even over, overwhelmed by the sheer improbability of the situation.

    This was her life. These were the results of her actions. When she could finally breathe again, her abs hurt and she had to wipe her eyes. “This is the worst.”

    Kara was smiling, staring at her with a strange light in her eyes. And would you look at that: Kara Zor-El had dimples. Cute ones. “Yep.”

    “And it’s all my fault.”

    “Pretty much. I kind of yanked Andrea’s chain yesterday, but yeah, I’d say that it’s mostly your fault.”

    Fake dating. Kara Zor-El. Lena would have to be a lunatic. “Wouldn’t it be a problem that you’re faculty and I’m a graduate student?”

    “It wouldn’t look great, but I don’t think so, no. Since I have no authority whatsoever over you and am not involved in your supervision. But I can ask around.”

    It was an epically bad idea. The worst idea ever entertained in the epically bad history of bad ideas. Except that it really would solve this current problem of hers, as well as some of Kara’s, in exchange for saying hi to her once a week and making an effort not to call her Dr. Zor-El and to sometimes kiss her, which she has done before and it was not the worst thing in the world. It seemed like a pretty good deal.

    “Can I think about it?” Lena asked.

    “Of course,” Kara said calmly. Reassuringly. Lena hadn’t thought Kara could be like this. After hearing all the stories and seeing her walk around with that perpetual frown of hers, Lena really hadn’t thought she’d be like this. Even if she didn’t quite know what this even meant.

    “And thank you, I guess. For offering. Kara.” She added the last word like an afterthought. Trying her name out on her lips. It felt weird, but not too weird.

    After a long pause, Kara nodded. “No problem. Lena.”

    The Details

    HYPOTHESIS: A private conversation with Kara Zor-El will become 150 percent more awkward after the word “sex” is uttered. By me.

    Three days later, Lena found herself standing in front of Kara’s office. She’d never been there before, but she had no problem finding it. The student scurrying out with misty eyes and a terrified expression was a dead giveaway, not to mention that Kara’s door was the only one in the hallway completely devoid of pictures of kids, pets, or significant others. Not even a copy of her article that had made the cover of Nature Methods, which Lena knew about from looking her up on Google Scholar the previous day. Just dark brown wood and a metal plaque that read: Kara M. Zor-El, Ph.D.

    Maybe the M stood for ‘Meanie.’

    She took a deep breath before knocking and then another between Kara’s “Come in” and the moment she finally managed to force herself to open the door. When she entered the office, Kara didn’t immediately look up and continued typing on her laptop. “My office hours were over five minutes ago, so —”

    “It’s me,” Lena said.

    Kara’s hands halted, hovering half an inch or so above the keyboard. Then she turned her chair toward the entrance. “Lena.”

    Lena hated to admit it, but she liked the way her name sounded when Kara said it. it sounded so gentle.

    “What did you say to her?” Lena asked. “The girl who ran out in tears?”

    It took Kara a moment to remember that less than sixty seconds ago there had been someone else in the office— someone whom she clearly made cry. “I just gave her feedback on something she wrote.”

    Lena nodded, silently thanking God that Kara was not her adviser and never would be.

    “I was going to email you. I talked to the dean earlier today,” Kara informed. She gestured to the chair in front of her desk, so Lena pulled it back and took a seat. “About you.”

    “Oh.” Lena’s stomach dropped. She’d much rather the dean didn’t know about her existence.

    “Well, about us,” Kara amended. “And socialization regulations.”

    “What did she say?”

    “There’s nothing against you and me dating since I’m not your adviser. However, there are some issues to consider. I won’t be able to collaborate with you in any formal capacity. And I’m part of the program’s awards committee, which means that I’ll have to excuse myself if you are nominated for fellowships or similar opportunities.”

    Lena nodded. “Fair enough.”

    “And I absolutely cannot be part of your thesis committee.”

    Lena huffed out a laugh. “That won’t be a problem. I wasn’t going to ask you to be on my committee.”

    Kara narrowed her eyes. “Why not? You study pancreatic cancer, right?”

    “Yep. Early detection.”

    “Then your work would benefit from the perspective of a computational modeler.”

    “Yeah, but there are other computational modelers in the department. And I’d like to eventually graduate, ideally without sobbing in a bathroom stall after each committee meeting.”

    Kara glared at her.

    Lena shrugged. “No offense. I’m a simple girl, with simple needs.”

    “So, have you decided?” Kara changed the topic.

    Lena pressed her lips together as Kara watched her calmly. She took a deep breath before saying, “Yes. Yes, I . . . I want to do it. It’s a good idea, actually.”

    “Very well, then.” Kara was completely expressionless— almost too expressionless. Like it was no big deal, and she didn’t care either way; like if Lena had said no, it wouldn’t have changed anything for her.

    “Though, I’ve been thinking about this a lot. And I think that it would be best if we laid down some ground rules. Before starting.”

    “Ground rules?”

    Lena nodded. “Yes. You know. What we are allowed and not allowed to do. What we can expect from this arrangement. I think that’s pretty standard protocol, before embarking on a fake-dating relationship.”

    Kara tilted her head. “Standard protocol?”

    “Yup.”

    “How many times have you done this?”

    “Zero. But I am familiar with the trope.”

    “The . . . what?” Kara blinked at her, confused.

    Lena rolled her eyes. Of course, Kara Zor-El didn’t know about tropes, her head was stuck too far up her ass to know anything besides academics.

    Lena ignored that question and went right into the conversation that needed to be had. “First of all, this should be a strictly on-campus arrangement. Not that I think you’d want to meet me off campus, but just in case you were planning to kill two birds with one stone, I’m not going to be your last-minute backup if you need to bring a date home for Christmas, or whatever.”

    “Noted,” Kara said dryly.

    “Okay. Second rule. Actually, it could be interpreted as an extension of the first rule. But”—Lena bit into her lip, willing herself to bring it up—“no sex.”

    For several moments Kara simply didn’t move. Not even a millimetre. Then her lips parted, but no sound came out, and that’s when Lena realized that she had just rendered Kara Zor-El speechless. The silence was almost deafening. Did Kara expect sex from her?

    “Listen”—Lena made to stand from the chair, panic rising in her throat—“I’m sorry, but if one of the reasons you offered to fake-date is that you thought that we would—”

    “No.” The word half exploded out of Kara. She looked genuinely appalled. “I’m shocked that you’d even feel the need to bring it up.”

    “Oh.” Lena’s cheeks heated at the indignation Kara’s voice. Right. Of course she didn’t expect that. Or even want that, with her. Look at Kara—why would she? “I’m sorry, I didn’t mean to assume—”

    “No, it makes sense to be up-front. I was just surprised.”

    “I know.” Lena nodded. Honestly, she was a little surprised, too. That she was sitting in Kara Zor-El’s’s office, talking about sex—not the meiosis kind of sex, but potential sexual intercourse between the two of them. “Sorry. I didn’t mean to make things weird.”

    “It’s okay. This whole thing is weird.”

    The silence between them stretched, and Lena noticed that Kara was blushing faintly. Just a dusting of red, but she looked so . . . Lena couldn’t stop staring.

    “No sex,” Kara confirmed with a nod.

    “No sex,” she repeated. “Okay. Third. It’s not really a rule, but here goes: I won’t date anyone else. As in real dating. It would be messy and complicate everything and . . .” Lena hesitated. Should she tell her? Was it too much information? Did she need to know? Oh, well. Why not, at this point? It wasn’t like she hadn’t kissed the woman or brought up sex in her place of work. “I don’t date, anyway. Jack was an exception. I’ve never . . . I’ve never dated seriously before, and it’s probably for the best. Grad school is stressful enough, and I have my friends, and my project on pancreatic cancer, and honestly there are better things to use my time for.” The last few words came out more defensively than she’d intended.

    Kara just stared and said nothing.

    “But you can date, of course,” Lena added hastily. “Though I’d appreciate it if you could avoid telling people in the department, just so I don’t look like an idiot, and you don’t look like you’re cheating on me and rumours don’t balloon out of control. It would benefit you, too, since you’re trying to look like you’re in a committed relationship—”

    “I won’t.”

    “Okay. Great. Thanks. I know lying by omission can be a pain, but—”

    “I mean, I won’t date someone else.”

    It was said with finality in Kara’s voice and Lena didn’t want to press further so she just nodded.

    “Okay. Fourth. We obviously can’t keep on doing this forever, so we should give ourselves a deadline.”

    “When would that be?”

    “I’m not sure. A month or so would probably be enough to convince Andrea that I’m firmly over Jack. But it might not be enough on your end, so . . . you tell me.”

    “August twelfth.”

    It was a little over a month from now. But also . . . “That’s a weirdly specific date.” Lena racked her head, trying to figure out why it could be meaningful. The only thing that came to mind was that she’d be in Boston that week for the annual biology conference.

    “It’s the day after the department’s final budget review. If they don’t release my funds by then, they won’t release them at all.”

    “I see. Well, then, let’s agree that on August twelfth we part ways. I’ll tell Andrea that our breakup was amicable but that I’m a little sad about it because I still have a bit of a crush on you.” She grinned at Kara. “Just so she won’t suspect that I’m still hung up on Jack. Okay.” She took a deep breath. “Fifth and last. For this to work we should probably . . . do things together. Every once in a while.”

    “Things?”

    “Things. Stuff.”

    “Stuff,” Kara repeated dubiously.

    “Yep. Stuff. What do you do for fun?”

    “Fun?” Kara repeated, like she’d never heard the word before.

    “Yeah. What do you do when you’re not at work?”

    “Sometimes I work at home, too. And I work out. And I sleep.”

    Lena had to actively stop herself from face-palming. Of course, that was such a Kara answer. “Um, great. Anything else?”

    “What do you do for fun?” Kara asked, somewhat defensively.

    “Plenty of things. I . . .” Go to the movies. Though she hadn’t been since the last time Sam had dragged her. Play board games. But every single one of her friends was too busy lately, so not that, either. She’d participated in that volleyball tournament, but it had been over a year ago. “Um. I work out?” She would have loved to wipe that smug expression off Kara’s face. So much. “Whatever. We should do something together on a regular basis. I don’t know, maybe get coffee? Like, once a week? Just for ten minutes, at a place where people could easily see us. I know it sounds annoying and like a waste of time, but it’ll be super short, and it would make the fake dating more credible, and—”

    “Sure.”

    That didn’t take much convincing.

     

    “Okay. Um . . how about Wednesday?”

    Kara angled her chair to face her computer and pulled up a calendar app. It was so full of colourful boxes that Lena felt a surge of vicarious anxiety.

    “It works before eleven a.m. And after six p.m.”

    “Ten?”

    Kara turned back to her. “Ten’s good.”

    “Okay.” She waited for Kara to type it in, but she made no move to. “Aren’t you going to add it to your calendar?”

    “I’ll remember,” Kara told her evenly.

    “Okay now for the finer details,” Lena said. “We should probably have pet names for each other or something like that, I think that’s what people who are dating do.”

    Kara bit the inside of her cheek to hold back her smile. “Yeah sure. As long as you don’t call me anything like pumpkin or butter cup.”

    Lena couldn’t help but laugh. “How about ‘Darling?’”

    “That’s okay. If you started calling me Pookie I would have to end this now.”

    Who would have thought, Kara Zor-El wasn’t always a stuck-up assshole.

    Lena held up her hands, “I wouldn’t dare. You better not call me that either, I will literally die of embarrassment, and it would be all your fault.”

    “We can’t have that now, can we? How about ‘Baby’? It’s a pretty common one.”

    Lena bit her bottom lip and nodded; she didn’t want to acknowledge the fuzzy feeling she got when Kara said that word.

    “Okay, now the PDA part. Nothing too excessive. I’m okay with handholding and a brief kiss here and there.”

    Kara nodded. “I’m okay with that too. Just give me a heads up the next time you kiss me.”

    Lena dropped her head to the desk. “I said I was sorry, I really thought you said yes.”

    “I’m joking Lena.”

    Lena lifted her head and saw a sight she didn’t think she would, Kara smiling. Well, an attempt at smiling. It was still cute though.

    “When do you want to start?” Lena asked.

    “How about next week?”

    It was Friday. Which meant that she had fewer than seven days to psychologically prepare for the experience of getting coffee with Kara Zor-El.

    “Sounds good. Let’s meet at the Starbucks on campus. It’s where most of the grads get coffee—someone’s bound to spot us.” Lena headed for the door, pausing to glance back at Kara. “I guess I’ll see you for fake-dating Wednesday, then?”

    “See you, baby.”

    Lena let out a nervous chuckle. “Goodbye, darling.”

    --
    “Pass the salt.”

    Lena would have, but Sam looked like she was already salty enough. So, she leaned her hip against the kitchen counter and folded her arms across her chest.

    “Sam.”

    “And the pepper.”

    “Sam.”

    “And the oil.”

    “Sam . . .”

    “Sunflower. Not that grape-seed crap.”

    “Listen. It’s not what you think—”

    “Fine. I’ll get them myself.”

    To be fair, Sam had every right to be mad. And Lena did feel bad for her. Sam was from Krypton, a planet that exploded just months after Sam was born. Like many Kryptonians, Sam’s parents didn’t make it to Earth. Sam was put in a pod and sent to Earth with just a blanket, romper, and a letter from her parents. She was lucky enough to be adopted by the family who owned the yard her pod crashed into. They gave her a good upbringing and just because she was not biologically her parents’ child did not mean she was excused from the family expectations; her family was STEM royalty. Generations of biologists, geologists, botanists, physicists, and who knows what other – ists. Her father was a dean at some state school on the East Coast. Her mother had a TED Talk on Purkinje cells with several million views on YouTube. Her brother has two Ph.Ds. and is studying for his third. Did Sam want to be in a Ph.D. program, headed for an academic career? Probably no. Did she have any other choice, considering the pressure her family had put on her since she was in diapers? Also no. Not to say that Sam was unhappy or ungrateful. Her plan was to get her Ph.D., find a nice cushy industry job, and make lots of money working nine-to-five—which technically qualified as “being a scientist,” which in turn was not something her parents would be able to object to. At least, not too strenuously.

    In the meantime, all she wanted was to have a grad school experience that was as un-traumatizing as possible. Out of everyone in Lena’s program, she was the one who best managed to have a life outside of grad school. She did things that were unimaginable to most grads, like cooking real food! Going for hikes! Meditating! Acting in a play! Dating like it was an Olympic sport! (“It is an Olympic sport, Lena. And I am training for gold.”)

    Which was why when Kara forced Sam to throw out tons of data and redo half her study, it made for a very, very miserable few months. In retrospect, that might have been when Sam started wishing a plague on the Zor-El house (she had been rehearsing for Romeo and Juliet at the time).

    “Sam, can we please talk about this?”

    “We’re talking.”

    “No, you are cooking and I am just standing here, trying to get you to acknowledge that you are mad because Kara—”

    Sam turned away from her casserole, wagging her finger in Lena’s direction. “Do not say it.”

    “Do not say what?”

    “You know what.”

    “Kara Zo—?”

    “Do not say her name.”

    Lena threw her hands up.

    “This is crazy. It’s fake, Sam.”

    Sam went back to chopping the asparagus. “Pass the salt.”

    “Are you even listening? It’s not real.”

    “And the pepper, and the—”

    “The relationship, it’s fake. We’re not really dating. We’re pretending so people will think that we’re dating.”

    Sam’s hands stopped mid-chop. “What?”

    “You heard me.”

    “Is it a . . . friends-with-benefits arrangement? Because—”

    “No. It’s the opposite. There are no benefits. Zero benefits. Zero sex. Zero friends, too.”
