    Sam stared at her, narrow-eyed. “To be clear, oral and butt stuff totally counts as sex—”

    “Sam.”

    Sam took a step closer, grabbing a dishrag to wipe her hands, nostrils flaring. “I’m scared to ask.”

    “I know it sounds ridiculous. She’s helping me out by pretending we’re together because I lied to Andy, and I need her to feel okay about dating Jack. It’s all fake. Kara and I have talked exactly”—she decided on the spot to omit any information pertinent to The Night—“three times, and I know nothing about her. Except that she’s willing to help me handle this situation, and I jumped at the chance.”

    Sam was making that face, the one she reserved for people who wore sandals paired with white socks. She could be a little scary, she had to admit. “This is . . . wow.” There was a vein pulsating on her forehead. “Lee, this is breathtakingly stupid.”

    “Maybe.” Yes. Yes, it was. “But it is what it is. And you have to support me in my idiocy, because you and Andy are my best friends.”

    “Isn’t Zor-El your best friend now?”

    “Come on, Sam. She’s an ass. But she’s actually been pretty nice to me, and—”

    “I’m not even—” Sam grimaced. “I’m not going to address this.”

    Lena sighed. “Okay. Don’t address this. You don’t have to. But can you just not hate me? Please? I know she’s been a nightmare to half the grads in the program, you included. But she’s helping me out. You and Andy are the only ones I care about knowing the truth. But I can’t tell Andy—”

    “—for obvious reasons.”

    “—for obvious reasons,” Lena finished at the same time, and smiled. Sam just shook her head disapprovingly, but her expression had softened.

    “Lee. You’re amazing. And kind, way too kind. You should find someone better than Zor-El. Someone to date for real.”

    “Yeah, right.” She rolled her eyes. “Because it went so well with Jack. Who, by the way, I only agreed to date following your advice! ‘Give the boy a chance,’ you said. ‘What could possibly go wrong?’ you said.” Sam glared, and Lena laughed. “Listen, I’m clearly bad at real dating. Maybe fake dating will be different. Maybe I’ve found my niche.”

    Sam sighed. “Does it have to be Zor-El? There are better faculty members to fake-date.”

    “Like who?”

    “I don’t know. Dr. Graves?”

    “Didn’t her wife just give birth to triplets?”

    “Oh, yeah. What about Alex Danvers? She’s hot. Cute smile, too. I would know—she always smiles at me.”

    Lena burst into laughter. “I could never fake-date Dr. Danvers, not with how assiduously you’ve been thirsting after her for the past two years.”

    “I have, haven’t I? Did I ever tell you about the serious flirting that happened between us at the undergrad research fair? I’m pretty sure she winked at me multiple times from the other side of the room. Now, some say she just had something in her eye, but—”

    “Me. I said that she probably had something in her eye. And you tell me about it every other day.”

    “Right.” Sam sighed. “You know, Lee, I would have fake-dated you myself in a heartbeat, to spare you from goddamned Zor-El. I would have held hands with you, given you my jacket when you were cold, and very publicly gifted you chocolate roses and teddy bears on Valentine’s Day.”

    How refreshing, to talk with someone who’d watched a rom-com. Or ten.

    “I know. But you also bring home a different person every week, and you love it, and I love that you love it. I don’t want to cramp your style.”

    “Fair.”

    “Can you please not hate me, then?”

    Sam tossed the kitchen cloth onto the counter and stepped closer.

    “Lee, I could never hate you. You’ll always be my little sister.” She pulled Lena into her chest, hugging her tight.

    “I’m only a year younger,” Lena grumbled into her shirt and Sam held her tighter.

    The Fake-Date

    HYPOTHESIS: The more time I spend with Kara, the less she seems like the person people describe.

    Lena arrived at the first fake-dating Wednesday late and in the foulest of moods, after a morning spent growling at her cheap, knockoff reagents for not dissolving, then not precipitating, then not sonicating, then not being enough for her to run her entire assay.

    She paused outside the coffee shop’s door and took a deep breath. She needed a better lab if she wanted to produce decent science. Better equipment. Better reagents. Better bacteria cultures. Better everything. Next week, when Michael Matthew arrived, she had to be on top of her game. She needed to prepare her spiel, not waste time on a coffee she didn’t particularly want, with a person she most definitely didn’t want to talk to, halfway through her experimental protocol.

    When she stepped inside the café, Kara was already there, wearing a black t-shirt that looked like it was designed, and produced specifically with the upper half of her body in mind. Kara Zor-El was beautiful. The realization sank into her as they got into the line, but she willed herself not to think much of it, instead she scanned the drink menu.

    In the coffee shop, there were a total of three biology grad students, one pharmacology postdoc, and one undergraduate research assistant eyeing them. Perfect.

    “So. How are you?” Lena asked, because it was the thing to do.

    “Fine. You?”

    “Fine.”

    It occurred to Lena that maybe she hadn’t thought this through as thoroughly as she should have. Because being seen together might have been their goal but standing next to each other in silence was not going to fool anyone into thinking that they were blissfully dating. And Kara was . . . well she seemed unlikely to initiate any kind of conversation.

    “So.” Lena shifted her weight to the balls of her feet a couple of times. “What’s your favourite colour?”

    Kara looked at her confused. “What?”

    “Your favourite colour.”

    “My favourite colour?”

    “Yep.”

    There was a crease between Kara’s eyes. “I—don’t know?”

    “What do you mean you don’t know?”

    “They’re colors. They’re all the same.”

    “There must be one you like most.”

    “I don’t think so.”

    “Red?”

    “I don’t know.”

    “Yellow? Vomit green?”

    Kara’s eyes narrowed. “Why are you asking?”

    Lena shrugged. “It feels like something I should know.”

    “Why?”

    “Because. If someone tries to figure out whether we’re really dating, it might be one of the first questions they ask. Top five, for sure.”

    “Does that seem like a likely scenario to you?”

    “About as likely as me fake-dating you.”

    Kara nodded, as if conceding her point. “Okay. Black, I guess.”

    Lena snorted. “Figures.”

    “What’s wrong with black?”

    “It’s not even a color. It’s no colors, technically.”

    “It’s better than vomit green.”

    “No, it isn’t.”

    “Of course it is.”

    “Yeah, well. It suits your scion-of-darkness personality.”

    “What does that even—”

    “Good morning.” The barista smiled at them cheerfully. “What will you have today?”

    Lena smiled back, gesturing at Kara to order first.

    “Coffee. Black.”

    Lena had to duck her head to hide her smile, but when she glanced at Kara again, the corner of her mouth was curved upward. Which, she reluctantly admitted to herself, was not a bad look for Kara. She ignored it and ordered the most fatty, sugary thing on the drink menu, asking for extra whipped cream. She was wondering if she should try to make up for it by buying an apple, too, or if she should just lean into it and top it off with a cookie, when Kara took a credit card out of her wallet and held it to the cashier.

    “Oh, no. No, no, no. No.” Lena put her hand in front of Kara’s and lowered her voice. “You can’t pay for my coffee.”

    Kara blinked. “I can’t?”

    “That’s not the kind of fake relationship we’re having.”

    Kara looked surprised. “It isn’t?”

    “Nope.” Lena shook her head.

    Kara leaned closer till her lips reached Lena’s ear. “Let me buy you coffee Lena. What kind of a girlfriend will I be if I let my girlfriend buy her own coffee when she is a grad student and has better things to spend her money on?”

    “Fake girlfriend,” Lena whispered, correcting Kara.

    Kara let out a faint chuckle. “Yeah. So is that a yes on letting me buy you coffee, not that the monstrosity you ordered can even be called coffee.”

    Lena let out a sigh, she barely made an income so if Kara wants to buy her coffee why not?

    “Fine and add a chocolate chip cookie,” Lena said, choosing to ignore Kara’s comment amount her choice in drink. She ordered black coffee; how dare she think she has the right to judge others’ coffee choices?

    Kara handed her credit card to the barista. “She’ll have two chocolate chip cookies as well. Thanks.”

    Lena didn’t say anything about Kara ordering her an extra cookie, she just smiled in appreciation.

    While they were waiting for their drinks, Lena’s mind began drifting off to her project and to whether she could convince Dr. Daxam to buy her better reagents soon. She looked distractedly around the coffee shop, finding that even though the research assistant, the postdoc, and one of the students were gone, two grads (one of whom serendipitously happened to work in Andrea’s lab) were still sitting at a table by the door, glancing toward them every few minutes.
    Excellent.

    Lena leaned her hip against the counter and looked up at Kara. Thank God this thing was only going to be ten minutes a week, or she’d develop a permanent crick in her neck just trying to look at Kara’s face.

    “Where were you born?” Lena asked.

    “Krypton. Argo City.”

    “I have a friend that’s from Krypton, she was a baby when…” when your planet exploded, and most of your species died because there were not enough pods to send to Earth. “She does not know much about the home she was born in; can you tell me about it when you have time? If you are comfortable with talking about it, that is.”

    Way to go Lena. You spend five minutes with her and bring up her dead planet.

    “Yeah sure,” Kara answered.

    “Here you go.” The barista voice cut through the awkward silence. She placed their drinks and Lena’s cookies on the counter.

    Lena picked up her order then cleared her throat. “Well, this was fun.”

    Kara put the lid on her coffee cup and then looked at Lena. “Is fake-dating Wednesday over?”

    “Yep. Great job, team, now hit the showers. You’re free until next week.” Lena stabbed her straw into her drink and took a sip, feeling the sugar explode in her mouth. Whatever she’d ordered, it was disgustingly good. She was probably developing diabetes as she spoke. “I’ll see you—”

    “Where were you born?” Kara asked before Lena could leave.

    Oh, they were doing this. Kara is probably just asking to be polite.

    “Newfoundland,” Lena answered.

    “When did you move here?”

    “Eight years ago. For college.”

    “Why the US? Canada has excellent school.”

    “I got a full ride.” It was true. Not the whole truth but part of it.

    Kara fidgeted with the cardboard cup holder. “Do you go back a lot?”

    “Not really, no.” Lena licked some whipped cream off her straw.

    “Do you plan to move back home once you graduate?”

    Lena tensed. “Not if I can help it.” She had lots of painful memories in Canada, and her only family, the people she wanted nearby, Andy and Sam, both lived in the US.

    Kara nodded, taking a sip of her coffee. “Favourite colour?”

    Lena opened her mouth to tell Kara her favourite colour, which was so much better than Kara’s, and . . . “Dammit.”

    Kara gave her a knowing look. “Difficult, isn’t it?”

    “There are so many good ones.”

    “Yup.”

    “I’m going to go with blue. Light blue. No, wait!”

    “Mmm.”

    “Let’s say white. Okay, white.”

    “You know, I don’t think I can accept that. White’s not really a colour. More like all colours put together—”

    Lena smacked Kara’s arm.

    “Ow,” Kara said, clearly not in pain. With a sly smile, she waved goodbye and turned away, heading for the biology building.

    “Hey, Kara?”

    Kara paused and looked back.

    “Thanks for the coffee. And the cookies”

    Kara hesitated and then nodded, once. A small smile forming. “My pleasure, baby.”

    --
    Lena was late for her second fake-dating Wednesday, too, but for different reasons—all Michael Matthew related.

    First, she’d overslept after staying up late the previous night rehearsing how she was going to sell him her project. She’d repeated her spiel so many times that Sam had started finishing her sentences, and then, at 1:00 a.m., Sam had hurled a nectarine at her and begged her to go practice in her room. Which she had, until 3:00 a.m.

    Then, in the morning, she’d realized that her usual lab outfit (mom jeans, ratty 5K T-shirt, and very, very messy bun) would probably not communicate ‘valuable future colleague’ to Dr. Matthew and spent an excessive amount of time looking for something appropriate. Dress for success and all that.

    Finally, it occurred to her that she had no idea what Dr. Matthew—arguably the most important person in her life at the moment, and yes, she was aware of how sad that sounded but decided not to dwell on it—even looked like. She looked him up on her phone and found out that he was somewhere in his late thirties, brunette with blue-grey eyes, and had very straight, very white teeth. When she arrived at the campus Starbucks, Lena was whispering to his Harvard headshot, “Please, let me come work in your lab.”

    Then she noticed Kara.

    Lena glanced at her, and she immediately knew that Kara was in the nastiest of moods. That rumour of her throwing a petri dish against a wall because her experiment hadn’t worked out, or because the electron microscope needed repairs, or because something equally inconsequential had happened came to mind. She considered ducking under the table.

    It’s okay, she told herself. This is worth it. Things with Andy were back to normal. Better than normal: she and Jack were officially dating.

    “Hey.” Lena smiled. Kara responded with a look that exuded moodiness and existential angst. Lena took a fortifying breath. “How are you?”

    “Fine.” Kara’s tone was clipped, her expression tenser than usual.

    Kara was wearing a red plaid shirt, unbuttoned, a white t-shirt, and jeans, looking more like a wood-chopping lumberjack than a scholar pondering the mysteries of computational biology. Lena couldn’t help noticing the muscles. Her hair was still a bit long but shorter than the previous week. It seemed a little surreal to Lena that she and Kara Zor-El were at a point where she was able to keep track of both her moods and her haircuts.

    “Ready to get coffee?” Lena asked.

    Kara nodded distractedly, barely looking at her. On a table in the back, a fifth-year was glancing at them while pretending to clean the monitor of her laptop.

    “Sorry if I was late. I just—”

    “It’s fine.”

    “Did you have a good week?”

    “Fine.”

    Okay. “Um . . . did you do anything fun last weekend?”

    “I worked.”

    They got in line to order, and it was all Lena could do to stop herself from sighing. “Weather’s been nice, right? Not too hot.”

    Kara grunted in response.

    It was starting to be a bit much. There was a limit to what Lena would do for this fake-dating relationship—even for a free mango Frappuccino. She sighed. “Is it because of the haircut?”

    That got Kara’s attention. Kara looked down at her, a vertical line deep between her eyebrows. “What?”

    “The mood. Is it because of the haircut?”

    “What mood?”

    Lena gestured broadly toward her. “This. The bad mood you’re in.”

    “I’m not in a bad mood.

    Lena snorted.

    “What?” Kara frowned, unappreciative of her snort.

    “Come on, darling”

    “What?”

    “You ooze moodiness.”

    “I do not.”

    “You so do. I saw that face, and I immediately knew.”

    “You did not.”

    “I did. I do. But it’s fine, you’re allowed to be in a bad mood.”

    It was their turn, so Lena took a step forward and smiled at the cashier. “Good morning. I’ll have a pumpkin spice latte. And that cream cheese danish over there. Yep, that one, thank you. And”—she pointed at Kara with her thumb—“she’ll have chamomile tea. No sugar,” she added cheerfully.

    Lena immediately took a few steps to the side, hoping to avoid damage in case Kara decided to throw a petri dish at her. She was surprised when Kara calmly handed her credit card to the boy behind the counter. Really, Kara wasn’t as bad as they made her out to be.

    “I hate tea,” Kara said. “And chamomile.”

    Lena beamed up at her. “That is unfortunate.”

    “You smartass.”

    Kara stared straight ahead, but Lena was almost certain that she was about to crack a smile. There was a lot to be said about Kara but not that she didn’t have a sense of humour.

    “So . . . not the haircut?”

    “Mm? Ah, no. It was a weird length,” Kara answered.

    Lena ran her hand through Kara’s hair, “Okay. Great. I like it like this.”

    “I . . .” Kara seemed flustered for a second, her lips moving without making a sound as she looked for an appropriate response. Then, out of the blue, she said, “I talked with the department chair this morning. He’s still refusing to release my research funds.”

    “Oh.” Lena cocked her head. “I thought they weren’t due to decide until August.”

    “They aren’t. This was an informal meeting, but the topic came up. He said that he’s still monitoring the situation.”

    “I see.” Lena waited for her to continue. When it became clear that Kara wouldn’t, she asked, “Monitoring . . . how?”

    “Unclear.”

    “I’m sorry. Does that mean that you can’t continue your research?”

    “I have other grants.”

    “So . . . the problem is that you cannot start new studies?”

    “I can. I had to rearrange different pots, but I should be able to afford to start new lines of research, too.”

    Uh? “I see.” Lena cleared her throat. “So . . . let me recap. It sounds like Stanford froze your funds based on rumours, which I agree is a crappy move. But it also sounds like for now you can afford to do what you were planning, so . . . it’s not the end of the world?” Kara gave her an affronted glare, suddenly looking even more cross. Oh, boy. “Don’t get me wrong, I understand the principle of the matter, and I’d be mad, too. But you have, how many other grants? Actually, don’t answer that. I’m not sure I want to know.”

    Kara probably had fifteen. She also had tenure, and dozens of publications, and there were all those honours listed on her website. Not to mention that she’d read on hers CV that Kara had one patent. Lena, on the other hand, had cheap knockoff reagents and old pipettes that regularly got stolen. She tried not to dwell on how much further ahead than her Kara was in her career, but it was unforgettable, how good Kara was at what she did. How annoyingly good.

    “My point is, this is not an insurmountable problem. And we’re actively working on it. We’re in this together, showing people that you’re going to stay here forever because of your amazing girlfriend.” Lena pointed to herself with a flourish, and Kara’s glare followed her hand. Clearly Kara was not a fan of rationalizing and working through her emotions. “Or, you could stay mad, and we could go to your lab and throw test tubes full of toxic reagents at each other until the pain of third-degree burns overrides your shitty mood? Sounds like fun, no?”

    Kara looked away and rolled her eyes, but Lena could see it in the curve of her cheeks that she was amused. Likely against her will. “You are such a smartass, baby.”

    “Aww you say the sweetest things darling, but may I remind you I’m not the one who grunted when asked how your week was.”

    “I did not grunt. And you ordered me chamomile tea.”

    Lena smiled. “You’re welcome.”

    They were quiet for a few moments as Lena chewed through the first bite of her Danish. Once she’d swallowed she said, “I’m sorry about your funds.”

    Kara shook her head. “I’m sorry about the mood.”

    Oh. “It’s okay. You’re famous for that.”

    “I am?”

    “Yep. It’s kind of your thing.”

    “Is that so?”

    “Mmm.”

    “Maybe I wanted to spare you.”

    Lena smiled, because it was actually a nice thing to say. And Kara was not a nice person, but she was very kind to her most of the time—if not always. She was almost smiling back, staring down at her in a way that Lena couldn’t quite interpret but that made her think weird thoughts, until the barista deposited their drinks on the counter.

    Kara suddenly looked like she was about to retch.

    “Kara? Are you okay?”

    Kara stared at Lena’s cup and took a step back. “The smell of that thing.”

    Lena inhaled deeply. Heaven. “You hate pumpkin spice latte?”

    Kara wrinkled her nose, moving even farther away. “Gross.”

    “How can you hate it? It’s the best thing produced in the past century.”

    “Please, stand back. The stench.”

    “Hey. If I have to choose between you and pumpkin spice latte, maybe we should rethink our arrangement.”

    Kara eyed her cup like it contained kryptonite. “Maybe we should.”

    Kara held the door open for her as they exited the coffee shop, taking care not to come too close to her drink. Outside it was starting to drizzle. Students were hastily packing up their laptops and notebooks from the patio tables to head to class or move to the library. Lena had been in love with the rain since as far back as she could remember. She inhaled deeply and filled her lungs with petrichor, stopping with Kara under the canopy.

    Kara took a sip of her chamomile tea, and it made Lena smile.

    “Hey,” Lena said, “I have an idea. Are you going to the fall biosciences picnic?”

    Kara nodded. “I have to. I’m on the biology department’s social-and-networking committee.”

    Lena laughed out loud. “No way.”

    “Yep.”

    “Did you actually sign up for it?”

    “It’s service. I was forced to rotate into the position.”

    “Ah. That sounds . . . fun.” Lena winced sympathetically, almost laughing again at Kara’s appalled expression. “Well, I’m going, too. Dr. Daxam makes us all go, says it promotes bonding among lab mates. Do you make your grads go?”

    “No. I have other, more productive ways of making my grads miserable.”

    Lena chuckled. Kara was funny, in that weird, dark way of hers. “I bet you do. Well, here’s my idea: we should hang when we’re there. In front of the department chair—since he’s ‘monitoring.’ I’ll bat my eyelashes at you; he’ll see that we’re basically one step away from marriage. Then he’ll make a quick phone call and a truck will drive up and unload your research funds in cash right there in front of—”

    “Hey, dude!” A brunette man approached Kara.

    Lena fell silent as Kara turned to smile at him and exchanged a hug. She blinked, wondering if she was seeing things, and took a sip of her latte.

    “I thought you’d sleep in,” Kara said.

    “The time difference screwed me up. I figured I might as well come to campus and get to work. Something to eat, too. You have no food.”

    “There are apples in the kitchen.”

    “Right. No food.”

    Lena couldn’t help but feel a twinge of jealousy as she watched Kara and this man interact. Why was Kara so comfortable around him? Why was he staying with Kara? Are they a thing? Lena took a step back, ready to excuse herself when the man turned his attention to her. He looked eerily familiar, even though she was certain she had never met him before.

    “And who’s this?” he asked curiously. His eyes were a very piercing blue.

    “This is Lena,” Kara said. There was a beat after her name, in which Kara should have probably specified how she knew Lena. She did not, and Lena really couldn’t blame her for not wanting to feed their fake-dating crap to someone who was clearly a good friend, or maybe more? Lena just kept her smile in place and let Kara continue. “Lena, this is my collaborator—”

    “Dude.” The man pretended to bristle. “Introduce me as your friend.”

    Kara rolled her eyes, clearly amused. “Lena, this is my friend and collaborator. Dr. Michael Matthew.

    The Girlfriend

    HYPOTHESIS: The more I need my brain to be on top of its game, the higher the probability that it will freeze on me.

    “Wait a minute.” Dr. Matthew tilted his head. His smile was still in place, but his gaze became a little sharper, his focus on Lena less superficial. “Do you happen to be . . .”

    Lena froze.

    Her mind was never calm, or orderly—more like a garbled mess of thoughts, really. And yet, standing there in front of Michael Matthew, the inside of her head went uncharacteristically quiet, and several considerations stacked themselves neatly into place.

    The first was that she was comically luckless. The chances that the person she depended on to finish her beloved research project would be acquainted—no, friends with the person she depended on to ensure her beloved Andy’s romantic happiness were laughably low. And yet. Then again, Lena’s special brand of luck was no news, so she moved on to the next consideration.

    She needed to admit who she was to Dr. Matthew. They were scheduled to meet at 3:00 p.m. and pretending not to recognize him now would mean the kiss of death to her plans to infiltrate herself into his lab. Academics had huge egos, after all.

    Last consideration: if she phrased this right, she could probably avoid Dr. Matthew hearing about the whole fake dating mess. Kara hadn’t mentioned it, which probably meant that she wasn’t planning to. Lena just needed to follow her lead.

    Yes. Excellent plan. She had this in the bag.

    Lena smiled, held on to her pumpkin spice latte, and answered, “Yes, I’m Lena Luthor, the—”

    “Girlfriend I’ve heard so much about?”

    Shit. Shit, shit, shit. She swallowed.

    “Um, actually I—”

    “Heard from whom?” Kara asked, frowning.

    Dr. Matthew shrugged. “Everyone.”

    “Everyone,” Kara repeated. She was scowling now. “In Boston?”

    “Yeah.”

    “Why are people at Harvard talking about my girlfriend?”

    “Because you’re you.”

    “Because I’m me?” Kara looked confused.

    “There have been tears. Some hair-pulling. A few broken hearts. Don’t worry, they’ll get over it.”

    Kara rolled her eyes, and Dr. Matthew returned his attention to Lena. He smiled at her, offering his hand. “It’s very nice to meet you. I had written off the whole girlfriend thing as rumours, but I’m glad you . . . exist. Sorry, I didn’t catch your name—I’m terrible at names.”

    “I’m Lena.” She shook his hand. He had a nice grip, not too tight and not too soft.

    “Which department do you teach, Lena?”

    Oh, crap. “Actually, I don’t. Teach, that is.”

    “Oh, sorry. I didn’t mean to assume.” He smiled, apologetic and self-effacing. There was a smooth charm to him. He was young to be a professor, though not as young as Kara. And he was tall, though not as tall as Kara. And he was good-looking though...yeah. Not as good-looking as Kara “What do you do, then? Are you a research fellow?”

    “Um, I actually—”

    “She’s a student,” Kara said.

    Dr. Matthew’s eyes widened.

    “A graduate student,” Kara clarified. There was a hint of warning in her tone, like she really wanted Dr. Matthew to drop the subject.

    Dr. Matthew, naturally, did not. “Your graduate student?”

    Kara frowned. “No, of course she’s not my—”

    This was the perfect opening. “Actually, Dr. Matthew, I work with Dr. Daxam.” Maybe this meeting was still salvageable. “You probably don’t recognize my name, but we’ve corresponded. We’re supposed to meet today. I’m the student who’s working on the pancreatic cancer biomarkers. The one who asked to come work in your lab for a year.”

    Dr. Matthew’s eyes widened even more, and he muttered something that sounded a lot like “What the hell?” Then his face stretched into a wide, open-mouthed grin. “Kara, you absolute ass. You didn’t even tell me.”

    “I didn’t know,” Kara muttered. Her gaze was fixed on Lena.

    “How could you not know that your girlfriend—”

    “I didn’t tell Kara, because I didn’t know you two were friends,” Lena interjected. And then she thought that maybe it wasn’t quite believable. If Lena really were Kara’s girlfriend, she’d have told Lena about her friends. Since, in a shocking plot twist, she did appear to have at least one.

    “That is, I, um . . . never put two and two together, and didn’t know that you were the Michael she always talked about.” There, better. Kind of. “I’m sorry, Dr. Matthew. I didn’t mean to —”

    “Mike,” he said, grin still in place. His shock seemed to be settling into a pleasant surprise. “Please, call me Mike.” His eyes darted between Kara and Lena for a few seconds. Then he said, “Hey, are you free?” He pointed at the coffee shop. “Why don’t we go inside and chat about your project now? No point in waiting until this afternoon.”

    Lena took a sip of her latte to temporize. Was she free? Technically, yes. She would have loved to run to the edge of campus and scream into the void until modern civilization collapsed, but that wasn’t exactly a pressing matter. And she wanted to look as accommodating as possible to Dr. Matthew— Mike. Beggars and choosers and all that.

    “I’m free.”

    “Great. You, Kara?”

    Lena froze.

    And so did Kara, for about a second, before pointing out, “I don’t think I should be present, if you’re about to interview her—”

    “Oh, it’s not an interview. Just an informal chat to see if Lena’s and my research match. You’ll want to know if your girlfriend is moving to Boston for a year, right? Come on.” Mike motioned for them to follow him and then stepped inside the Starbucks.

    Lena and Kara exchanged a silent look that somehow managed to speak volumes. It said, What the hell do we do? and How the hell would I know? and This is going to be weird, and No, it’s going to be plain bad. Then Kara sighed, put on a resigned face, and headed inside. Lena followed her, regretting her life choices.

    “Daxam’s retiring, huh?” Mike asked after they’d found a secluded table in the back.

    Lena had no choice but to sit across from him—and on Kara’s left. Like a good ‘girlfriend’ she supposed. Kara in the meantime was sullenly sipping her chamomile tea.

    “In the next few years,” Lena confirmed. She loved her adviser, who had always been supportive and encouraging. Since the very beginning she had given Lena the freedom to develop her own research program, which was almost unheard of for Ph.D. students. Having a hands-off mentor was great when it came to pursuing her interests, but . . .

    “If Daxam’s retiring soon, she’s not applying for grants anymore—understandable, since she won’t be around long enough to see the projects through—which means that your lab is not exactly flush with cash right now,” Mike summarized perfectly. “Okay, tell me about your project. What’s cool about it?”

    “I . . . ,” Lena began—she scrambled to collect her thoughts. “So, it’s—” Another pause. Longer this time, and more painfully awkward. “Um . . .”

    “What kind of question is that?” Kara interjected.

    When Lena glanced at her, she was scowling at Mike, who just shrugged.

    “What’s cool about your project?” Kara repeated back.

    “Yeah. Cool. You know what I mean.”

    “I don’t think I do, and maybe neither does Lena.”

    Mike huffed. “Fine, what would you ask?”

    Kara turned to Lena, her knee brushed Lena’s leg, warm and oddly reassuring through her jeans. “What issues does your project target? Why do you think it’s significant? What gaps in the literature does it fill? What techniques are you using? What challenges do you foresee?”

    Mike huffed. “Right, sure. Consider all those long, boring questions asked, Lena.”

    Lena was reminded of that girl from the bathroom, from years ago. ‘I have no idea if you’re good enough’ she’d told her. ‘What matters is whether your reason to be in academia is good enough’. She’d said that Lena’s reason was the best one, and therefore, she could do this. She needed to do this.

    “Okay,” she started again after a deep breath, gathering what she’d rehearsed the previous night with Sam. “Here’s the deal. Pancreatic cancer is very aggressive and deadly. It has very poor prognosis, with only one out of four people alive a year after diagnosis.” Her voice, she thought, sounded less breathy and more self-assured. Good. “The problem is that it’s so hard to detect, we are only able to diagnose it very late in the game. At that point, the cancer has already spread so widely, most treatments can’t do much to counteract it. But if diagnosis were faster—”

    “People could get treatment sooner and have a higher chance of survival,” Mike said, nodding a bit impatiently. “Yep, I’m well aware. We already have some screening tools, though. Like imaging.”

    Lena wasn’t surprised he brought it up, since imaging was what Mike’s lab focused on. “Yes, but that’s expensive, time-consuming, and often not useful because of the pancreas’s position. But . . .” She took another deep breath. “I think I have found a set of biomarkers. Not from tissue biopsy— blood biomarkers. Non-invasive, easy to obtain. Cheap. In mice they can detect pancreatic cancer as early as stage one.”

    She paused. Mike and Kara were both staring at her. Mike was clearly interested, and Kara looked . . . a little weird, to be honest. Impressed, maybe? Nah, impossible.

    “Okay. This sounds promising. What’s the next step?”

    “Collecting more data. Running more analyses with better equipment to prove that my set of biomarkers is worthy of a clinical trial. But for that I need a larger lab.”

    “I see.” Mike nodded with a thoughtful expression and then leaned back in his chair. “Why pancreatic cancer?”

    “It’s one of the most lethal, and we know so little about how—”

    “No,” Mike interrupted. “Most third-year Ph.D. students are too busy infighting over the centrifuge to come up with their own line of research. There must be a reason you’re so motivated. Did someone close to you have cancer?”

    Lena swallowed before reluctantly answering, “Yes.”

    “Who?”

    “Mike,” Kara said, a trace of warning in her voice. Her knee was still against Lena’s thigh. Still warm. And yet, Lena felt her blood turn cold. She really, really didn’t want to say it. And yet she couldn’t ignore the question. She needed Mike’s help.

    “My mother.”

    Okay. It was out there now. She’d said it, and she could go back to trying not to think about it—

    “Did she die?”

    Lena hesitated and then nodded silently, not looking at either Mike or Kara.

    “How old were you—”

    “Mike,” Kara interrupted, tone sharp. She set her tea down with more force than necessary. “Stop harassing my girlfriend.”

    It was less of a warning and more of a threat.

    “Right. Yes. I’m an insensitive ass.” Mike smiled, apologetic.

    Lena noticed that he was looking at her shoulder. When she followed his gaze, she realized that Kara had placed her arm on the back of her chair. She wasn’t touching Lena, but there was something . . . protective about the position.

    “Okay, Lena. Tell you what.” Mike leaned forward, elbows on the table. “I’ve read your paper. And the abstract you submitted to the SBD conference. Are you still planning to go?”

    “If it’s accepted.”

    “I’m sure it will be. It’s excellent work. But it sounds like your project has progressed since you submitted that, and I need to know more about it. If I decide that you can work in my lab next year, I’ll cover you completely—salary, supplies, equipment, whatever you need. But I need to know where you’re at to make sure that you’re worth investing in.”

    Lena felt her heart racing. This sounded promising. Very promising.

    “Here’s the deal. I’m going to give you two weeks to write up a report on everything you’ve been doing so far— protocols, findings, challenges. In two weeks, send me the report and I’ll make a decision based on it. Does that sound feasible?” Mike asked.

    Lena grinned, nodding enthusiastically. “Yes!” She could absolutely do that. She’d need to pull the intro from one of her papers, the methods from her lab protocols, the preliminary data from that grant she’d applied for and not won. And she’d have to rerun some of her analyses—just to make sure that the report was absolutely flawless for Mike. It would be lots of work in little time, but who needed sleep? Or bathroom breaks?

    “Great. In the meantime, I’ll see you around and we can chat more. Kara and I will be joined at the hip for a couple of weeks since we’re working on that grant we just got. Are you coming to my talk tomorrow?”

    Lena had no idea he was giving a talk, let alone when or where, but she said “Of course! Can’t wait!” with the certainty of someone who had installed a countdown widget on her smartphone.

    “And I’m staying with Kara, so I’ll see you at her place.”

    Oh no. “Um . . .” She risked a glance at Kara, who was unreadable. “Sure. Though we usually meet at my place, so . . .”

    “I see. You disapprove of her taxidermy collection, don’t you?” Mike stood with a smirk. “It was a pleasure meeting you, Lena. I’ll see you back at yours Kar and please buy food, apples aren’t food.”

    The second he was gone, Lena instantly turned to Kara. Now that they were alone there were about ten million topics for them to debrief on, but the only thing she could think of was, “Do you really collect taxidermized animals?”

    Kara gave her a scathing look and took her arm away from around Lena’s shoulders.

    “I’m sorry. I had no idea he was your friend, or that you two had a grant together. You do such different research, the possibility didn’t even cross my mind.”

    “You did mention that you don’t believe cancer researchers can benefit from collaborating with computational modelists.”

    “You—” Lena noticed the way Kara’s mouth was twitching and wondered when exactly they’d gotten on teasing terms. “How do you two know each other?”

    “He was a postdoc in my lab, back when I was a Ph.D. student. We’ve kept in touch and collaborated through the years.”

    So Mike must be four or five years older than Kara. “You went to Harvard, right?”

    Kara nodded, and a terrifying thought occurred to Lena.

    “What if he feels obliged to take me on because I’m your fake girlfriend?”

    “Mike won’t. He once fired his cousin for breaking a flow cytometer. He’s not exactly tender-hearted.”

    Takes one to know one, Lena thought. “Listen, I’m sorry this is forcing you to lie to your friend. If you want to tell him that this is fake . . .”

    Kara shook her head. “If I did, I’d never live it down.”

    Lena bent her head down and blushed. “Thanks.”

    Kara lifted her chin with two fingers and stared into her eyes. She took a step closer but they were interrupted by a throat clearing.

    “If you are done Miss Luthor please make your way to your seat,” Dr. Vazquez said.

    “Bye baby.”

    "Bye...your majesty," Lena said, subtly bowing.

    Kara chuckled. "Smartass."

    The Talk

    Chapter Notes

    HYPOTHESIS: When compared with multiple types and models of furniture, Kara Zor-El’s lap will be rated in the top fifth percentile for comfort, cosiness, and enjoyment.

    The moment Lena opened the door of the auditorium she and Andrea exchanged a wide-eyed look and said, in unison, “Holy shit.”

    In her two years at Stanford she had been to countless seminars, trainings, lectures, and classes in this lecture hall, and yet she’d never seen the room this full. Maybe Dr. Matthew was giving out free beer?

    “I think they made the talk mandatory for immunology and pharmacology,” Andrea said. “And I overheard at least five people in the hallway saying that Matthew is ‘a known science hottie.’”. She stared critically at the podium, where Dr. Mathhew was chatting with Dr. Ardeen from immunology. “I guess he’s cute. Though not nearly as cute as Jack.”

    Lena smiled. The air in the room was hot and humid, smelling like sweat and too many human beings. “You don’t have to stay. This is probably a fire hazard and not even remotely relevant to your research—”

    “It beats doing actual work.” Andrea grabbed Lena’s wrist, pulling her through the throng of grads and postdocs crowding the entrance and down the stairs on the side. They were just as packed. “And if this guy is going to take you away from me and to Boston for an entire year, I want to make sure that he deserves you.” She winked. “Consider my presence the equivalent of a father cleaning his rifle in front of his daughter’s boyfriend or girlfriend before prom.”

    There was nowhere to sit, of course, not even on the floor or on the steps. Lena spotted Kara in an aisle seat a few meters away. She was back to her usual black shirt and deep in conversation with Alex Danvers. When Kara’s eyes met Lena’s, Lena grinned and waved at her. For some yet unknown reason that likely had to do with the fact that they were sharing this huge, ridiculous, unlikely secret, Kara now felt like a friendly face. Kara didn’t wave back, but her gaze seemed softer and warmer, and her mouth curved into that tilt that Lena had learned to recognize as Kara’s version of a smile.

No, no, no.” Lena followed Andrea’s gaze and saw at least twenty new people arrive. The crowd immediately started pushing Lena toward the front of the room. Andrea yelped when a first-year from neuroscience who weighed about four times as much as she did stepped on her toe. “This is ridiculous.”

    “I know. I can’t believe more people are—” Lena’s hip bumped against something—someone.

    Lena turned to apologize, and—it was Kara. She was still chatting with Dr. Danvers, who wore a displeased expression and was muttering, “Why are we even here?”

    “Because he’s a friend,” Kara said.

    “Not my friend.”

    Kara sighed and turned to look at Lena.

    “Hey—sorry.” Lena gestured in the direction of the entrance. “A bunch of new people just came in and apparently the space in this room is finite. I think it’s a law of physics, or something.”

    “It’s okay,” Kara said.

    “I’d take a step back, but . . .”

    On the podium, Dr. Ardeen took the mic and began introducing Dr. Matthew.

    “Here,” Kara told Lena, making to stand from her chair. “Take my seat.”

    “Oh.” It was nice of her to offer. Not fake-dating-to-save-her-ass, spend-twenty-bucks-on-junk-food-for-her nice, but still very nice. Lena couldn’t possibly accept. Plus, Kara was a professor, which meant that she was older and all that. Thirtysomething. She did look fit, but she probably had a bum knee or something. “Thank you, but—”

    “Actually, that would be a terrible idea,” Andrea interjected. Her eyes were darting between Lena and Kara. “No offense, Dr. Zor-El, but you’re two times larger than Lena. If you stand, the room’s going to burst.”

    Lena blinked. And then she blinked again. And then she blinked some more.

    Near the podium, Dr. Ardeen was still introducing Dr. Matthew—“Got his Ph.D. from Vanderbilt and then moved to a postdoctoral fellowship at Harvard University, where he pioneered several techniques in the field of imaging”—but her voice sounded as if it was coming from far, far away. Possibly because Lena couldn’t stop thinking about what Andrea had proposed, which was just . . .

    “Andy, I don’t think it’s a good idea,” Lena mumbled under her breath, avoiding glancing in Kara’s direction.

    Andrea gave her a look. “Why? You’re taking up space we don’t have, and it’s only logical that you use Zor-El as a chair. I would, but she’s your girlfriend, not mine.”

    For a moment, Lena tried to imagine what Kara would do if Andrea decided to sit on her lap, and figured that it would probably end up involving someone being murdered and someone doing the murdering—she wasn’t sure who’d be doing what. The mental image was so ridiculous that she almost giggled out loud. Then she noticed the way Andrea was looking at her expectantly.

    “Andy, I can’t.”

    “Why?”

    “Because. This is a scientific talk.”

    “Psh. Remember last year, when Jess and Winn made out for half of that CRISPR lecture?”

    “Nah, it wasn’t. Also, Sam swears that during a seminar she saw that tall guy from immunology get a hand job from—”

    Because millions of dollars in research funds couldn’t be worth having some girl she barely knew sit on her lap in the most crowded room in the history of crowded rooms.

    Is this okay? Lena tried to ask Kara with her eyes. Because maybe this is a little too much. Way more than saying hi to each other and having coffee together.

    Lena gasped. “My legs aren’t tiny, they are just fine, you’re the one with abnormally large…everything.”

    “If you two are done discussing the size of each others body parts, can you go sit on her lap Lee? I can barely breathe,” Andrea interrupted.

    Lena sighed and stepped toward Kara and sat on her thigh, her knees tucked between Kara’s spread legs.

    Lena was there.

    Sitting.

    On.

    Kara.

    This. Yep, this. This was her life now. She was going to murder Andrea for this. Slowly. Maybe painfully, too. She was going to be jailed for bestfriendicide, and she was a-okay with it.

Lena could smell her— the woodsiness of her shampoo, her body wash, and something else underneath, dark and good and clean. It all felt familiar, and after a few seconds Lena realized that it was because of the last time they had been this close. Because of The Night. Because of the kiss. “So, so sorry.”

    “I’m sorry. I had no idea she would suggest this, and I couldn’t think of a way to—”

Her voice was low when she added, “It’s fine, baby” The words vibrated in Lena’s ear, rich and warm. “More material for my Title IX complaint.”

    Shit. “God, I’m so sorry—”

    “Lena.” Lena lifted her eyes to catch Kara’s and was shocked to find her . . . not smiling, but something like it. “I was kidding. You weigh nothing. I don’t mind.”

    “I—”

    “Ssh. Just focus on the talk. Mike might ask you questions about it.”

    This was just . . . Seriously, this whole business, it was completely, utterly . . . Comfortable. Kara Zor-El’s lap was one of the most comfortable places on earth, as it turned out. She was warm and solid in a pleasant, soothing way, and she didn’t seem to mind too much having Lena half draped over her.

    “It’s just that I’ve read all of Mike’s articles. I already know what he’s saying.”

    “Yeah, same. We cited all this stuff in our grant proposal.” Kara sighed, and Lena felt Kara’s body move under hers. “This is dull.”

    Kara seemed to think about it. “No.” She hesitated. “It looks like your friends are buying that we’re together.”

    “I think so. I’m not exactly a convincing liar, and sometimes I worry that Andrea might get suspicious. But I walked in on her and Jack making out in the grad lounge the other day.”

    They fell quiet and listened to the last few minutes of the talk in silence. In front of them, Lena could see at least two professors taking a nap, and several surreptitiously working on their laptops. Next to Kara, Dr. Danvers had been playing Candy Crush on her phone for the past half an hour. Some people had left, and Andrea had found a seat about ten minutes ago. So had several of the students who had been next to Lena, which meant that she could have technically stood up and left Kara alone. Technically. Technically, there was an open chair somewhere in the third-to-last row. Technically.

Better than she ever thought it would. Kara blinked once and then nodded. Maybe her arm tensed a little around Lena. Maybe it didn’t, and Lena’s mind was playing tricks on her. It was starting to get late, after all. Her last coffee had been too long ago, and she wasn’t fully awake, her thoughts fuzzy and relaxed. “What about you?”

Aliens already stand out for being from a different planet, the last thing we need is to attract more attention to us and be viewed as a threat.”

    Kara chuckled. “No, it does not baby.”

    After the talk, Lena considered walking down to the podium to compliment Dr. Matthew and ask him one or two questions she already knew the answers to. Sadly, there were dozens of people waiting to speak to him, and she decided that the asskissing wasn’t worth standing in line.     “A fair amount. I need to run a few control studies to make my results stronger. Plus there’s other stuff I should be working on—the TA’ing, and my poster presentation for the SBD conference in Boston.” Lena bent her head back, felt the sun warm her skin, and smiled. “If I hole up in the lab every night this week and the next, I should be able to finish it on time.”

    “SBD is something to look forward to, at least.”

    Lena nodded. She usually wasn’t a fan of academic conferences, given how prohibitively expensive registration, travel, and lodgings could be. But Sam and Andrea were going to be at SBD, too, and Lena was excited to explore Boston with them. Plus, the intradepartmental drama that always happened at academic functions with open bars was sure to be A+ entertainment.

    “I am organizing this outreach event for BIPOC women in STEM from all over the country—I’m going to get Ph.D. students like me to talk face-to-face with undergrads who are applying and reassure them that if they come to grad school they won’t be alone.”

She and Andrea walked around a line of vehicles stuck in the lot, until they ran into a group of biology grads.

He pointed at a red truck stuck sideways in the most inconvenient turn.     The Picnic

    HYPOTHESIS: There will be a significant positive correlation between the amount of sunscreen poured in my hands and the intensity of my desire to murder Andrea.

    Mikes report was about a third done and sitting tight at thirty four pages single-spaced, Times New Roman (12 point), no justification. It was 11:00 a.m., and Lena had been working in the lab since about five—analyzing peptide samples, writing down protocol notes, taking covert naps while the PCR machine ran—when James barged in, looking absolutely furious.

    It was unusual, but not too unusual. James was a bit of a hothead to begin with, and grad school came with a lot of angry outbursts in semi-public places, usually for reasons that, Lena was fully aware, would appear ridiculous to someone who’d never stepped foot in academia. They’re making me TA Intro to Bio for the fourth time in a row; the paper I need is behind a paywall; I had a meeting with my supervisor and accidentally called her “Mom.”

    James and Lena shared an adviser, Dr. Daxam, and while they’d always gotten along fine, they had never been particularly close. Lena had hoped, by picking a female adviser, to avoid some of the nastiness that was so often directed at women in STEM. Unfortunately, she had still found herself in an all-male lab, which was . . . a less-than-ideal environment. That was why when James came in, slammed the door, and then threw a folder on his bench, Lena was not sure what to do. She watched him sit down and begin to sulk. Winn, another lab mate, followed him inside a moment later with an uneasy expression and started patting his back.

    Lena looked longingly at her RNA samples. Then she stepped closer to James’ bench and asked, “What’s wrong?”

    She had expected the answer to be ‘The production of my reagent has been discontinued, or My p-value is .06, or Grad school was a mistake, but now it’s too late to back out of it because my self-worth is unbreakably tied to my academic performance, and what would even be left of me if I decided to drop out?’

    Instead, what she got was: “Your stupid girlfriend is what’s wrong.”

    By now the fake dating had been going on for over two weeks: Lena didn’t startle anymore when someone referred to Kara as her girlfriend. Still, James’ words were so unexpected and full of venom that she couldn’t help but answer, “Who?”

    “Zor-El.” He spat the name out like a curse.

    “Oh.”

    “She’s on James’ dissertation committee,” Winn explained in a significantly milder tone, not quite meeting Lena’s eyes.

    “Oh. Right.” This could be bad. Very bad. “What happened?”

    “She failed my proposal.”

    “Shit.” Lena bit into her lower lip. “I’m sorry, James.”

    “This is going to set me back a lot. It’ll take me months to revise it, all because Zor-El had to go and nitpick. I didn’t even want her on my committee; Dr. Daxam forced me to add her because she’s so obsessed with her stupid computational stuff.”

    Lena chewed on the inside of her cheek, trying to come up with something meaningful to say and failing miserably. “I’m really sorry.”

    “Lena, do you guys talk about this stuff?” Winn asked out of the blue, eyeing her suspiciously. “Did she tell you she wasn’t going to pass James?”

I should just pocket my chips and my candy and go back to my stupid protein samples, but for some reason you are so comfortable to be around. And I am drawn to you, even though I don’t know why.

    “Do you plan to stay in academia?” Kara asked. “After you graduate.”

    “Yes. Maybe. No.”

    Kara smiled, and Lena laughed. “Undecided.”

    “Right.”

    “It’s just . . . there are things that I love about it. Being in the lab, doing research. Coming up with study ideas, feeling that I’m doing something meaningful. But if I go the academic route, then I’ll also need to do a lot of other things that I just . . .” She shook her head.

    “Other things?”

    “Yeah. The PR stuff, mostly. Write grants and convince people to fund my research. Network, which is a special kind of hell. Public speaking, or even one-on-one situations where I have to impress people. That’s the worst, actually. I hate it so much—my head explodes and I freeze and everyone is looking at me ready to judge me and my tongue paralyzes and I start wishing that I was dead and then that the world was dead and —” She noticed Kara’s smile and gave her a rueful look. “You get the gist.”

    “There are things you can do about that, if you want. It just takes practice. Making sure your thoughts are organized. Stuff like that.”

    “I know. And I try to do that—I did it before my meeting with Mike. And I still stammered like an idiot when he asked me a simple question.” And then you helped me, ordered my thoughts, and saved my ass, without even meaning to. “I don’t know. Maybe my brain is broken.”

    Kara shook her head. “You did great during that meeting with Mike, especially considering that you were forced to have your fake girlfriend friend sit next to you. Mike certainly seemed impressed, which is no small feat. And if anyone screwed up, it was definitely him. He should not have forced you to speak about your personal life.”

    Lena looked away, toward the blue glow of the vending machine. “It’s okay. It had been a while. Since high school, really.”

    “That’s . . . young.”

    “I was fifteen. One day my mom and I were there, just . . . I don’t even know. Kayaking. Thinking about getting a cat. Arguing over the way I’d pile stuff on top of the trash can when it was overflowing, and I didn’t want to take it out. And next thing I knew she had her diagnosis, and three weeks later she’d already—” She couldn’t say it. Her lips, her vocal folds, her heart, they just wouldn’t form the words. So she swallowed them. “The child welfare system couldn’t figure out where to send me until I became of age and you already know the story about Lionel. My grandparents had died when I was a kid, because apparently that’s what people around me do.” She tried to say it jokingly, she really tried. To not sound bitter. She thought she even succeeded. “I was just . . . alone.”

    “What did you do?”

    “Foster home until sixteen, then I emancipated.” She shrugged, hoping to brush off the memory. “If only they’d caught it earlier, even just by a few months—maybe she’d be here. Maybe surgery and chemo would have actually done something. And I . . . I was always good at science stuff, so I thought that the least I could do was . . .”

    “I . . . maybe.” Kara pressed her lips together. “I panicked.”

    Lena chuckled wetly, accepting Kara’s gross tissue and using it to blow her nose. They’d kissed three times, after all. Why not share a bit of snot?

    “Like what?”

    “Weepy. I . . . I shouldn’t talk about this.”

    “Why? Lena, I already told you I’m always here if you want to talk and that involves when you get weepy. Next time I’ll have clean tissues I promise.”

    Lena waved her hand dismissively. “I’m not his favorite person, but it’s fine. He’ll get over it.” She bit into her lip. “I’m sorry about the other night. I was rude. You have every right to be mad.”

    Kara shook her head. “It’s okay. I understand where you were coming from.”

    “I do get what you’re saying. About not wanting to form a new generation of crappy millennial scientists.”

    “I don’t believe I’ve ever used the expression ‘crappy millennial scientists.’ ”

    “But FYI, I still think that you don’t need to be that harsh when you give feedback. We get the gist of what you’re saying, even if you give criticism more nicely.”

    Kara looked at her for a long time. Then she nodded, once. “Noted.”

    “Are you going to be less harsh, then?”

    “Unlikely.”

    Lena sighed. “You know, when I have no more friends and everyone hates me because of this fake-dating thing, I’ll be super lonely and you are going to have to hang out with me every day. I’ll annoy you all the time. Is it really worth being mean to every grad in the program?”

    “Good to hear.”

    “I should go back to the lab.” Lena sat up, one hand coming to massage the base of her neck. “My disastrous blotting is not going to fix itself.”

    Lena groaned. “It’s just . . .” She reached for her phone and tapped on the home button, pulling up a picture of her last Western blot. “See?” She pointed at the target protein. “This— it shouldn’t . . .”

    Kara nodded, thoughtful. “You’re sure the starting sample was good? And the gel?”

    “Yep, not runny, or dried out.”

    “It looks like the antibody might be the problem.”

    Lena looked up at her. “You think so?”

    “Yep. I’d check the dilution and the buffer. If not that, it might also be a wonky secondary antibody. Come by my lab if it still doesn’t work; you can borrow ours. Same for other pieces of equipment or supplies. If there’s anything you need, just ask my lab manager.”

    “Oh, wow. Thank you.” Lena smiled. “Now I’m actually a bit sorry that I can’t have you on my dissertation committee. Perhaps rumours of your cruelty have been greatly exaggerated.”

    Kara’s mouth twitched. “Maybe you just pull out the best in me?”

    Lena grinned. “Then maybe I should stick around. Just, you know, to save the department from your terrible moods?”

    Kara glanced at the picture of the failed Western blot in her hand. “Well, it doesn’t look like you’re going to graduate anytime soon.”

    Lena half laughed, half gasped. “Oh my God. Did you just —?”

    “Objectively—”

    “This is the rudest, meanest thing—” She was laughing. Holding her stomach as she waved her finger at Kara.

    “I think I can find meaner things. If I really put myself to it.”

    “We’re done.” She wished she weren’t smiling. Then maybe Kara would take her seriously instead of just looking at her with that patient, amused expression. “Seriously. It was nice while it lasted.” She made to stand and leave indignantly, but Kara grabbed the sleeve of her shirt and gently tugged at it until she was sitting down again, next to her on the narrow couch —maybe even a little closer than before. She continued glaring, but Kara regarded her blandly, clearly unperturbed.

    “There’s nothing bad about taking more than five years to graduate,” Kara offered in a conciliatory tone.

    Lena huffed. “You just want me to stay around forever. Until you have the biggest, fattest, strongest Title IX case to ever exist.”

    “That was my plan all along, in fact. The one and only reason I kissed you out of the blue.”

    “Oh, shut up.” Lena ducked her chin into her chest, biting into her lip and hoping Kara wouldn’t notice her grinning like the idiot she was. “Hey, can I ask you something?” Kara looked at her expectantly, like she seemed to a lot lately, so Lena continued, her tone softer and quieter. “Why are you really doing this?”

    “Doing what?”

    “The fake dating. I understand that you want to look like you’re not a flight risk, but . . . Why aren’t you really dating someone? I mean, you’re not that bad.”

    “High praise.”

    “No, come on, what I meant was . . . Based on your fake dating behaviour, I’m sure that a lot of women . . . well, some women would love to real date you.” Lena bit into her lip again, playing with the hole that was opening up on the knee of her jeans. “We’re friends. We weren’t when we started, but we are now. You can tell me.”

    “Are we?”

    Lena nodded. Yes. Yes, we are. Come on. “Well, you did just break one of the sacred tenets of academic friendships by mentioning my graduation timeline. But I’ll forgive you if you tell me if this is really better for you than . . . you know, getting a real girlfriend.”

    “It is.”

    “Really?”

    “Yes.” Kara seemed honest. She was honest. Kara was not a liar; Lena would bet her life on it.

    “Why, though? Do you enjoy sunscreen-mediated fondling? And the opportunity to donate hundreds of your dollars to the campus Starbucks?”

    Kara smiled faintly. And then she wasn’t smiling anymore. Not looking at her, either, but somewhere in the direction of the crumpled plastic wrapper that she’d tossed on the table a few minutes go.

    “Lena.” Kara took a deep breath. “You should know that—”

    “Oh my God!” They both startled, Lena considerably more so than Kara, and turned toward the entrance. Jack stood there, one hand dramatically clutching his sternum. “You guys scared the shit out of me. What are you doing sitting in the dark?”

    What are you doing here? Lena thought ungraciously.

    “Just chatting,” Lena said. Though it didn’t seem like a good descriptor of what was going on. And yet, she couldn’t put her finger on why.

    “You scared me,” Jack repeated once more. “Are you working on your report, Lee?”

    “Yeah.” She stole a quick glance at Kara, who was motionless and expressionless next to her. “Just taking a quick break. I was about to go back, actually.”

    “Oh, cool. Me too.” Jack smiled, pointing in the direction of his lab. “I need to go isolate a bunch of virgin fruit flies. Before they’re not virgins anymore, you know?” He wiggled his eyebrows, and Lena had to force out a small, unconvincing laugh. She usually enjoyed his sense of humour. Usually. Now she just wished . . . She wasn’t sure what she wished.

    “You coming with, Lee?”

    No, I’m fine right here, actually.

    “Sure.” Reluctantly, she stood.

    Kara did the same, gathering their wrappers and her empty bottle and sorting them in the recycling bins.

    “Have a good night, Dr. Zor-El,” Jack said from the entrance.

    Kara just nodded at him, a touch curtly. The set of her eyes was yet again impossible to decipher.

    I guess that’s it, then, Lena thought. Where the weight in her chest had come from, she had no clue. She was probably just tired. Had eaten too much, or not enough.

    “Bye Kara.” she murmured before Kara could head for the entrance and leave the room. Her voice was pitched low enough that Jack couldn’t possibly have heard her. Maybe Kara hadn’t, either. Except that Kara paused for a moment. And then, when she walked past her, Lena had the impression of knuckles brushing against the back of her hand.

    “Good night, baby.”

    The Report

    HYPOTHESIS: The more I mention an attachment in an email, the less likely I will be to actually include said attachment.

    SATURDAY, 6:34 p.m.

    FROM: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu
    TO: Michael-Matthew@harvard.edu

    SUBJECT: Re: Report on Pancreatic Cancer Study

    Hi Mike,
    Here is the report you asked for, with a detailed description of what I have done so far and my thoughts on future directions and the resources I will need to expand. I’m excited to hear your thoughts on my work!

    Sincerely,
    Lena

    SATURDAY, 6:35 p.m.
    FROM: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu
    TO: Michael-Matthew@harvard.edu

    SUBJECT: Re: Report on Pancreatic Cancer Study

    Hi Mike,
    Oops, forgot the attachment.
    Sincerely,
    Lena

    TODAY, 3:20 p.m.

    FROM: Michael-Matthew@harvard.edu
    TO: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu

    SUBJECT: Re: Report on Pancreatic Cancer Study
    Lena,
    Done reading the report. Do you think you could come over to Kara’s to chat about it? Maybe tomorrow morning (Tue) at nine? Kara and I will be leaving for Boston on Wed afternoon.

    MM

    Lena’s heart beat faster—whether at the idea of being in Kara’s home or at the thought of getting her answer from Mike, she wasn’t sure. She immediately texted Kara.

    Lena: Mike just invited me to your place to talk about the report I sent him. Would it be okay if I came over?

    Kara: Of course. When?

    Lena: Tomorrow at 9 a.m. Will you be home?

    Kara: Probably. There are no bike lanes to my house. Do you need a ride? I can pick you up.

    She thought about it for a few moments and decided that she liked the idea a little too much.

    Lena: My roommate can drive me, but thanks for offering.

    --
    Sam dropped her off in front of a beautiful Spanish colonial house with stucco walls and arched windows and refused to back out of the driveway until Lena agreed to slide a can of pepper spray into her backpack. She walked over the brick-tile path and up to the entrance, marvelling at the green of the yard and at the cozy atmosphere of the porch. She was about to ring the doorbell when she heard her name.

    Kara was behind her, bathed in sweat and clearly just back from her morning run. She was wearing sunglasses, shorts, and a Princeton Undergrad Mathletes T-shirt that stuck to her body. Out of the ensemble, the only nonblack items were the AirPods in her ears, peeking through the damp waves of her hair.

    Lena felt her cheeks curve into a smile, trying to imagine what Kara was listening to. Probably Coil, or Kraftwerk. The Velvet Underground. A TED Talk on water-efficient landscaping. Whale noises. She would have given a huge chunk of her salary in exchange for five minutes alone with Kara’s phone, just to mess with her playlist. Add Taylor Swift, Ariana, and maybe some Olivia Rodrigo. She couldn’t see Kara’s eyes behind the dark lenses, but she didn’t need to.

    Lena realized that she’d been staring. “Um, yeah. Sorry. You?”

    Kara nodded. “Did you find the house all right?”

    “Yes. I was just about to knock.”

    “No need.” Kara passed her and opened the door, waiting until she’d stepped inside to close it after them.

    Kara’s place was light, spacious, and simply furnished.

    “No taxidermized animals?” Lena asked under her breath.

    Kara was clearly about to flip her off when they found Mike in the kitchen, typing on his laptop. He looked up at Lena and grinned—which, she hoped, was a good sign.

    “Thanks for coming, Lena. I wasn’t sure I’d have time to go to campus before leaving. Sit down, please.”

Mike was taking notes on his laptop.

    Oh.

He’d asked lots of interesting, smart questions about her project, but he hadn’t told her whether he wanted to work with her next year. What if he’d just been faking it because Kara was his friend? Kara had said that Mike wasn’t like that, but what if she’d been wrong and now—

    Kara is going to be the death of her.

    “You are known for your hands-on approach to things,” Lena smirked.

    “You guys are disgusting,” Andrea grimaced.

    “And right in front of my cheesecake,” Jack added.

    Lena laughed. “Relax we are just messing with you guys. So, I have some good news-”

    “Oh my God you eloped and got married,” Andrea interrupted.

    “No, we did not get married. Dr. Matthews is letting me join his lab next year.”

    Andrea jumped out of her chair and threw her hands around Lena. “Oh, I’m so proud of you Lee. I told you he couldn’t refuse you, you’re brilliant.”

    “Thank you, Andy. I am going to miss you all while I’m gone though.”

    Andrea sat back in her chair. “I’m going to miss you like crazy and will probably visit you every weekend but it’s going to be so worth it because you are going to do amazing things. I knew from the day I met you that you were destined for greatness, and this is another step towards it.”

    Lena wiped the tears that escaped her eyes. “I love you so much Andy, you’re the best friend ever.”

    “Congratulations Lee,” Jack said.

    “Thank you. Okay enough about me, Kara and I came here to celebrate both of our successes. Whilst being the most antagonistic person I know, my girlfriend is also the most intelligent person I know, and she and Mike got their grant approved.” Lena pressed a kiss on Kara’s cheek. “I’m really proud of you darling.”

    Kara blushed. “Thank you, baby.”

    “You two are sickenly adorable,” Andrea said with a smile. “I’m so happy that you’re happy Lee. And congratulations Kara.”

    “Yeah, congrats Kara,” Jack said.

    “Thank you.”

    “So, what are you guys gonna do with you leaving for a year, Lee?” Andrea asked.

    Nothing really, Lena thought. By then this fake-dating show will be history. Lena didn’t want to accept how that made her feel.

    “We’ll figure it out, right darling?”

    Kara kissed the side of Lena’s head. “Yeah, we’ll figure it out baby.”

    The L Word

    Chapter Notes

    Not proofread so I apologize for any mistakes.

    HYPOTHESIS: If I fall in love, things will invariably end poorly.

    The knockout mouse had been hanging from a wire for a length of time that should have been impossible, considering how it had been genetically modified. Lena frowned at it and pressed her lips together. It was missing crucial DNA. All the hanging-from-a-wire proteins had been erased. There was no way it could hold on for this long. It was the whole point of knocking out its stupid genes—

    Her phone lit up, and the corner of her eye darted to its screen. She was able to read the name of the sender (Kara) but not the content of the message. It was 8:42 on Wednesday, which immediately had her worried that Kara might want to cancel their fake date. Maybe Kara thought because they had that double date with Andrea and Jack yesterday, they didn’t need to meet today. Kara had seemed to be enjoying herself, but maybe she hadn’t been.

    Lena glanced at her gloved hands, and then back at her mouse, who was still holding on to the wire. “Dude, stop trying so hard.” She kneeled until she was at eye level with the cage. The mouse kicked around with its little legs, its tail flopping back and forth. “You’re supposed to be bad at this. And I’m supposed to write a dissertation about how bad you are. And then you get a chunk of cheese, and I get a real job that pays real money and the joy of saying ‘I’m not that kind of doctor’ when someone is having a stroke on my airplane.” The mouse squeaked and let go of the wire, flopping on the floor of the testing cage with a thud. “That’ll do it.” She quickly got rid of her gloves and unlocked her phone with her thumb.

    Kara: *image*

    Lena opened the picture Kara had sent and instantly started laughing. It was a picture of Kara’s desk nameplate with a blue sticky note over it and ‘Dr. Antagonistic’ written on it.

    Lena: Very fitting😂🤣🤣🤣😂😂🤣😂

    Kara: How you got into my lab I do not know, but seriously baby is this what you do in your free time?

    Lena giggled. She truly had not thought she was the type to, but here she was, covering her mouth while giggling like a fool in the middle of the lab. Her mouse was staring up at her, its tiny red eyes a mix of judgment and surprise. Lena hastily turned away and looked back at her phone.

    Lena: I do not know what you are talking about.

    That’s a lie. Earlier when she saw the door to Kara's lab open she went in to say hello but it was just a janitor inside so Lena decided to get up to a little mischief. She can’t wait till Kara opens her daily planner and see her name written all over it.

    Kara: Really🤨
    Kara: I didn’t take you for a liar baby.

    Lena: I’ve been lying since we met😜.

    Kara: Okay, fair point.

    The next message came in two minutes later and in all caps.

    Kara: MY PLANNER LENA!!!!

    Lena’s cheeks pulled with how hard she was grinning. She was about to reply with a kiss emoji when a voice interrupted her.

    “Gross.”

    She looked up from her phone. Andrea stood in the lab’s entrance, sticking out her tongue.

    “Hey. What are you doing here?”

    “Borrowing gloves. And being grossed out.”

    Lena frowned. “Why?”

    “We’re out of the small size.” Andrea stepped inside, rolling her eyes. “Honestly, they never buy enough because I’m the only woman in the lab, but it’s not like I don’t go through gloves as fast as—”

    “No, why are you grossed out?”

    Andrea made a face and plucked two purple gloves from Lena’s stash.

    “Because of how in love you are with Zor-El. Is it okay if I take a few pairs?”

    “What are you—” Lena blinked at her, still clutching her phone. Was Andrea going crazy? “I’m not in love with her.”

    “Uh-huh, sure.” Andrea finished stuffing her pockets with gloves and then looked up, finally noticing Lena’s distressed expression. Her eyes widened. “Hey, I was kidding! You’re not gross. I probably look the same when I’m texting Jack. And it’s actually very sweet, how gone you are for her—”

    “But I’m not. Gone.” Lena was starting to panic. “I don’t —It’s just—”

    Andrea pressed her lips together, as if biting back a smile. “Okay. If you say so.”

    “No, I’m serious. We’re just—”

    “Dude, it’s okay.” Andrea’s tone was reassuring and a little emotional. “It’s just, you’re so amazing. And special. And honestly, my favorite person in the whole world. But sometimes I get worried that no one but Sam and me will ever get to experience how incredible you are. Well, until now. Now I’m not worried anymore, because I’ve seen you and Kara together, at the picnic. And in the parking lot. And yesterday. And . . . every other time, really. You’re both crazy in love, and over the moon about it. It’s cute! Except that first night,” she added, pensive. “I maintain that was pretty awkward.”

    Lena stiffened. “Andy, it’s not like that. We’re just . . . dating. Casually. Hanging out. Getting to know each other. We’re not . . .”

    “Okay, sure. If you say so.” Andrea shrugged, clearly not believing a word of what Lena was saying. “Hey, I gotta go back to my bacterial culture. I’ll come bug you when I’m on break, okay?”

    Lena nodded slowly, watching her friend’s back as she headed for the door. Lena’s heart skipped a beat when Andrea paused and turned around, her expression suddenly serious. “Lee, I just want you to know that . . . I was very worried about you getting hurt from my dating Jack, but now I’m not anymore. Because I know what you really look like when you . . . Well.” Andrea gave her a sheepish grin. “I won’t say it, if you don’t want me to.”

    She left with a wave of her hand, and Lena stood frozen, watching the doorframe long past the moment Andrea had disappeared. Then she lowered her gaze to the floor, slumped on the stool behind her, and thought one single thing: Shit. __ It wasn't the end of the world. These things happened. Even the best of people developed crushes—Andy had said love, oh God, she had said love—on the person they were fake-dating. It didn’t mean anything.

    Except that: Fuck. Fuck, fuck, fuck.

    Lena locked the door of her office behind her and plopped herself into a chair, hoping today wouldn’t be the one time in the semester that her office mates decided to show up before 10:00 a.m. It was all her fault. Her stupid doing. She had known, she had known, that she’d begun to find Kara attractive. She had known almost from the very beginning, and then she’d started talking with Kara, she’d started getting to know her even though it was never part of the plan, and—damn Kara to hell for being so different from what she’d expected. For making her want to be with her more and more. Damn Kara. It had been there, staring at Lena for the past few days, and she hadn’t noticed. Because she was an idiot.

    She stood abruptly and dug into her pocket for her phone, pulling up Sam’s contact.

    Lena: We have to meet.

    Bless Sam, because it took her fewer than five seconds to answer.

    Sammy: Lunch? I’m about to dig into the neuromuscular junction of a juvenile rat.

    Lena: I need to talk to you NOW.
    Lena: Please.

    Sammy: Starbucks. In 10.

    “I told you so.”

    Lena didn’t bother lifting her forehead from the table. “You didn’t.”

    “Well, maybe I didn’t say, ‘Hey, don’t do this fake-dating shit because you’re going to fall for Zor-El,’ but I did say that the whole idea was idiotic and a car wreck waiting to happen —which I believe encompasses the current situation.”

    Sam was sitting across from her, by the window of the crowded coffee shop. Around them students chatted, laughed, ordered drinks—rudely unaware of the sudden maelstrom in Lena’s life. She pushed up from the cold surface of the table and pressed her palms into her eyes, not quite ready to open them yet. She might never be ready again.

    “How could this happen? I am not like this. This is not me. How could I—and Kara Zor-El, of everyone. Who is into Kara Zor-El?”

    Sam snorted. “Everyone, Lee. She’s a tall, broody, sullen hunk with a genius IQ. Everyone likes tall, broody, sullen hunks with genius IQs. She’s out of this world. Literally.”

    “Clearly you do.”

    “Oh, she is. Just, you don’t notice, because you’re halfway gone for her.”

    “I am not—” She smacked her forehead. Repeatedly. “Shit.”

    Sam leaned forward and grabbed Lena’s hand. “Hey,” she told her, voice pitched to a comforting tone. “Settle down. We’ll figure it out.” Sam even tacked on a smile. Lena loved her so much in that moment, even with all the I told you sos. “First of all, how bad is it?”

    “I don’t know. Is there a scale?”

    “Well, there is liking, and there is liking.”

    Lena shook her head, feeling utterly lost. “I just like her. I want to spend time with her.”

    “Okay, that doesn’t mean anything. You also want to spend time with me.”

    Lena grimaced, feeling herself blush scarlet. “Not quite like that.”

    Sam was quiet for a beat. “I see.” She knew how big of a deal this was for Lena. They’d talked about it multiple times —how rare it was for her to experience attraction, especially sexual attraction. If there was something wrong with her. If her past had stunted her in some way.

Anything but deal with this. “It was there, and I didn’t figure it out. I just thought she was smart and attractive and that she had a nice smile and that we could be friends and—” She rubbed her palms into her eye sockets, wishing she could go back and erase her life choices. The entire past month. “Do you hate me?”

    “Me?” Sam sounded surprised.

    “Yes.”

    “No. Why would I hate you?”

    “Because she’s been horrible to you, made you throw out a ton of data. It’s just—with me she’s not—”

    “I know. Well,” Sam amended, waving her hand, “I don’t know know. But I can believe she’s different with you than when she was in my damn graduate advisory committee.”
