But, Lee, I saw you guys at the picnic. She definitely wasn’t interacting with you like she does with me. Plus, you know,” Sam added begrudgingly, “she’s not not hot. I can see why you’d hit that.”

    “No, but I’m trying to be supportive here. You weren’t in love with her at the time.”

    “Sure.” Sam brushed nonexistent dust off her t-shirt. “Way to bring a rom-com to life, by the way. So, how are you going to break the news?”

    “No.”

    “Whatever.” Sam rolled her eyes. “You’re going to tell her, right?”

    “Of course not.” Lena snorted out a laugh. “You can’t tell the person you’re fake-dating that you”—her brain scanned itself for the correct word, didn’t find it, and then stumbled on —“like them. It’s just not done. Kara will think I orchestrated this. That I was after her all along.”

    “That’s ridiculous. You didn’t even know her at the time.”

    “Maybe I did, though. Do you remember the girl I told you about, who helped me decide about grad school? The one I met in the bathroom over my interview weekend?”

    “She might have been Kara. I think.”

    “You think? You mean you didn’t ask her?”

    “Of course not.”

    “Why ‘of course’?”

    “Because maybe it wasn’t her. And if she was, she clearly doesn’t remember, or she’d have mentioned it weeks ago.” Kara hadn’t been the one wearing expired contacts, after all.

    Lena laughed. “There is no way.”

    “Why not?”

    “Because.”

    “Because what?”

    “Because she’s her. She’s Kara Zor-El, and I . . .” She trailed off. No need to continue. And I’m me. I am nothing special.

You’re independent, and a genius scientist, and selfless, and loyal—hell, Lee, look at this ridiculous mess you created just so your friend could date the guy she likes without feeling guilty. There’s no way Zor-El hasn’t noticed.”

    Maybe not. Maybe all the talking, and those looks Kara gave her, and Kara shaking her head when Lena ordered extra whipped cream; the way Kara let herself be teased out of her moods; the texts; Kara calling her ‘baby’ even when no one is around for them to put on a show; how she seemed to be so at ease with Lena, so noticeably different from the Kara Zor-El Lena used to be half-scared of—maybe all of that was not much. But she and Kara were friends now, and they could remain friends even past August twelfth.

    Lena’s heart sank at the thought of giving up the possibility of it.

    “I do, though.”

    Sam sighed, once again enveloping Lena’s hand with hers. “You have it bad, then.”

    Lena pressed her lips together, blinking rapidly to push back the tears. “Maybe I do. I don’t know—I’ve never had it before. I’ve never wanted to have it.”

    Sam smiled reassuringly, even though Lena felt anything but reassured. “Listen, I know it’s scary. But this is not necessarily a bad thing.”

    One single tear was making its way down Lena’s cheek. She hastened to clean it with her sleeve.

    “It’s the worst.”

    “You’ve finally found someone you’re into. And okay, it’s Zor-El, but this could still turn out to be great.”

    “It couldn’t. It can’t.”

    “Lee, I know where you’re coming from. I get it.” Sam’s hand tightened on hers. “I know it’s scary, being vulnerable, but you can allow yourself to care. You can want to be with people as more than just friends or casual acquaintances.”

    “But I can’t.”

    “I don’t see why not.”

    “Because all the people I’ve cared about are gone,” she snapped. Somewhere in the coffee shop, the barista called for a caramel macchiato. Lena immediately regretted her harsh words. “I’m sorry. It’s just . . . it’s the way it works. My mom. My grandparents. My father—one way or another, everyone is gone. If I let myself care, Kara will go, too.” There. She’d put it into words, said it out loud, and it sounded all the truer because of it.

    Sam exhaled. “Oh, Lee.”

    Sam was one of the few people to whom Lena had opened up about her fears—the constant feeling of not belonging, the never-ending suspicions that since so much of her life had been spent alone, then it would end the same way. That she’d never be worthy of someone caring for her. Sam’s knowing expression, a combination of sorrow and understanding and pity, was unbearable to watch.

    Lena looked elsewhere—at the laughing students, at the coffee cup lids stacked next to the counter, at the stickers on a girl’s MacBook—and slid her hand away from under Sam’s palm.

    “You should go.” She attempted a smile, but it felt wobbly. “Finish your surgeries.”

    Sam didn’t break eye contact. “I care. Andy cares—Andy would have chosen you over Jack. And you care, too. We all care about one another, and I’m still here. I’m not going anywhere.”

    “It’s different.”

    “How?”

    Lena didn’t bother answering and used her sleeve to dry her cheek. Kara was different, and what Lena wanted from her was different, but she couldn’t—didn’t want to articulate it. Not now.

    “Lee.”

    “No,” she said, firm. With her tears gone, she felt marginally better. Maybe she was not who she had thought, but she could fake it. She could pretend, even to herself. “It’s a terrible idea. I-"

    “Lee.”

    “How would that conversation even work? How would I phrase it? What are the right words?”

    “Actually you should probably—”

    “Do I just say ‘Hey, I’m into you’? That all I think about is-“

    “Lena.”

    “That I have a huge crush-”

    “Lena.”

    In the end, what tipped her off was not Sam’s words, or Sam’s panicky expression, or the fact that sam was clearly looking at a spot somewhere above her shoulders. In the end, Andrea chose that exact moment to text her, which drew Lena’s eyes to the numbers on the screen. 10:00 a.m. It was ten. On a Wednesday morning. And Lena was currently sitting in the campus Starbucks, the very same Starbucks where she had spent her Wednesday mornings for the past few weeks. She whirled around and— She wasn’t even surprised to find Kara. Standing behind her. Close enough that unless both her eardrums had ruptured since the last time they’d talked, she must have heard every single word that came out of Lena’s mouth.

    She wished she could expire on the spot. She wished she could crawl outside her body and this café, melt in a pool of sweat, and seep between the tiles on the floor, just vanish into thin air. But all these things were currently beyond her skill set, so she fixed a weak smile on her face and looked up at Kara.

    The Lie

    HYPOTHESIS: Whenever I lie, things will get worse by a factor of 630.

    “Did you . . . did you hear that?” Lena blurted out.

    Sam hurried to clear the table of her stuff, muttering tightly, “I was just about to go.”

    Lena barely noticed, busy watching Kara slide the chair back to sit across from her. Shit.

    “Yes,” Kara said, bland and even, and Lena felt like she was about to disintegrate into a million tiny pieces, here, in this exact spot.

    She wanted Kara to take it back. Wanted her to say “No, heard what?” She wanted to go back to earlier this morning and rewind it all, this horrible mess of a day. Not look at the texts on her phone, not let Andrea walk in on her mooning over her fake girlfriend, not pour her heart out to Sam in the worst possible place. Kara couldn’t know. She simply couldn’t. She’d think that Lena had kissed her on purpose, that she’d masterminded this whole fiasco, that she’d manipulated her into this situation. Kara would feel compelled to break up with her well before she could reap any benefits from their arrangement. And Kara would hate her. The prospect was terrifying, so she said the one thing she could think of.

    “It wasn’t about you.”

    The lie rolled off her tongue like a mudslide: unpremeditated, quick, and bound to leave a huge mess behind.

I used to think the worst of you, and now . . . “It’s supposed to help both of us. If it stops doing so . . .”

    “Really? Well, then.” Her smile broadened and she pulled up a chair from a nearby table. Kara closed her eyes, resigned. “So, what are we talking about?”

It was obvious that Kara and Mike were very close, too, but the relationship Kara had with Alex was something else, and . . . and Lena had no idea what to do with this piece of information. Maybe she should tell Sam. She’d either have a field day or go completely berserk.

    “Well,” Alex said, standing up. “This was fantastic. I’ll go get coffee, but we should hang out soon, the three of us. I can’t remember the last time I had the pleasure of embarrassing Kara in front of a girlfriend. For now, though, she’s all yours.” She followed the word ‘yours’ with a smirk that had Lena blushing. Kara rolled her eyes when Alex left for the coffee counter.

    Fascinated, Lena followed Alex with her gaze for several moments. “Um, that was . . . ?”

    “Alex for you.” Kara seemed barely annoyed.

    Lena nodded, still a little dazed. “I can’t believe I’m not your first.”

    “My first?”

    “Your first fake date.”

    “Right. I guess prom qualifies.” Kara seemed to mull it over. “Alex has had some . . . bad luck with relationships. Undeserved bad luck.”

    It warmed her chest, the protective concern in Kara’s tone. Made her wonder if Kara was even aware of it.

    “Why doesn’t she want to drive Mike to the airport?” It seemed odd that Alex wasn’t fond of Mike.

    Kara shrugged. “Alex has always had a very deep, very irrational dislike of Mike, ever since grad school.”

    “Oh. Why?”

    “Not sure. Not sure Alex knows, either. Mike says she’s jealous. I think it’s just a personality thing.”

    Lena fell silent, absorbing the information. “You didn’t tell Alex about us, either. That it’s not real.”

    “No.”

    “Why?”

    Kara looked away. “I don’t know.” Her jaw tensed. “I think I just didn’t . . .” Her voice trailed off, and she shook her head before giving Lena a smile, small and a little forced. “She speaks very highly of you, you know?”

    “Alex? Of me?”

    “Of your work. And your research.”

    “Oh.” She had no idea what to say to that. When did you talk about me? And why? “Oh,” she repeated uselessly.

    She wasn’t sure why now, in this very moment, but the possible ramifications of their arrangement on Kara’s life hit her in full for the first time. They had agreed to fake-date because they both had something to gain from it, but it occurred to her that Kara also had significantly more to lose. Out of all the people she loved, Lena was only lying to one, Andrea, and that was absolutely unavoidable. She could not care less about other students’ opinions. Kara, though . . . she was lying on a daily basis to her colleagues and her friends. Her grads interacted with her every day believing that she was dating one of their peers. Did they think Kara lecherous? Had her relationship with Lena changed their perception of her? And what about other faculty members in the department, or in adjacent programs? Just because dating a grad student was allowed, it didn’t mean that it wasn’t frowned upon. And what if Kara met—or had already met—someone she actually liked? When they’d struck their deal, Kara had said she wasn’t going to date, but that had been weeks ago. Lena herself had been convinced that she’d never be interested in dating anyone at the time—and didn’t that make her want to laugh now, in a remarkably unfunny way? Not to mention that she alone was benefitting from their arrangement. Andrea and Jack had bought her lie, but Kara’s research funds were still frozen. And yet, she was still helping her despite all of this. And Lena was repaying her kindness by getting ideas and developing feelings that were sure to make Kara feel uncomfortable.

    “Do you want to get coffee?”

    Lena looked up from her hands. “No.” She cleared her throat against the burning sensation lodged behind her sternum. The idea of coffee made her nauseous. “I think I need to go back to the lab.” She bent down to retrieve her backpack, meaning to stand and leave immediately, but halfway through, a thought swept over her, and she found herself staring at Kara. Kara was sitting across from her with a concerned expression, a slight frown creasing her brow. Lena attempted a smile. “We are friends, right?”

    Kara’s frown deepened. “Friends?”

    “Yes. You and I.”

    Kara studied her for a long moment. Something new passed through Kara’s face, stark and a little sad. Too fleeting to interpret. “Yes, Lena.”

    Lena nodded, unsure as to whether she should be feeling relieved. This was not how she’d thought today would go, and there was a strange pressure behind her eyelids, which had her sliding her arms through the straps of her backpack that much quicker. She waved Kara goodbye with a tremulous smile, and she’d have already been out of this damn Starbucks, if Kara hadn’t said with that voice of hers:

    “Lena.” Lena paused right in front of Kara’s chair and looked down at her. It was so odd, to be the taller one for once. “This might be inappropriate, but . . .” Kara’s jaw shifted, and she closed her eyes for a second. As if to collect her thoughts. “Lena. You are really . . . You are extraordinary, and I cannot imagine that if you told Jack how you feel he wouldn’t . . .” Kara trailed off and then nodded. A punctuation of sorts, as her words and the way she’d said them brought Lena that much closer to tears.

    Kara thought it was Jack. Kara thought Lena had been in love with Jack when they’d begun their arrangement—she thought Lena was still in love with him. Because Lena had just told a half-assed lie that she was too afraid to take back and— It was going to happen. She was going to cry, and what she wanted most in the world was to not do it in front of Kara.

    “I’ll see you next week, okay?”

    Lena didn’t wait for Kara’s response and walked briskly toward the exit, her shoulder bumping into someone she should have apologized to. Once she was outside, she took a deep breath and marched to the biology building, trying to empty her mind, forcing herself to think about the section she was slated to TA later today, the fellowship application she’d promised Dr. Daxam she’d send by tomorrow, the fact that Andrea’s father would be in town next weekend and had made plans to cook Argentinian food for everyone. A chilly wind weaved through the leaves of the campus trees, pushing Lena’s sweater against her body. She hugged herself and didn’t look back to the café.

    The Selection

    Chapter Notes

    HYPOTHESIS: If I am bad at doing activity A, my chances of being asked to engage in activity A will rise exponentially.

    Campus felt strangely empty with Kara gone, even on days in which Lena likely wouldn’t have met her anyway. It didn’t make much sense: Stanford was most definitely not empty, but teeming with loud, annoying undergrads on their way to and from class.

    Lena’s life, too, was full: her mice were old enough for the behavioural assays to be run, she’d finally gotten revisions for a paper she’d submitted months earlier, and she had to start making concrete plans for her move to Boston next year; the class she was TA’ing had a test coming up, and undergrads magically began to pop by during office hours, looking panicky and asking questions that were invariably answered in the first three lines of the syllabus. Sam spent a couple of days trying to convince Lena to tell Kara the truth, and then became—thankfully—too discouraged by Lena’s stubbornness and too busy trying to meditate away her own dating drama to insist. Sam did bake several batches of butterscotch cookies, though, patently lying that she was ‘not rewarding your self-destructive behaviours, Lee, but just perfecting my recipe’. Lena ate them all, and hugged her from behind while Sam sprinkled sea salt on top of the last batch.

    On Saturday, Andrea came over for beer and s’mores, and she and Lena daydreamed about leaving academia and finding industry jobs that paid a proper salary and acknowledged the existence of free time.

    “We could, like, sleep in on Sunday mornings. Instead of having to check on our mice at six a.m.”

    “Yeah.” Andrea sighed wistfully. Pride and Prejudice and Zombies was running in the background, but neither of them was paying attention. “We could buy real ketchup instead of stealing packets from Burger King. And order that wireless vacuum cleaner I saw on TV.”

    Lena giggled drunkenly and turned to her side, making the bed squeak. “Seriously? A vacuum cleaner?”

    “A wireless one. It’s the shit, Lee.”

    “That is . . .”

    “What?”

    “Just . . .” Lena giggled some more. “It’s the most random thing.”

    “Shut up.” Andrea smiled but didn’t open her eyes. “I have severe dust allergies. You know what, though?”

    “Are you going to hit me with a Trivial Pursuit vacuum cleaner fact?”

    The corners of Andrea’s eyes crinkled. “Nah,” she said, “I don’t have any. Wait—I think that maybe the first female corporate CEO worked for a vacuum cleaner company.”

    “No way. That is actually cool.”

    “But maybe I’m making it up.” Andrea shrugged. “Anyway, what I meant to say is . . . I think I still want it?”

    “The vacuum cleaner?” Lena yawned without bothering to cover her mouth.

    “No. An academic job. And everything that comes with it. The lab, the grad students, the outrageous teaching load, the race for the NIH grants, the disproportionately low salary. The whole shebang. Jack says that Sam has it right. That industry jobs are where it’s at. But I think I want to stay and become a professor. It’ll be miserable, for sure, but it’s the only way to create a good environment for women like us, Lee. Give some competition to all these entitled white men.” She grinned, beautiful and fierce. “Jack can go into industry and make a ton of blood money that I’ll invest in wireless vacuum cleaners.”

    Lena drunkenly studied the drunken determination on Andrea’s drunken face, thinking that there was something reassuring in knowing that her closest friend was starting to figure out what she wanted her life to be like. Who she wanted to live it with. It did send a pang deep in Lena’s stomach, in that spot that seemed to feel Kara’s absence most acutely, but she pushed it down, trying not to think about it too hard. Instead, she reached for her friend’s hand, squeezed it once, and inhaled the sweet scent of apple from her hair. “You’ll be so good at it, Andy. I can’t wait to see you change the world.”

    —
    All in all, Lena’s life continued as it always had—except that for the first time, there was something else she’d rather be doing. Someone else she’d rather be with. So, this is liking someone, she mused. Feeling like the biology building was not worth going to because if Kara was out of town, even the most remote chance of running into her had been taken away from Lena; constantly spinning around after catching a glimpse of blonde hair, or when hearing a voice that sounded like Kara’s but really wasn’t; thinking of Kara because her friend Jess mentioned she was drinking camomile tea, or when she overheard James and Winn talking about taking a trip to Boston for a week; feeling stuck in an odd limbo, waiting, just waiting, waiting . . . for nothing.

    Kara was going to come back in a few days, and Lena’s lie that she was in love with someone else was still going to be there. August twelfth would arrive all too soon, and anyway, the assumption that Kara could ever see Lena in any romantic light was preposterous. All considered, she was lucky Kara liked her enough to want to be her friend.

    On Sunday, her phone pinged while she was running at the gym. When Kara’s name popped up at the top of the screen, she immediately jumped to read it. Except that there wasn’t much to read: just the image of a huge drink in a plastic cup, topped with what looked like a muffin. The bottom of the image proudly stated, ‘Pumpkin Pie Frappuccino,’ and below that, Kara’s text:

    Kara: Think I can smuggle this on the plane?

    Lena didn’t need to be told that she was grinning at her phone like an idiot.

    Lena: Well, TSA is notoriously incompetent.
    Lena: Though maybe not that incompetent?

    Kara: Too bad.
    Kara: Wish you were here, then.

    Lena’s smile stayed in place for a long time. And then, when she remembered the mess she was in, it faded into a heavy sigh.

    —

    She was carrying a tray of tissue samples to the electron microscope lab when someone patted her on the shoulder, startling her. Lena nearly tripped and destroyed several thousand dollars’ worth of federal grant funding. When she turned, Dr. Danvers was staring at her with her usual mischievous grin—like they were best buddies about to go for a beer and a jolly good time, instead of a Ph.D. student and a former member of her advisory committee who’d never quite gotten around to reading any of the paperwork she’d turned in.

    “Dr. Danvers.”

    “I thought we’d settled on Alex?”

    Had they? “Right. Alex.”

    Alex smiled, pleased. “Girlfriend’s out of town, huh?”

    “Oh. Um . . . Yes.”

    “You going in there?” Alex pointed at the microscope lab with her chin, and Lena nodded. “Here, let me get it.” Alex swiped her badge to unlock the door and held it open for Lena.

    “Thank you.” Lena settled her samples on a bench and smiled gratefully, sliding her hands into her back pockets. “I was going to get a cart, but I couldn’t find one.”

    “There’s only one left on this floor. I think someone’s bringing them home and reselling them.” Alex grinned, and—Sam was right. Had been right for the past two years: there really was something easy-going and effortlessly attractive about Alex. Not that Lena seemed to be interested in anything but tall, blonde, sullen hunks with genius IQs. “Can’t blame ’em. I’d have done the same in my grad school days. So, how’s life?”

    “Um, fine. You?”

    Alex ignored her question and casually leaned against the wall. “How bad is it?”

    “Bad?”

    “Kara being gone. Hell, even I miss that little shit.” Alex chuckled. “How are you holding up?”

    “Oh.” Lena took her hands out of her pockets, crossed her arms in front of her chest, and then changed her mind and dropped them woodenly by her sides. Yep. Perfect. Acting natural. “Fine. Good. Busy.”

    Alex looked genuinely relieved. “Great. Have you guys been talking on the phone?”

    No. Of course not. Talking on the phone is the hardest, most stressful thing in the world, and I can’t do it with the nice lady who schedules my dental cleanings, let alone with Kara Zor-El. “Ah, mostly texting, you know?”

    “Yeah, I do know. However buttoned-up and sulky Kara is with you, please know that she’s making an effort and she’s a million times worse with everyone else. Me included.” Alex sighed and shook her head, but there was a fondness behind it. An easy affection that Lena couldn’t miss. My oldest friend, Alex had said about Kara, and clearly she hadn’t been lying. “She’s actually gotten a lot better, since you guys started dating.”

    Lena felt on the verge of a full-body cringe. Unsure of what to say, she settled for a simple, painful, awkward: “Really?”

    Alex nodded. “Yep. I’m so glad she finally scrounged up the courage to ask you out. She’d been going on and on about this ‘amazing girl’ for years, but she was concerned about being in the same department, and you know how she is . . .” Alex shrugged and waved her hand. “I’m glad she finally managed to pull her head out of her ass.”

    Lena’s brain stuttered. Her neurons went sluggish and cold, and it took her several seconds to process that Kara had been wanting to ask her out for years. She couldn’t wrap her head around it, because . . . it was not possible. It didn’t make sense. Kara didn’t even remember Lena existed before she’d Title-IXed her in the hallway a few weeks ago. The more she thought about it, the more she grew convinced that if Kara had any recollection of their bathroom meeting, she would have said as much. Kara was famously direct, after all. Alex must have been referring to someone else. And Kara must have feelings for that someone. Someone she worked with, someone who was in their department. Someone who was ‘amazing’. Lena’s mind, half frozen until a few seconds ago, began to spiral with the knowledge. Setting aside the fact that this conversation was an utter invasion of Kara’s privacy, Lena couldn’t stop herself from considering the implications of their arrangement for Kara. If the person Alex was talking about was one of Kara’s colleagues, there was no chance that she hadn’t heard about Kara and Lena dating. It was possible that she’d seen the two of them get coffee together on a Wednesday, or Lena sitting on Kara’s lap during Mike’s talk, or—God, Lena slathering Kara with sunblock at that godforsaken picnic. And that kiss. Which couldn’t be good for Kara’s prospects. Unless Kara didn’t mind, because she was sure beyond any doubt that her feelings were unrequited—and oh, wouldn’t that be funny? About as funny as a Greek tragedy.

    “Anyway.” Alex pushed away from the wall, her hand coming up to scratch her nape. “I think we should go on a double date one of these days. I’ve been taking a break from dating—too much heartache—but maybe it’s time to dip my toes in again. Hopefully I’ll get myself a girlfriend soon.”

    The weight in Lena’s stomach sank even lower. “That would be lovely.” She attempted a smile.

    “Right?” Alex grinned. “Kara would hate it with the intensity of a thousand suns.” She really would. “But I could tell you so many juicy stories about her, approximately aged fifteen to twenty-five.” Alex was delighted at the prospect. “She’d be mortified.”

    “Are they about taxidermy?”

    “Taxidermy?”

    “Nothing. Just something Mike had said about . . .” She waved her hand. “Nothing.”

    Alex’s gaze turned sharp. “Kara said you might be going to work with Mike next year. Is that true?”

    “Oh . . . yeah. That’s the plan.”

    Alex nodded, pensive. Then seemed to come to some sort of decision and added, “Watch your back while you’re around him, okay?”

    “My back?” What? Why? Did this have anything to do with what Kara had mentioned—Alex not liking Mike? “What do you mean?”

    “Kara’s back, too. Especially Kara’s back.” Alex’s expression remained intense for a moment, and then lightened up. “Anyway, Mike only met Kara in grad school but I was there in her teenage years—that’s when the good stories are from.”

    “Oh. You probably shouldn’t tell me. Since . . .” Since she’s faking a relationship with me and surely doesn’t want me in her business. Also, she’s probably in love with someone else.

    “Oh, of course. I’ll wait until she’s present. I want to see her face when I tell you everything about her Vampire diaries phase.”

    Lena blinked. “Her…?”

    Alex nodded solemnly and stepped out, closing the door behind her and leaving Lena alone in the chilly, semi dark lab.

    Lena had to take several deep breaths before she could focus on her work.

    —

    When she received the email, she initially thought it must be an error. Maybe she’d misread—she hadn’t been sleeping well, and as it turned out, having an unwanted, unreciprocated crush came with all sorts of scatter-headedness—though after a second look, then a third and a fourth, she realized that wasn’t the case. So maybe the mistake was on the SBD conference’s side. Because there was no way—absolutely no way—that they’d really meant to inform her that the abstract she’d submitted had been selected to be part of a panel.

    A panel with faculty. It was just not possible. Graduate students were rarely selected for oral presentations. Most of the time they just made posters with their findings. Talks were for scholars whose careers were already advanced—except that when Lena logged into the conference website and downloaded the program, her name was there. And out of all the speakers’ names, hers was the only one not followed by any letters. No MD. No Ph.D. No MD-Ph.D.

    Crap.

    She ran out of the lab clutching her laptop to her chest. James gave her a dirty look when she almost crashed into him in the hallway, but she ignored him and stormed inside Dr. Daxam’s office out of breath, her knees suddenly made of jelly.

    “Can we talk?” She closed the door without waiting for an answer.

    Her adviser looked up from behind her desk with an alarmed expression. “Lena, what is—”

    “I don’t want to give a talk. I can’t give a talk.” She shook her head, trying to sound reasonable but only managing panic- stricken and frantic. “I can’t.”

    Dr. Daxam cocked her head and steepled her hands. The veneer of calm her adviser projected was usually comforting, but now it made Lena want to flip the nearest piece of furniture.

    Calm down. Deep breaths. Use your mindfulness and all that stuff Sam’s always yapping her mouth about.

    “Dr. Daxam, my SBD abstract was accepted as a talk. Not as a poster, a talk. Out loud. On a panel. Standing. In front of people.” Lena’s voice had made its way to a shriek. And yet, for reasons beyond understanding, Dr. Daxam’s face split into a grin.

    “This is wonderful news!”

    Lena blinked. And then blinked again. “It’s . . . not?”

    “Nonsense.” Dr. Daxam stood and walked around her desk, running her hand up and down Lena’s arm in what she clearly intended as a congratulatory gesture. “This is fantastic. A talk will give you much more visibility than a poster. You might be able to network for a postdoctoral position. I am so, so happy for you.”

    Lena’s jaw dropped. “But . . .”

    “But?”

    “I cannot give a talk. I can’t talk.”

    “You’re talking right now, Lena.”

    “Not in front of people.”

    “I am people.”

    “You’re not many people. Dr. Daxam, I can’t talk in front of a lot of people. Not about science.”

    “Why?”

    “Because.” Because my throat will dry up and my brain will shut down and I will be so bad that someone from the audience will take out a crossbow and shoot me in the kneecap. “I’m not ready. To speak. In public.”

    “Of course you are. You’re a good public speaker.”

    “I’m not. I stammer. I blush. I meander. A lot. Especially in front of large crowds, and—”

    “Lena,” Dr. Daxam interrupted her with a stern tone. “What do I always tell you?”

    “Um . . . ‘Don’t misplace the multichannel pipette’?”

    “The other thing.”

    Lena sighed. “ ‘Carry yourself with the confidence of a mediocre white man.’ ”

    “More than that, if possible. Since there is absolutely nothing mediocre about you.”

    Lena closed her eyes and took enough deep breaths to pull back from the verge of a panic attack. When she opened them, her adviser was smiling encouragingly. “Dr. Daxam.” Lena grimaced. “I really don’t think I can do this.”

    “I know you don’t.” There was some sadness in her expression. “But you can. And we’ll work together until you feel up to the task.” This time, she put both her hands on Lena’s shoulders. Lena was still hugging her laptop to her chest, like she would a life buoy in the open sea, but the touch was oddly comforting. “Don’t worry. We have a couple of weeks to get you ready.”

    You say that. You say “we,” but I’ll be the one to speak in front of hundreds of people, and when someone asks a threeminute-long question meant to get me to admit that deep down my work is poorly structured and useless, I’ll be the one to crap her pants.

    “Right.” Lena had to force her head into an up-and-down motion and take a deep breath. She exhaled slowly. “Okay.”

    “Why don’t you put together a draft? You could practice during the next lab meeting.” Another reassuring smile, and Lena was nodding again, not feeling reassured in the least. “And if you have any questions, I’m always here. Oh, I am so disappointed that I won’t get to see your talk. You must promise to record it for me. It will be just as if I was there.”

    Except that you won’t be there, and I’ll be alone, she thought bitterly while closing the door of Dr. Daxam’s office behind her. She slumped against the wall and squeezed her eyes shut, trying to quiet the agitated mess of thoughts fluttering inside her head. And then she opened them again when she heard her name in Sam’s voice.

    Sam was standing in front of her with Andrea, studying her with a half-amused, half-worried expression. They were holding Starbucks cups. The smell of caramel and peppermint wafted over, making her stomach churn.

    “Hey.” Andrea took a sip of her drink. “Why are you taking a standing nap next to your adviser’s office?”

    “I . . .” Lena pushed away from the wall and walked a few steps away from Dr. Daxam’s door, rubbing her nose with the back of her hand. “My abstract got accepted. The SBD one.”

    “Congrats!” Andrea smiled. “But that was pretty much a given, right?”

    “It was accepted as a talk.”

    For a few seconds, two pairs of eyes just stared at her in silence. Lena thought that Sam might be wincing, but when she turned to check, there was just a vague smile pasted on her face.

    “That’s . . . awesome?”

    “Yeah.” Andrea’s eyes darted to Sam and back to Lena. “That’s, um, great.”

    “It’s a disaster of epic proportions.” Andrea and Sam exchanged a worried glance. They knew very well how Lena felt about public speaking. “What is Dr. Daxam saying about it?”

    “The usual.” She rubbed her eyes. “That it will be fine. That we’ll work on it together.”

    “I think she’s right,” Andrea said. “I’ll help you practice. We’ll make sure you know it by heart. And it will be fine.”

    “Yeah.” Or it won’t. “Also, the conference is in less than two weeks. We should book the hotel—or are we doing Airbnb?”

    Something odd happened the moment she asked the question. Not with Andrea—she was still peacefully sipping on her coffee—but Sam’s cup froze halfway to her mouth, and she bit her lip while studying the sleeve of her sweater.

    “About that . . . ,” Sam began.

    Lena frowned. “What?”

    “Well.” Sam shuffled her feet a little, and maybe it was accidental, the way she seemed to be drifting away from Lena —but she didn’t think so. “We already have.”

    “You already booked something?”

    Andrea nodded cheerfully. “Yes.” She didn’t appear to notice that Sam was about to have a stroke. “The conference hotel.”

    “Oh. Okay. Let me know what I owe you then, since—”

    “The thing is . . .” Sam seemed to move even farther away.

    “What thing?”

    “Well.” Sam fidgeted with the cardboard holder of her cup, and her eyes darted to Andrea, who seemed blissfully oblivious to her discomfort. “Jack’s hotel room is paid for because of that fellowship he’s on, and he asked Andy to stay with him. And then Jess, Winn, and Hannah offered for me to stay with them.”

    “What?” Lena glanced at Andrea. “Seriously?”

    “It will save all of us a lot of money. And it will be my first trip with Jack,” Andrea interjected distractedly. She was typing something on her phone. “Oh my God, guys, I think I found it! A location for the Boston event for BIPOC women in STEM! I think I’ve got it!”

    “That’s great,” Lena said weakly. “But I thought . . . I thought we’d room together.”

    Andrea glanced up, looking contrite. “Yeah, I know. That’s what I told Jack, but he pointed out that you . . . you know.” Lena tilted her head, confused, and Andrea continued, “I mean, why would you want to spend money on a room when you could stay with Zor-El?”

    Oh. “Because.” Because. Because, because, because. “I . . .”

    “I’ll miss you, but it’s not as if we’ll be in the rooms for anything other than sleeping.”

    “Right...” She pressed her lips together, and added, “Sure.”

    Andrea’s grin made her want to groan. “Awesome. We’ll get meals together and hang out for poster sessions. And at night, of course.”

    “Of course.” It was all Lena could do not to sound bitter. “I look forward to it,” she added with as good a smile as she could muster.

    “Okay. Great. I gotta go—the Women in Science outreach committee is meeting in five. But let’s get together this weekend to plan fun activities for Boston. Jack said something about a ghost tour!”

    Lena waited until Andrea was out of earshot before turning to face Sam.

    Sam was already raising her hands defensively. “First of all, Andy came up with this plan while I was monitoring that twenty-four-hour experiment—worst day of my life, I cannot graduate soon enough. And after that—what was I supposed to do? Inform her that you’re not going to stay with Zor-El because you’re fake-dating? Oh, but wait—now that you’ve got a huge crush on her maybe it’s sort of real—”

    “Okay, I get it.” Her stomach was starting to ache. “You still could have told me.”

    “I was going to. And then I dumped Neuro Judal and she went crazy and egged my car. And after that my dad called me to say hi and asked me about how my projects are going, which devolved into him grilling me on why I’m not using a C. elegans model, and, Lee, you know how incredibly nosy and micromanaging he can be, which led to us having an argument and my mom got involved and—” Sam stopped and took a deep breath. “Well, you were there. You heard the screams. Bottom line is, it totally slipped my mind, and I’m so sorry.”

    “It’s fine.” Lena scratched her temple. “I’m going to have to find someplace to stay.”

    “I’ll help you,” Sam told her eagerly. “We can look online tonight.”

    “Thanks, but don’t worry about it. I’ll manage.” Or not. Probably. Likely. Since the conference was in less than two weeks, and everything was likely already booked up. What was left was undoubtedly so out of her price range, she’d have to sell a kidney to be able to afford it. Which could be an option—she did have two.

    “You’re not mad, right?”

    “I . . .” Yes. No. Maybe a little. “No. It’s not your fault.”

    She hugged Sam back when she leaned into her, reassuring Sam with a few awkward pats on the shoulder. As much as she’d have liked to blame her for this, she only had to look at herself. The crux of her problems—most of them, at least— was her moronic, hare-brained decision to lie to Andrea in the first place. To begin this fake-dating sham. Now she was giving a talk at this stupid conference, probably after sleeping at a bus station and eating moss for breakfast, and despite all of this she couldn’t stop thinking about Kara. Just perfect.

    Laptop under her arm, Lena headed back to the lab, the prospect of getting her slides in order for her talk simultaneously daunting and depressing. There was something leaden and unpleasant weighing on her stomach, and on impulse she made a detour to the restroom and entered the stall farthest from the door, leaning against the wall until the back of her head hit the cold tile surface. When the weight in her belly began to feel too heavy, her knees gave out on her and her back slid down until she sat on the floor. Lena stayed like that for a long time, trying to pretend that this wasn’t her life.

    Chapter End Notes

    I'm on mid-semester break so I will probably update chapters like every second or third day instead of once a week.

    The Room Situation

    Chapter Notes

    Not proof, please excuse any mistakes💗

    HYPOTHESIS: Approximately two out of three fake-dating situations will eventually involve room-sharing; 50 percent of room-sharing situations will be further complicated by the presence of only one bed.

    There was an Airbnb twenty-five minutes from the conference centre, but it was an inflatable mattress on the floor of a storage room, charging 180 bucks per night, and even if she could have afforded it, one of the reviews reported that the host had a penchant for role-playing Viking with the guests, so . . . No, thank you.

    She found a more affordable one forty-five minutes away by subway, but when she went to reserve the room, she discovered that someone had beaten her to it by mere seconds, and she was tempted to hurl her laptop across the coffee shop. She was trying to decide between a seedy motel and a cheap couch in the suburbs when a shadow cast over her. She looked up with a frown, expecting an undergrad wanting to use the outlet she’d been hoarding, and instead found . . .

    “Oh.” Kara was standing in front of her, the late-afternoon sunlight haloing her hair and shoulders, fingers closed around an iPad as she looked down at her with a somber expression. It had been less than a week since she’d last seen Kara—six days to be precise, which was just a handful of hours and minutes. Nothing, considering that she’d barely known her a month. And yet it was as if the space she was in, the whole campus, the entire city was transformed by knowing that Kara was back. Possibilities. That’s what Kara’s presence felt like. Of what, she was not certain. “You’re . . .” Her mouth was dry. An event of great scientific interest, considering that she’d taken a sip from her water bottle maybe ten seconds ago. “You’re back.”

    “I am.”

    She hadn’t forgotten Kara’s voice. Or her height. Or the way her stupid clothes fit her. She couldn’t have—she had two medial temporal lobes, fully functioning and tucked nicely inside her skull, which meant that she was perfectly able to encode and store memories. She hadn’t forgotten anything, and she wasn’t sure why right now it felt as if she had.

    “I thought . . . I didn’t—” Yes, Lena. Wonderful. Very eloquent. “I didn’t know that you were back.”

    Kara’s face was a little closed off, but she nodded. “I flew in last night.”

    “Oh.” She should have probably prepared something to say, but she hadn’t expected to see Kara until Wednesday. If she had, maybe she wouldn’t have been wearing her oldest leggings and most tattered T-shirt, and her hair wouldn’t have been a mess. Not that she was under any illusion that Kara would have noticed her if she’d been wearing a swimsuit or a gala dress. But still. “Do you want to sit?” She leaned forward to gather her phone and notebook, making room on the other side of the small table. It was only when Kara hesitated before taking a seat that it occurred to her that maybe Kara had no intention of staying, that now she might feel forced to do so. Kara folded herself into the chair gracefully, like a big cat. Great job, Lena. Who doesn’t love a needy person who hounds them for attention? “You don’t have to. I know you’re busy. MacArthur grants to win and grads to brutalize and broccoli to eat.”

    Kara would probably rather be anywhere else. Lena bit her thumbnail, feeling guilty, starting to panic, and— And then Kara smiled. And suddenly there were grooves around her mouth and dimples in her cheeks and her face was completely altered by them. The air at the table thinned. Lena couldn’t quite breathe.

    “You know, there’s a middle ground between living off brownies and exclusively eating broccoli.”

    She grinned, for no reason other than—Kara was here, with her. And she was smiling.

    “That’s a lie.”

    Kara shook her head, mouth still curved. “How are you?”

    Better now. “Good. How was Boston?”

    “Good.”

    “I’m glad you’re back. I’m pretty sure the biology dropout rates have seen a steep reduction. We can’t have that.”

    Kara gave her a patient, put-upon look. “You look tired, smart-ass.”

    Oh. “Yeah, I . . .” She rubbed her cheek with her hand, ordering herself not to feel self-conscious about her looks, just like she’d always made a point not to. It would be an equally stupid idea to wonder what the woman Alex mentioned the other day looked like. Probably stunning. “I’m fine. It’s been a week, though.” She massaged her temple.

    Kara cocked her head. “What happened?”

    “Nothing . . . My friends are stupid, and I hate them.” She felt instantly guilty and made a face. “Actually, I don’t hate them. I do hate that I love them, though.”

    “Is this the sunscreen friend? Andrea?”

    “The one and only. And my roommate, too, who really should know better.”

    “What did they do?”

    “They . . .” Lena pressed into both eyes with her fingers. “It’s a long story. They found alternative accommodations for SBD. Which means that now I have to find a place on my own.”

    “Why did they do that?”

    “Because . . .” She briefly closed her eyes and sighed. “Because they assumed that I’d want to stay with you. Since you’re my . . . you know. ‘Girlfriend.’ ”

    Kara went still for a couple of seconds. And then: “I see.”

    “Yep. A pretty bold assumption, but . . .” She spread her arms and shrugged.

    Kara bit the inside of her cheek, looking pensive. “I’m sorry you won’t get to room with them.”

    She waved her hand. “Oh, that’s not it. That would have been fun, but it’s just that now I need to find something else nearby, and there are no affordable options.” Her eyes fell on the screen of her laptop. “I’m thinking of booking this motel that’s an hour away and—”

    “Won’t they know?”

    She looked up from the grainy, shady-looking picture of the place. “Mm?”

    “Won’t Andrea know that you’re not staying with me?”

    Oh. “Where are you staying?”

    “The conference hotel.”

    Of course. “Well.” She scratched her nose. “I wouldn’t tell her. I don’t think she’ll pay too much attention.”

    “But she’ll notice if you’re staying one hour away.”

    “I . . .” Yes. They would notice, and ask questions, and Lena would have to come up with a bunch of excuses and even more half-truths to deal with it. Add a few blocks to this Jenga tower of lies she’d been building for weeks. “I’ll figure it out.”

    Kara nodded slowly. “I’m sorry.”

    “Oh, it’s not your fault.”

    “One could argue that it is, in fact, my fault.”

    “Not at all.”

    “I would offer to pay for your hotel room, but I doubt there’s anything left in a ten-mile radius.”

    “Oh, no.” She shook her head emphatically. “And I wouldn’t accept it. It’s not a cup of coffee. And a scone. And a cookie. And a pumpkin Frappuccino.” She batted her eyes at Kara and leaned forward, trying to change the topic. “Which, by the way, is new on the menu. You could totally buy it for me, and that would make my day.”

    “Sure.” Kara looked slightly nauseous.

    “Awesome.” She grinned. “I think it’s cheaper today, some kind of Tuesday sale, so—”

    “But you could room with me.”

    The way Kara put it forward, calm and sensible, almost made it sound like it was no big deal. And Lena almost fell for it, until her ears and brain seemed to finally connect with each other and she was able to process the meaning of what Kara had just said. That she. Could room. With her. Lena knew full well what sharing quarters with someone entailed, even for a very short period. Sleeping in the same room meant seeing embarrassing pajamas, taking turns to use the bathroom, hearing the swish of someone trying to find a comfortable position under the sheets loud and clear in the dark. Sleeping in the same room meant— No. Nope. It was a terrible idea. And Lena was starting to think that maybe she had maxed those out for a while. So she cleared her throat.

    “I could not, actually.”

    Kara nodded calmly. But then, then she asked equally calmly, “Why?” and Lena wanted to bang her head against the table.

    “I couldn’t.”

    “The room is a double, of course,” Kara offered, as if that piece of information could have possibly changed her mind.

    “It’s not a good idea.”

    “Why?”

    “Because people will think that we . . .” She noticed Kara’s look and immediately hushed. “Okay, fine. They already think that. But.”

    “But?”

    “Kara.” She rubbed her forehead with her fingers. “There will be only one bed.”

    Kara frowned. “No, as I said it’s a double—”

    “It’s not. It won’t be. There will be only one bed, for sure.”

    Kara gave her a puzzled look. “I got the booking confirmation the other day. I can forward it to you if you want; it says that—”

    “It doesn’t matter what it says. It’s always one bed.” Kara stared at her, perplexed, and Lena sighed and leaned helplessly against the back of her chair. Kara has clearly never seen a rom-com or read a romance novel in her life. “Nothing. Ignore me.”

    “My symposium is part of a satellite workshop the day before the conference starts, and then I’ll be speaking on the first day of the actual conference. I have the room for the entire conference, but I’ll probably need to leave for some meetings after night two, so you’d be by yourself from night three. We’d only overlap for one night.”

    She listened to the logical, methodical way Kara listed sensible reasons why she should just accept her offer and felt a wave of panic sweep over her.

    “It seems like a bad idea.”

    “That’s fine. I just don’t understand why.”

    “Because.” Because I don’t want to. Because I have it bad. Because I’d probably have it even worse, after that. Because it’s going to be the week of August twelfth, and I’ve been trying hard not to think about it.

    “Are you afraid that I’ll try to kiss you without your consent? To sit on your lap, or fondle you under the pretext of applying sunscreen? Because I would never—”

    Lena chucked her phone at Kara. Kara caught it in her left hand, studied its glitter amino-acid case with a pleased expression, and then carefully set it next to her laptop.

    “I hate you,” She told Kara, sullen. She might have been pouting. And smiling at the same time.

    Kara’s mouth twitched. “I know.”

    “Am I ever going to live that stuff down?”

    “Unlikely. And if you do, I’m sure something else will come up.” Lena huffed, crossing her arms over her chest, and they exchanged a small smile. “I can ask Alex or Mike if I can stay with them, and leave you my room,” Kara suggested. “But they know that I already have one, so I’d have to come up with excuses—”

    “No, I’m not going to kick you out of your room.” She ran a hand through her hair and exhaled. “You’d hate it.”

    Kara tilted her head. “What?”

    “Rooming with me.”

    “I would?”

    “Yeah. You seem like a person who . . .” You seem like you like to keep others at arm’s length, uncompromising and ever so hard to know. You seem like you care very little about what people think of you. You seem like you know what you’re doing. You seem equally horrible and awesome, and just the thought that there’s someone you’d like to open up to, someone who’s not me, makes me feel like I can’t sit at this table any longer. “Like you’d want your own space.”

    Kara held her gaze. “Lena, I think I’ll be fine.”

    “But if you end up not being fine, then you’d be stuck with me.”

    “It’s one night.” Kara’s jaw clenched and relaxed, and she added, “We are friends, no?”

    Her own words, thrown back at her. I don’t want to be your friend, she was tempted to say. Thing was, she also didn’t want to not be Kara’s friend. What she wanted was completely outside of her ability to obtain, and she needed to forget it. Scrap it from her brain.

    “Yes. We are.”

    “Then, as a friend, don’t force me to worry about you using public transportation late at night in a city you’re not familiar with. Biking on roads without bike lanes is bad enough,” Kara muttered, and she immediately felt a weight sink into her stomach. Kara was trying to be a good friend. Kara cared for her, and instead of being satisfied with what she currently had, she had to ruin it all and—and want more.

    She took a deep breath. “Are you sure? That it wouldn’t bother you?”

    Kara nodded, silent.

    “Okay, then. Okay.” She forced herself to smile. “Do you snore?”

    Kara huffed out a laugh. “I don’t know.”

    “Oh, come on. How can you not know?”

    Kara shrugged. “I just don’t.”

    “Well, that probably means you don’t. Otherwise, someone would have told you.”

    “Someone?”

    “A roommate.” It occurred to her that Kara was thirty-four and likely hadn’t had a roommate in about a decade. “Or a girlfriend. or a boyfriend. A romantic partner.”

    Kara smiled faintly and lowered her gaze. “I guess my ‘girlfriend’ will tell me after SBD, then.” Kara said it in a quiet, unassuming tone, clearly trying to make a joke, but Lena’s cheeks warmed, and she couldn’t quite bear to look at Kara anymore.

    Instead, she picked at a thread on the sleeve of her cardigan and searched for something to say. “My stupid abstract.” She cleared her throat. “It was accepted as a talk.”

    Kara met her eyes. “Faculty panel?”

    “Yeah.”

    “You’re not happy?”

    “No.” She winced.

    “Is it the public-speaking thing?”

    Kara remembered. Of course she had.

    “Yeah. It will be awful.”

    Kara stared at her and said nothing. Not that it would be fine, not that the talk would go smoothly, not that she was overreacting and underselling a fantastic opportunity. Kara’s calm acceptance of her anxiety had the exact opposite effect of Dr. Daxam’s enthusiasm: it relaxed her.

    “When I was in my third year of grad school,” Kara said quietly, “my adviser sent me to give a faculty symposium in his stead. He told me only two days before, without any slides or a script. Just the title of the talk.”

    “Wow.” Lena tried to imagine what that would have felt like, being expected to perform something so daunting with so little forewarning. At the same time, part of her marvelled at Kara self-disclosing something without being asked a direct question. “Why did he do that?”

    “Who knows?” Kara tilted her head back, staring at a spot above her head. Her tone held a trace of bitterness. “Because he had an emergency. Because he thought it’d be a formative experience. Because he could.”

    Lena just bet that he could. She didn’t know Kara’s former adviser, but academia was very much an old boys’ club, where those who held the power liked to take advantage of those who didn’t without repercussions.

    “Was it? A formative experience?”

    Kara shrugged again. “As much as anything that keeps you awake in a panic for forty-eight hours straight can be.”

    Lena smiled. “And how did you do?”

    “I did . . .” Kara pressed her lips together. “Not well enough.” She was silent for a long moment, her gaze locked somewhere outside the café’s window. “Then again, nothing was ever good enough.”

    It seemed impossible that someone might look at Kara’s scientific accomplishments and find them lacking. That she could ever be anything less than the best at what she did. Was that why she was so severe in her judgment of others? Because she’d been taught to set the same impossible standards for herself?

    “Do you still keep in touch with him? Your adviser, I mean.”

    “He’s retired now. Mike has taken over what used to be his lab.”

    It was such an uncharacteristically opaque, carefully worded answer. Lena couldn’t help being curious.

    “Did you like your advisor?”

    “It’s complicated.” Kara rubbed a hand over her jaw, looking pensive and far away. “No. No, I didn’t like him. I still don’t. He was . . .” It took Kara so long to continue, she almost convinced herself that Kara wouldn’t. But she did, staring at the late-afternoon sunlight disappearing behind the oak trees. “Brutal. My adviser was brutal.”

    Lena chuckled, and Kara’s eyes darted back to her face, narrow with confusion. “Sorry.” She was still laughing a little. “It’s just funny, to hear you complain about your old mentor. Because . . .”

    “Because?”

    “Because he sounds exactly like you.”

    “I’m not like him,” Kara retorted, more sharply than Lena had come to expect from her.

    It made her snort.

    “Kara, I’m pretty sure that if we were to ask anyone to describe you with one word, ‘brutal’ would come up one or ten times.” She saw Kara stiffen before she was even done speaking, the line of his shoulders suddenly tense and rigid, her jaw tight and with a slight twitch to it. Her first instinct was to apologize, but she was not sure for what. There was nothing new to what she’d just told Kara—they’d discussed her blunt, uncompromising mentoring style before, and Kara had always taken it in stride. Owned it, even. And yet her fists were clenched on the table, and her eyes were darker than usual. “I . . . Kara, did I—” she stammered, but Kara interrupted her before she could continue.

    “Everyone has issues with their advisers,” Kara said, and there was a finality to her tone that warned Lena not to finish her sentence. Not to ask What happened? Where did you just go?

    So she swallowed and nodded. “Dr. Daxam is . . .” She hesitated. Kara’s knuckles were not quite as white anymore, and the tension in her muscles was slowly dissolving. It was possible that she’d imagined it. Yes, she must have. “She’s great. But sometimes I feel like she doesn’t really understand that I need more . . .” Guidance. Support. Some practical advice, instead of blind encouragement. “I’m not even sure what I need, myself. I think that might be part of the problem —I’m not very good at communicating it.”

    Kara nodded and appeared to choose her words carefully. “It’s hard, mentoring. No one teaches you how to do it. We’re trained to become scientists, but as professors, we’re also in charge of making sure that students learn to produce rigorous science. I hold my grads accountable, and I set high standards for them. They’re scared of me, and that’s fine. The stakes are high, and if being scared means that they’re taking their training seriously, then I’m okay with it.”

    Lena tilted her head. “What do you mean?”

    “My job is to make sure that my adult graduate students don’t become mediocre scientists. That means I’m the one who’s tasked with demanding that they rerun their experiments or adjust their hypotheses. It comes with the territory.”

    Lena had never been a people pleaser, but Kara’s attitude toward others’ perception of her was so cavalier, it was almost fascinating.

    “Do you really not care?” she asked, curious. “That your grads might dislike you as a person?”

    “Nah. I don’t like them very much, either.” She thought of Jess and Ben and the other half a dozen grads and postdocs mentored by Kara whom she didn’t know very well. The thought of Kara finding them as annoying as they found her despotic made her chuckle. “To be fair, I don’t like people in general.”

    “Right.” Don’t ask, Lena. Do not ask. “Do you like me?”

    A millisecond of hesitation as Kara pressed her lips together. “Nope. You’re a smart-ass with abysmal taste in beverages.” Kara traced the corner of her iPad, a small smile playing on her lips. “Send me your slides.”

    “My slides?”

    “For your talk. I’ll take a look at them.”

    Lena tried not to gape at her. “Oh—you . . . I’m not your grad. You don’t have to.”

    “I know.”

    “You really don’t have to—”

    “I want to,” Kara said, voice pitched low and even as she looked into Lena eyes, and Lena had to avert her gaze because something felt too tight in her chest.

    “Okay.” She finally managed to snap out the loose thread on her sleeve. “How likely is it that your feedback will cause me to cry under the shower?”

    “That depends on the quality of your slides.”

    She smiled. “Don’t feel like you have to hold back.”

    “Believe me, I don’t.”

    “Good. Great.” She sighed, but it was reassuring, knowing that Kara was going to be checking her work. “Will you come to my talk?” she heard herself ask, and was as surprised by the request as Kara seemed to be.

    “I . . . Do you want me to?”

    No. No, it’s going to be horrible, and humiliating, and probably a disaster, and you’re going to see me at my worst and weakest. It’s probably best if you lock yourself into the bathroom for the entire duration of the panel. Just so you don’t accidentally wander in and see me making a fool of myself. And yet. Just the idea of having Kara there, sitting in the audience, made the prospect seem like less of an ordeal.

    Kara was not her adviser, and she wasn’t going to be able to do much if Lena got inundated by a barrage of impossible questions, or if the projector stopped working halfway through the talk. But maybe that wasn’t what she needed from Kara. It hit her then what was so special about Kara.

    That no matter her reputation, or how rocky their first meeting, since the very beginning, Lena had felt that Kara was on her side. Over and over, and in ways that she could never have anticipated, Kara had made her feel unjudged. Less alone. She exhaled slowly. The realization should have been rattling, but it had an oddly calming effect.

    “Yes,” she told Kara, thinking that this might very well turn out to be all right. She might never have what she wanted from Kara, but for now at least, Kara was in her life. That was going to have to be enough.

    “I will, then.”

    She leaned forward. “Will you ask a long-winded, leading question that will cause me to ramble incoherently and lose the respect of my peers, thus forever undermining my place in the field of biology?”

    “Possibly.” Kara was smiling. “Should I buy you that disgusting”—Kara gestured toward the register—“pumpkin sludge now? And cookies?”

    She grinned. “Oh, yes. I mean, if you want to.”

    “I’d rather buy you anything else.”

    “Too bad.” Lena jumped to her feet and headed for the counter, tugging at Kara’s sleeve and forcing Kara to stand with her.

    Kara followed meekly, mumbling something about black coffee that Lena chose to ignore.

    Enough, she repeated to herself. What you have now, it will have to be enough.

    The SBD Talk

    Chapter Notes

    TW: Non-Consensual touching and other shit that I don't exactly know what to call

    I apologize in advance for this chapter.

    HYPOTHESIS: This conference will be the worst thing to ever happen to my professional career, general well-being, and sense of sanity.

    There were two beds in the hotel room. Two double beds to be precise, and as she stared at them, Lena felt her shoulders sag with relief and had to resist the urge to fist-pump.

    Take that, you stupid rom coms.

    She may have fallen for the woman she’d begun to fake-date like some born-yesterday fool, but at least she wouldn’t be sharing a bed with her any time soon. Given her disastrous past couple of weeks, she’d really, really needed the win.

    There were a number of little clues that Kara had slept on the bed closest to the entrance—a book on the bedside table in a language that looked like it was probably kryptonese or some other alien language, a thumb drive and the same iPad she’d seen Kara carry around on several occasions, an iPhone charger dangling from the power outlet. A suitcase tucked by the foot of the bed, black and expensive looking. Unlike Lena’s, it probably hadn’t been fished out of the Walmart bargain bin.

    “I guess this is mine, then,” she murmured, sitting on the bed closest to the window and bouncing a few times to test the firmness of the mattress.

    It was a nice room. Not ridiculously fancy, but Lena was suddenly grateful for the way Kara had snorted and looked at her like she was crazy when she’d offered to pay for half of it. At least the place was wide enough that they weren’t going to have to brush up against each other every time they moved around. Staying in here with Kara wouldn’t feel like a singularly sadistic version of seven minutes in heaven. Not that they’d be together much. She was going to give her talk in a couple of hours—ugh—then go to the department’s social and hang out with her friends until . . . well, as long as she feasibly could. Odds were that Kara already had tons of meetings scheduled, and maybe they wouldn’t even see each other.

    Lena would be asleep when Kara came back tonight, and tomorrow morning one of them would pretend not to wake up while the other got ready. It was going to be fine.

    Harmless.

    At the very least, not make things worse than they currently were.

    Lena’s usual conference outfit was black jeans and her least-frayed cardigan, but a few days ago Andrea had mentioned that the ensemble might be too casual for a talk. After sighing for hours Lena had decided to bring the black wrap dress she’d bought on sale before interviewing for grad school and black heels borrowed from Sam. It had seemed like a good idea at the time, but as soon as she slipped into the bathroom to put on the dress, she realized that it must have shrunk the last time she washed it. It didn’t quite hit her knees anymore, not by a couple of inches.

    She groaned and snapped a picture for Andrea and Sam, who texted her, respectively, Still conference appropriate and a fire emoji.

    Lena prayed that Andrea was right as she combed the waves in her hair and fought against dried-out mascara—her fault for buying makeup at the dollar store, clearly. She had just got out of the bathroom, rehearsing her talk under her breath, when the door opened and someone—Kara, of course it was Kara—entered the room.

    Kara was holding her key card and typing something in her phone, but stopped as soon as she looked up and noticed Lena. Her mouth opened, and— That was it. It just stayed open.

    “Hey.” Lena forced her face into a smile. Her heart was doing something weird in her chest. Beating a little too quickly. She should probably have it checked as soon as she got back home. One could never be too careful about cardiovascular health.

    “Hi.” Kara snapped her mouth closed and cleared her throat. “You’re . . .” Kars swallowed and shifted on her feet. “Here.”

    “Yep.” Lena nodded, still smiling. “Just arrived. My flight landed on time, surprisingly.”

    Kara seemed a little slow. Maybe jet-lagged from her own flight, or perhaps last night she’d been out late with her famous scientist friends, or with the mysterious woman Alex had talked about. She just stared at Lena, silent for several moments, and when she spoke, it was only to say, “You look . . .”

    Lena glanced down at her dress and heels, wondering if her eye makeup was already smudged. She’d put it on three whole minutes ago, so it was more than likely. “Professional?”

    “That’s not what I . . .” Kara closed her eyes and shook her head, as if collecting herself. “But, yes. You do. How are you?”

    “Good. Fine. I mean, I wish I were dead. But aside from that.”

    Kara laughed silently and moved closer. “You’ll be okay.”

    Lena had thought sweaters were a good look for Kara, but only because she’d never seen Kara wear a suit. Kara had a secret weapon all along, she thought, trying not to stare too hard. And now she’s unleashing it. Damn her.

    “Agreed.” Lena pushed her hair back and smiled. “After I die.”

    “You’re fine. You have a script. You memorized it. Your slides are good.”

    “I think they were better before you made me change the PowerPoint background.”

    “It was acid green.”

    “I know. It made me happy.”

    “It made me nauseous.”

    “Mm. Anyway, thanks again for helping me figure it out.” And for answering the 139 questions I asked. Thank you for taking less than ten minutes to reply to my emails, every time, even when it was 5:30 a.m. and you misspelled ‘consensus’, which is unusual of you and makes me suspect that maybe you were still half asleep. “And for letting me crash with you.”

    “No problem.”

    Lena scratched the side of her nose. “I figured you were using that bed, so I put my stuff here, but if you . . .” She gestured confusedly at the room.

    “No, that’s where I slept last night.”

    “Okay.” She was not counting how many inches there were between the two beds. Definitely not. “So how’s the conference so far?”

    “Same old. I was mostly at Harvard for a few meetings with Mike. I only got back for lunch.” Lena’s stomach rumbled loudly at the mention of food. “You okay?”

    “Yeah. I think I forgot to eat today.”

    Kara’s eyebrows arched. “I didn’t think you capable.”

    “Hey!” She glared at Kara. “The sustained levels of despair I’ve been engaging in for the past week require a staggering number of calories, in case you— What are you doing?” Kara was leaning over her suitcase, rummaging for something that she held out to Lena. “What is it?”

    “Calories. To fuel your despair habits.”

    “Oh.” She accepted it and then studied the protein bar in her hands, trying not to burst out crying. It was just food. Probably a snack Kara brought for the plane ride and ended up not eating. Kara didn’t need to despair, after all. She was Dr. Kara Zor-El. “Thanks. Are you...” The wrapping of the bar crinkled as she shifted it from one hand to another. “Are you still coming to my talk?”
