    “Today at four, room 278. Session three-b. The good news is that it partially overlaps with the keynote address, which means that hopefully only a handful of people will show up . . .” Kara’s spine stiffened noticeably. Lena hesitated. “Unless you were planning to go to the keynote address?”

    Kara wet her lips. “I . . .”

    Lena’s eyes chose that precise moment to fall to the conference badge dangling from Kara’s neck. Kara Zor-El, Ph.D. Stanford University Keynote Speaker. Her jaw dropped. “Oh my God.” She looked up at Kara, wide-eyed, and . . . Oh God. At least Kara had the grace to look sheepish. “How did you not tell me that you are the keynote speaker?”

    Kara scratched her jaw, oozing discomfort. “I didn’t think of it.”

    “Oh my God,” she repeated. To be fair, it was on her. The name of the keynote speaker was likely printed in font size 300 in the program, and all the promotional material, not to mention the conference app and the emails. Lena must have had her head very much up her butt to fail to notice. “Kara.” She made to rub her eyes with her fingers, and then thought better of it. Damn makeup. “I can’t be fake dating SBD’s keynote speaker.”

    “Well, there are technically three keynote speakers, and the other two are married women in their fifties who live in Europe and Japan, so—”

    Lena crossed her arms on her chest and gave Kara a flat look until she quieted. Lena couldn’t help laughing. “How did this not come up?”

    “It’s not a big deal.” Kara shrugged. “I doubt I was their first choice.”

    “Right.” Sure. Because a person existed who’d refuse to be keynote speaker at SBD. She tilted her head. “Did you think I was an idiot, when I started complaining about my ten-minute talk that will be attended by fourteen and a half people?”

    “Not at all. Your reaction was understandable.” Kara thought about it for a moment. “I do sometimes think you’re an idiot, mostly when I see you put ketchup and cream cheese on bagels.”

    “It’s a great mix.”

    Kara looked pained. “When are you presenting in your panel? Maybe I can still make it.”

    “No. I’m exactly halfway through.” She waved a hand, hoping to seem unconcerned. “It’s fine, really.” And it was. “I’m going to have to record myself with my iPhone, anyway.” She rolled her eyes. “For Dr. Daxam. She couldn’t come to the conference, but she said she wants to listen to my first talk. I can send it to you, if you’re a fan of stammering and second-hand embarrassment.”

    “I’d like that.”

    Lena flushed and changed the topic. “Is that why you have a room for the entire length of the conference even though you’re not staying? Because you’re a big shot?”

    Kara frowned. “I’m not.”

    “Can I call you ‘big shot’ from now on?”

    Kara sighed, walking to the bedside table and pocketing the USB Lena had noticed earlier. “I have to take my slides downstairs, smart-ass.”

    “Okay.” Kara could leave. It was fine. Totally fine. Lena didn’t let her smile falter. “I guess I’ll maybe see you after my talk, then?”

    “Of course.”

    “And after yours. Good luck. And congrats. It’s such a huge honour.”

    Kara didn’t seem to be thinking about that, though. She lingered by the door, her hand on the knob as she looked back at Lena. Their eyes held for a few moments before Kara told her, “Don’t be nervous, okay?”

    Lena pressed her lips together and nodded. “I’ll just do what Dr. Daxam always says.”

    “And what’s that?”

    “Carry myself with the confidence of a mediocre white man.”

    Kara grinned, and—there they were. The heart-stopping dimples. “It will be fine, Lena.” Her smile softened. “And if not, at least it will be over.”

    It wasn’t until a few minutes later, when she was sitting on her bed staring at the Boston skyline and chewing on her lunch, that Lena realized that the protein bar Kara had given her was covered in chocolate.

    —
    She checked whether she had the correct room for the third time—nothing like talking about pancreatic cancer to a crowd that expected a presentation on the Golgi apparatus to make an impression—and then felt a hand close around her shoulder. She spun around, noticed who it belonged to, and immediately grinned. “Mike!”

    He was wearing a charcoal suit. His hair was combed back, making him look older than he had in California, but also professional. He was a friendly face in a sea of unfamiliar ones, and his presence took the edge off her intense desire to puke in her own shoe.

    “Hey, Lena.” He held the door open for her. “I thought I might see you here.”

    “Oh?”

    “From the conference program.” He looked at her oddly. “You didn’t notice we’re on the same panel?”

    Oh, crap. “Uh—I . . . I didn’t even read who else was on the panel.” Because I was too busy panicking.

    “No worries. It’s mostly boring people.” He winked, and his hand slid to her back, guiding her toward the podium. “Except for you and me, of course.”

    Her talk didn’t go poorly. It didn’t go perfectly, either. She stumbled on the word ‘channel rhodopsin’ twice, and by some weird trick of the projector her staining looked more like a black blob than a slice.

    “It looks different on my computer,” Lena told the audience with a strained smile. “Just trust me on this one.” People chuckled, and she relaxed marginally, grateful that she’d spent hours upon hours memorizing everything she was supposed to say.

    The room was not as full as she’d feared, and there were a handful of people—likely working on similar projects at other institutions—who took notes and listened raptly to her every word. It should have been overwhelming and anxiety inducing, but about halfway through she realized that it made her oddly giddy, knowing that someone else was passionate about the same research questions that had taken up most of the past two years of her life.

    In the second row, Sam faked a fascinated expression, while Andrea, Jack, and a bunch of other grads from Stanford nodded enthusiastically whenever Lena happened to look in their direction. Mike alternated between staring intensely at her and checking his phone with a bored expression—fair, since he’d already read her report.

    The session was running late, and the moderator ended up giving her time for only one question —an easy one. At the end, two of the other panellists—well-known cancer researchers whom Lena had to restrain herself not to fangirl over—shook her hand and asked her several questions about her work. She was simultaneously flustered and overjoyed.

    “You were so amazing,” Andrea told her when it was over, pushing up to hug her. “Also, you look hot and professional, and while you were talking, I had a vision of your future in academia.”

    Lena wrapped her arms around Andrea. “What vision?”

    “You were a high-powered researcher, surrounded by students who hung on your every word. And you were answering a multiparagraph email with an uncapitalized no.”

    “Nice. Was I happy?”

    “Of course not.” Andrea snorted. “It’s academia.”

    “Ladies, the department social starts in half an hour.” Sam leaned in to kiss Lena on the cheek and squeeze her waist. When she was wearing heels, Sam was just a tiny bit shorter than her. She definitely wanted a picture of the two of them side by side. “We should go celebrate the single time Lena managed to pronounce ‘channel rhodopsin’ right with some free booze.”

    “You dick.”

    Sam pulled her in for a tight hug and whispered in her ear, “You did amazing Lee.” And then, louder: “Let’s go get wasted!”

    “Why don’t you guys go ahead? I’ll get my USB and put my stuff back in the hotel.”

    Lena made her way through the now-empty room to the podium, feeling like a huge weight had been lifted off her shoulders. She was relaxed and relieved. Professionally, things were starting to look up: as it turned out, with adequate preparation she could actually string together several coherent sentences in front of other scientists. She also had the means to carry out her research next year, and two big names in her field had just complimented her work. She smiled, letting her mind wander to whether she should text Kara to tell her that she was right, she did make it out alive; she should probably ask how her keynote address had gone, too. If Kara’s PowerPoint had acted up and she’d mispronounced words like ‘microarrays’ or ‘karyotyping,’ whether she planned to go to the department social. Kara was probably meeting up with friends, but maybe she could buy Kara a thank-you drink for all her help. She would even pay, for once.

    “It went well,” someone said.

    Lena turned to find Mike standing behind her, arms folded across his chest as he leaned against the table. He looked as though he’d been staring at her for a while.

    “Thank you. Yours, too.” His talk had been a more condensed repeat of the one he’d given at Stanford, and Lena had to admit that she’d spaced out a bit.

    “Where’s Kara?” he asked.

    “Still giving her keynote, I think.”

    “Right.” Mike rolled his eyes. Probably with fondness, though Lena didn’t quite catch it in his expression. “She does that, doesn’t she?”

    “Does what?”

    “Outdoes you.” He pushed away from the table, ambling closer. “Well, outdoes everyone. It’s not personal.” Lena frowned, confused, wanting to ask Mike what he meant by that, but he continued, “I think you and I will get along great next year.”

    The reminder that Mike believed in her work enough to take her in his lab quashed her discomfort. “We will.” She smiled. “Thank you so much for giving me and my project a chance. I can’t wait to start working with you.”

    “You’re welcome.” He was smiling, too. “I think there are a lot of things we can gain from each other. Wouldn’t you agree?”

    It seemed to Lena like she had much more to gain from it than he did, but she nodded anyway. “I hope so. I think imaging and blood biomarkers complement each other perfectly, and only by combining them can we—”

    “And I have what you need, don’t I? The research funds. The lab space. The time and ability to mentor you properly.”

    “Yes. You do. I . . .” All of a sudden, she could pick out the gray rim of his cornea. Had he gotten closer? He was tall, but not that much taller than her. He didn’t usually feel this imposing. “I’m grateful. So grateful. I’m sure that—” She felt his unfamiliar smell in her nostrils, and his breath, hot and unpleasant against the corner of her mouth, and— fingers, a vise-tight grip around her upper arm, and why was he—what was he— “What—” Heart in her throat, Lena freed her arm and took several steps back. “What are you doing?” Her hand came up to her biceps and—it hurt, where he’d clasped her. God—had he really done that? Tried to kiss her? No, she must have imagined it. She must be going crazy, because Mike would never—

    “A preview, I think.”

    She just stared at him, too stunned and numb to react, until he moved closer and bent once more toward her. Then it was happening all over again. She pushed him away. As forcefully as she could, she pushed him away with both her hands on his chest, until he stumbled back with a cruel, condescending laugh. Abruptly, her lungs seized and she couldn’t breathe. “A preview of—what? Are you out of your mind?”

    “Come on.” Why was he smiling? Why was that oily, hateful expression on his face? Why was he looking at her like— “A pretty girl like you should know the score by now.” He looked at her from head to toe, and the lewd gleam in his eyes made her feel disgusting. “Don’t lie to me and say you didn’t pick out a dress that short for my benefit. Nice legs, by the way. I can see why Kara’s wasting her time with you.”

    “The— What are you—”

    “Lena.” He sighed, putting his hands in his pockets. He should have looked nonthreatening, lounging like that. But he felt like anything but. “You don’t think I accepted you into my lab because you are good, do you?” Slack-jawed, she took one more step back. One of her heels almost caught in the carpet, and she had to hold on to the table to avoid falling. “A girl like you. Who figured out so early in her academic career that fucking well-known, successful scholars is how to get ahead.” He was still smiling. The same smile Lena had once thought kind. Reassuring. “You fucked Kara, didn’t you? We both know you’re going to fuck me for the same reason.”

    She was going to vomit. She was going to vomit in this room, after all, and it had nothing to do with her talk. “You are disgusting.”

    “Am I?” He shrugged, unperturbed. “That makes two of us. You used Kara to get to me and to my lab. To this conference, too.”

    “I didn’t. I didn’t even know Kara when I submitted—”

    “Oh, please. You’re telling me you thought your pitiful abstract was selected for a talk because of its quality and scientific importance?” He made a disbelieving face. “Someone here has a very high opinion of herself, considering that her research is useless and derivative and that she can barely put together two words without stuttering like an idiot.”

    She froze. Her stomach sank and twisted; her feet cemented to the ground. “It’s not true,” she whispered.

    “No? You think it’s not true that scientists in the field want to impress the great Kara Zor-El, Krypton’s youngest science prodigy, enough to kiss the ass of whoever she’s fucking at the moment? I certainly did when I told her very mediocre girlfriend that she could come work for me. But maybe you’re right,” he said, all mocking affability. “Maybe you know STEM academia better than I do.”

    “I’m going to tell Kara about this. I’m going to—”

    “By all means.” Mike widened his arms. “Go ahead. Be my guest. Do you need to borrow my phone?”

    “No.” Her nostrils flared. A wave of icy anger swept over her. “No.” She turned around and marched to the entrance, fighting the nausea and bile climbing up her throat. She was going to find Kara. She was going to find the conference organizers and report Mike. She was never going to see his face again.

    “Quick question. Who do you think Kara will believe, Lena?” She halted abruptly, just a few feet from the door. “Some bitch she’s been fucking for about two weeks, or someone who’s been a close friend for years? Someone who helped her get the most important grant of her career? Someone who’s had her back since she was younger than you are? Someone who’s actually a good scientist?”

    She spun around, shaking with rage. “Why are you doing this?”

    “Because I can.” Mike shrugged again. “Because as advantageous as my collaboration with Kara has been, sometimes it’s a bit annoying how she needs to be best at everything, and I like the idea of taking something away from her for once. Because you are very pretty, and I look forward to spending more time with you next year. Who would have guessed that the alien had such good taste?”

    “You are crazy. If you think that I’ll work in your lab, you are—”

    “Oh, Lena. But you will. Because you see—while your work is not particularly brilliant, it does complement nicely the ongoing projects in my lab.”

    She let out a single, bitter laugh. “Are you really so deluded that you think I would ever collaborate with you after this?”

    “Mmm. It’s more that you don’t have a choice. Because if you want to finish your project, my lab is your only opportunity. And if you don’t . . . well. You sent me information on all your protocols, which means that I can easily replicate them. But don’t worry. Maybe I’ll mention you in the acknowledgment section.”

    She felt the ground flip under her feet. “You wouldn’t,” she whispered. “It’s research misconduct.”

    “Listen, Lena. My friendly advice is: suck it up. Keep Kara happy and interested as long as possible, and then come to my lab to finally do some decent work. If you keep me happy, I’ll make sure you can save the world from pancreatic cancer. Your nice little sob story about your mom or your aunt or your stupid kindergarten teacher dying from it is only going to get you so far. You’re mediocre.”

    Lena turned around and ran from the room.

    —
    When she heard the beep of the key card, she immediately wiped her face with the sleeves of her dress. It didn’t quite do the trick: she’d been crying for a solid twenty minutes, and even an entire paper towel roll wouldn’t have been enough to hide what she’d been up to. Really, though, it wasn’t Lena’s fault. She’d been sure Kara had to attend the opening ceremony, or at least the department social after her talk. Wasn’t she on the social-and-networking committee? She should have been elsewhere. Socializing. Networking. Committeeing. But here she was.

    Lena heard steps as Kara walked inside, then her stopping at the entrance of the bedroom, and . . . She couldn’t convince her eyes to meet Kara’s. She was a mess after all, a miserable, disastrous mess. But she should at least attempt to divert Kara’s attention. Maybe by saying something. Anything.

    “Hey.” She tried a smile, but continued to stare down at her own hands. “How did your address go?”

    “What happened?” Kara’s voice was calm, pitched low.

    “Did you only just finish?” Her smile was holding. Good. Good, that was good. “How was the Q and A—”

    “What happened?”

    “Nothing. I . . .” She didn’t manage to finish the sentence. And the smile— which, if she was honest with herself, hadn’t been much of a smile to begin with—was crumbling.

    Lena heard Kara come closer but didn’t look at her. Her closed eyelids were all that was keeping the floodgates shut, and they weren’t doing a good job of it, either. She startled when she found Kara kneeling in front of her. Right by her chair, Kara’s head level with hers, studying her with a worried frown. She made to hide her face in her palms, but Kara’s hand came up to her chin and lifted it up, until she had no choice but to meet her eyes. Then Kara’s fingers slid up to her cheek, cupping it as she asked, yet again, “Lena, what happened?”

    “Nothing.” Her voice shook. It kept disappearing somewhere, melting in the tears.

    “Lena.”

    “Really. Nothing.”

    Kara stared at her, questioning, and didn’t let go. “Did someone buy the last bag of chips?”

    A laugh bubbled out of her, wet and not wholly under her control. “Yes. Was it you?”

    “Of course.” Kara’s thumb swiped across her cheekbone, stopping a falling tear. “I bought all of them.”

    This smile felt better than the one she’d cobbled together earlier. “You monster.” She must have been leaning into Kara’s hand, because Kara’s thumb was stroking her again. Ever so gently.

    “Is that how you talk to your fake girlfriend?” Kara looked so worried. Her eyes, the line of her mouth. And yet—so patient. “What happened, Lena?”

    She shook her head. “I just . . .” She couldn’t tell Kara. And she couldn’t not tell her. But above all, she couldn’t tell her. Who do you think Kara will believe, Lena? She had to take a deep breath. Push Mike’s voice out of her head and calm herself before continuing. Come up with something to say, something that wouldn’t make the sky fall in this hotel room. “My talk. I thought it went okay. My friends said it did. But then I heard people talking about it, and they said…”

    Kara really should stop touching her. She must be getting her whole hand wet. The sleeve of her coat, too.

    “What did they say?”

    “Nothing. That it was derivative. Boring. That I stammered. They knew that I’m your girlfriend and said that was the only reason I was chosen to give a talk.” She shook her head. She needed to let it go. To put it out of her head. To think carefully about what to do.

    “Who? Who were they?”

    Oh, Kara. “Someone. I’m not sure.”

    “Did you see their badges?”

    “I . . . didn’t pay attention.”

    “Were they on your panel?” There was something underneath Kara’s tone. Something pressing that hinted at violence and rage and broken bones. Kara’s hand was still gentle on her cheek, but her eyes narrowed. There was a new tension in her jaw, and Lena felt a shiver run down her spine.

    “No,” she lied. “It doesn’t matter. It’s okay.” Kara’s lips pressed into a straight line, her nostrils flared, so Lena added, “I don’t care what people think of me, anyway.”

    “Right,” Kara scoffed. This Kara, right here, was the moody, irascible Kara who grads in her program complained about.

    Lena shouldn’t have been surprised to see her this angry, but Kara has never been like this with her before.

    “No, really, I don’t care what people say—”

    “I know you don’t. But that’s the problem, isn’t it?” Kara stared at her, and she was so close. Lena could see how the blues and greens in her eyes. “It’s not what they say. It’s what you think. It’s that you think they’re right. Don’t you?”

    “I . . .”

    “Lena, you are a great scientist. And you will become an even better one.” The way Kara was looking at her, so earnest and serious—it was going to break her. “Whatever this asshole said, it speaks nothing of you and a whole lot of them.” Kara’s fingers shifted on her skin to weave through the hair behind her ear. “Your work is brilliant.”

    She didn’t even think it through. And even if she had, she probably couldn’t have stopped herself. She just leaned forward and hid her face in Kara’s neck, hugging her tight. A terrible idea, stupid and inappropriate, and Kara was surely going to push her away, any minute now, except that…Kara’s palm slid to her nape, almost as if to press Lena into her, and Lena just stayed there for long minutes, crying warm tears into the flesh of Kara’s throat, feeling how grounding, how warm, how solid Kara was—under her fingers and in her life.

    You just had to go and make me fall for you, she thought, blinking against Kara’s skin. You absolute ass.

    Kara didn’t let her go. Not until she pulled back and wiped her cheeks again, feeling like maybe this time around she’d be able to hold it together. She sniffled, and Kara leaned over to grab a box of tissues from the TV table.

    “I really am fine,” she said. Kara sighed. “Okay, maybe . . . maybe I’m not fine right now, but I will be.” She accepted the tissue that Kara plucked for her and blew her nose. “I just need a while to . . .” Kara studied her and nodded, her eyes unreadable again. “Thank you. For what you said. For letting me snot all over your hotel room.”

    Kara smiled. “Anytime.”

    “And your coat, too. Are you…Are you going to the department social?” she asked, dreading the moment she would have to get out of this chair. Of this room. Be honest, that sensible, ever-knowing voice inside her whispered. It’s Kara’s presence that you don’t want to be out of.

    “Are you?”

    Lena shrugged. “I said I would. But I don’t feel like talking to anyone right now.” She dried her cheeks once more, but miraculously the flow had stopped. Kara Zor-El, responsible for 90 percent of the department’s tears, had actually managed to make someone stop crying. Who would’ve thought? “Though I feel like the free alcohol could really help.”

    Kara stared at her pensively for a moment, biting the inside of her cheek. Then she nodded, seeming to reach some sort of decision, and stood with her hand held out to Lena. “Come on.”

    “Oh.” Lena had to crane her neck to look up at her. “I think I’m going to wait a bit before I—”

    “We’re not going to the social.”

    We? “What?”

    “Come on,” Kara repeated, and this time Lena took her hand and didn’t let go. She couldn’t, with the way Kara’s fingers were closing around hers. Kara looked pointedly at her shoes, until she got the hint and slipped them on, using Kara’s arm to keep her balance.

    “Where are we going?”

    “To get some free alcohol. Well”—Kara amended—“free for you.”

    She almost gasped when she realized what Kara meant. “No, I—Kara, no. You have to go to the department social. And to the opening ceremony. You’re the keynote speaker!”

    “And I keynote-spoke.” Kara grabbed Lena’s red duffle coat from the bed and pulled her toward the entrance. “Can you walk in those shoes?”

    “I—yes, but—”

    “I have my key card; we don’t need yours.”

    “Kara.” She grabbed Kara’s wrist, and Kara immediately turned to look at her. “Kara, you can’t skip those events. People will say that you—”

    Kara’s smile was lopsided. “That I want to spend time with my girlfriend?”

    Lena’s brain stopped. Just like that. And then it started again, and— The world was a little different.

    When Kara tugged her hand again, she smiled and simply followed her out of the room.

    Chapter End Notes

    The Date

    Chapter Notes

    Not proof read, bear with me.

    HYPOTHESIS: There is no moment in life that cannot be improved by food delivered by conveyor belt.

    Everyone saw them. People whom Lena had never met before, people whom she recognized from blog posts and science Twitter, people from her department who’d been her teachers in previous years. People who smiled at Kara, who addressed her by her first name or as Dr. Zor-El, who told her ‘Great talk’ or ‘See you around.’ People who completely ignored Lena, and people who studied her curiously—her, and Kara, and the place where their hands were joined. Kara mostly nodded back, only stopping to chat with Alex.

    “You guys skipping the boring shit?” Alex asked with a knowing smile.

    “Yep.”

    “I’ll make sure to drink your booze, then. And to extend your apologies.”

    “No need.”

    “I’ll just say you had a family emergency.” Alex winked. “Perhaps future-family emergency, how does that sound?”

    Kara rolled her eyes and pulled Lena outside. She had to hurry to keep up with Kara, not because Kara was walking particularly fast, but because Kara’s legs were so long, one stride was worth about three of hers.

    “Um . . . I’m wearing heels, here.”

    Kara turned to her, her eyes traveling down Lena’s legs and then rapidly moving away. “I know. You’re less vertically challenged than usual.”

    Lena’s eyes narrowed. “Hey, I’m five-six. That’s actually pretty tall.”

    “Hm.” Kara’s expression was noncommittal.

    “What’s that face?”

    “What face?”

    “Your face.”

    “Just my regular face?”

    “No, that’s your ‘you’re not tall’ face.”

    Kara smiled, just a smidge. “Are the shoes okay for walking? Should we go back?”

    “They’re fine, but can we slow down?” Kara feigned a sigh, but she did. Her hand let go of Lena’s and pushed against her lower back to steer her to the right. Lena had to hide a small shiver. “So . . .” She stuffed her fists in the pockets of her coat, trying to ignore how the tips of her fingers were still tingling. “Those free drinks you mentioned? Do they come with food?”

    “I’ll get you dinner.” Kara’s lips curved a little more. “You’re not a cheap date, though.”

    Lena leaned into Kara’s side and bumped her shoulder against Kara’s biceps. It was hard not to notice that there was no give. “I really am not. I fully plan to eat and drink my feelings.”

    Kara’s smile was more uneven than ever. “Where do you want to go, smart-ass?”

    “Let’s see . . . What do you like? Aside from tap water and hard-boiled spinach?”

    Kara gave her a dirty side-look. “How about burgers?”

    “Meh.” She shrugged. “I guess. If there’s nothing else.”

    “What’s wrong with burgers?”

    “I don’t know. Just not my favourite.”

    “You’re weird.”

    “What about Mexican? Do you like Mexican? Or Italian? Pizza would be great. And maybe there’s something celery-based that you could order. Oh, what about Chinese?”

    “Had it for lunch.”

    “Well, people in China have Chinese food multiple times a day, so you shouldn’t let that stop you from— Oh.”

    It took Kara two whole steps to realize that Lena had stopped in the middle of the sidewalk. She whirled around to look at Lena. “What?”

    “There.” She pointed to the red-and-white sign across the road.

    Kara’s gaze followed, and for a long moment she simply stared, blinking several times. And then: “No.”

    “There,” Lena repeated, feeling her cheeks widen into a grin.

    “Lena.” There was a deep vertical line between Kara’s eyebrows. “No. There are way better restaurants we can—”

    “But I want to go to that one.”

    “Why? There’s—”

    Lena moved closer to her and grasped the sleeve of her coat. “Please. Please?”

    Kara pinched her nose, sighed, and pursed her lips. But not five seconds later she put her hand between Lena’s shoulder blades to guide her across the street.

    The problem, Kara explained in hushed tones as they waited to be seated, was not the sushi train, but the all-you-can-eat for twenty dollars.

    “It’s never a good sign,” Kara told Lena, but her voice sounded more resigned than combative, and when the server ushered them inside, Kara followed her meekly to the booth.

    Lena marvelled at the plates traveling on the conveyor belt weaving across the restaurant, unable to stop her open-mouthed grin. When she remembered Kara’s presence and turned her attention back to her, Kara was staring at her with an expression halfway between exasperated and indulgent.

    “You know,” Kara told her, eyeing a seaweed salad passing by her shoulder, “we could go to a real Japanese restaurant. I am very happy to pay for however much sushi you want to eat.”

    “But will it move around me?”

    Kara shook her head. “I take it back: you are a disturbingly cheap date.”

    Lena ignored her and lifted the glass door, grabbing a roll and a chocolate doughnut. Kara muttered something that sounded a lot like ‘very authentic,’ and when the waitress stopped by she ordered them both a beer.

    “What do you think this is?” Lena dipped a piece of sushi in her soy sauce. “Tuna or salmon?”

    “Probably spider meat.”

    Lena popped it into her mouth. “Delicious.”

    “Really.” Kara looked sceptical.

She could tease apart the feelings struggling in Kara’s face—the surprise, the confusion, a hint of stubbornness, a split second of vulnerability that all melted in a blank, empty expression. Then she could see Kara’s throat work.

    “Right,” Kara said. “Right.”

    Kara was staring at her shoes, absolutely motionless. Slowly accepting Lena’s words. Lena took a step back and rocked on her heels. Outside, an iPhone rang, and a few seconds later someone burst into laughter. Normal noises, on a normal day. Normal, all of this.

    “It’s for the best,” Lena said, because the silence between them—that, she just couldn’t stand. “It’s what we agreed on.”

    “I can’t thank you enough for everything you’ve done for me. Not just about Andrea. When we met, I felt so alone, and . . .” For a moment she couldn’t continue. “Thank you for all the pumpkin spice, cookies, and for that Western blot, and for hiding your taxidermized squirrels when I visited, and . . .” Lena couldn’t bring herself to go on anymore, not without choking on her words. The stinging in her eyes was burning now, threatening to spill over, so she nodded once, decisively, a period to this dangling sentence with no end in sight.

    And that would have been it. It would have surely been the end. They would have left it at that, if Lena hadn’t passed Kara on her way to the door.

    If Kara hadn’t reached out and stopped her with a hand on her wrist.

    If Kara hadn’t immediately pulled that hand back and stared at it with an appalled expression, as if shocked that she’d dared to touch Lena without asking for permission first.

    If Kara hadn’t said, “Lena. If you ever need anything, anything at all. Anything. Whenever. You can come to me.” Kara’s jaw worked, like there were other words, words she was keeping inside. “I want you to come to me.”

    Lena almost didn’t register wiping wetness off her cheek with the back of her hand or moving closer to Kara. It was Kara’s scent that jolted her alert—soap and something dark, subtle but oh so familiar. Her brain had Kara mapped out, stored away across all senses. Eyes to Kara’s almost smile, hands to Kara’s skin, the smell of Kara in her nostrils. She didn’t even need to think about what to do, just push up on her toes, press her fingers against Kara’s biceps, and kiss her gently on the cheek. An apt goodbye, she thought. Appropriate. Acceptable. And so was Kara’s hand coming up to her lower back, pulling her closer and stopping her from sliding back on her heels, or the way Kara’s head turned, until Lena’s lips were not brushing the skin of her cheek anymore. Her breath hitched, a chuff against the corner of Kara’s mouth, and for a few precious seconds she just savoured it, the deep pleasure that ran through them both as they closed their eyes and let themselves just be, here, with each other.

    Quiet.

    Still.

    One last moment.

    Then Lena opened her mouth and turned her head, breathing against Kara’s lips, “Please.”

    Kara groaned but Lena was the one who closed the space between them, who deepened the kiss, who combed her hands into Kara’s hair, short nails scraping against Kara’s scalp. She was the one who pulled Kara even closer, and Kara was the one who pushed her back against the wall and moaned into her mouth. It was frightening. Frightening, how good this felt. How easy it would be to never stop. To let time stretch and unbend, forget about everything else, and simply stay in this moment forever.

    But Kara pulled back first, holding Lena’s eyes as she tried to collect herself.

    “It was good, wasn’t it?” Lena asked, with a small, wistful smile. She wasn’t herself sure what she was referring to. Maybe Kara’s arms around her. Maybe this last kiss. Maybe everything else. The sunscreen, Kara’s ridiculous answers on her favourite colour, the quiet conversations late at night . . . all of it had been so very good.

    “It was.” Kara’s voice sounded too deep to be her own. When she pressed her lips against Lena’s forehead one last time, Lena felt her love for Kara swell fuller than a river in flood.

    “I think I should leave,” Lena said gently, without looking at Kara.

    Kara let her go wordlessly, so she did. When she heard the click of the door closing behind her, it was like falling from a great height.

    The Truth pt1

    Chapter Notes

    HYPOTHESIS: When in doubt, asking a friend will save my ass.

    Lena spent the following day in the hotel, sleeping, crying, and doing the very thing that had gotten her into this mess to begin with: lying. She told Sam and Andrea that she’d be busy with friends from college for the entire day, pulled the blackout curtains together, and then buried herself in her bed. Which, technically, was Kara’s bed.

    She didn’t let herself think about the situation too much. Something inside her—her heart, very possibly—was broken in several large pieces, not shattered as much as neatly snapped in half, and then in half again. All she could do was sit down amid the debris of her feelings and wallow. Sleeping through most of the day helped dull the pain a great deal. Numb, she was rapidly starting to realize, was good.

    She lied the day after, too. Feigned a last-minute request from Dr. Daxam when asked to join her friends at the conference or on excursions around Boston, and then took a deep, fortifying breath. She drew the curtains open, forced her blood to start flowing again (with fifty crunches, fifty jumping jacks, and fifty push-ups, though she cheated on the last by going on her knees), then showered and brushed her teeth for the first time in thirty-six hours.

    It wasn’t easy.

    Seeing Kara’s Biology Ninja T-shirt in the mirror made her tear up, but she reminded herself that she’d made her choice. She’d decided to put Kara’s well-being first, and she didn’t regret it. But she’d be damned if she let Mike Fucking Matthew take credit for a project she had worked on for years. A project that meant the world to her. Maybe her life was nothing but a little sob story, but it was her little sob story. Her heart may be broken, but her brain was doing just fine.

    Kara had said that the reason most professors hadn’t bothered to reply, perhaps even read her email, was that she was a student. So she followed her advice: she emailed Dr. Daxam and asked her to introduce her to all the researchers she’d previously contacted, plus the two people who’d been on her panel and had shown interest in her work. Dr. Daxam was close to retirement and had more or less given up on producing science, but she was still a full professor at Stanford. It had to mean something.

    Then Lena googled extensively about research ethics, plagiarism, and theft of ideas. The issue was a little murky, given that Lena had—quite recklessly, she now realized— described all her protocols in detail in her report for Mike. But once she began examining the situation with a clearer head, she decided that it wasn’t as dire as she’d initially thought. The report she’d written, after all, was well-structured and thorough. What she decided to focus on was that despite all his insults and rude comments, Mike, one of the top cancer researchers in the United States, had expressed interest in stealing her research ideas. She took it as a very, very backhanded compliment.

    “Shit!” Lena covered her mouth with her hand. “I am so sorry. How did it go?”

    “Perfect.” Andrea’s eyes were shiny with happiness. “We had such great attendance, and everyone loved it. We’re thinking of making this a yearly thing, and formally establishing an organization. Peer-to-peer mentoring! Hear this: every grad is assigned two undergrads. Once they get into grad school, they mentor two more undergrads each. And in ten years we take over the entire damn world.”

    “I am, aren’t I? Okay, now’s your turn to grovel. Aaand, go.”

    “I haven’t.”

    “Yes, Lee, you have.” Andrea’s eyes were earnest, burning into hers, and it occurred to Lena then, the enormity of what had happened. Of what Mike had done.

    She took a shuddering breath. “If the situation were inverted, I would want to know. But it’s different.”

    “Why is it different?”

    Because I’m in love with Kara. And she’s not in love with me. Lena massaged her temples, trying to think against the mounting headache. “I don’t want to take something she loves away from her. Kara respects and admires Mike, and I know Mike’s had Kara’s back in the past. Maybe she’s better off not knowing.”

    “If only there were a way to find out what Kara would prefer,” Sam said.

    Lena sniffled in response. “Yeah.”

    “If only there were someone who knows Kara very well that we might ask,” Sam said, louder this time.

    “Yeah,” Andrea repeated, “that would be great. But there isn’t, so—”

    “If only there were someone in this room who recently started dating Kara’s closest friend of about two decades,” Sam near-yelled, full of passive-aggressive indignity, and Andrea and Lena exchanged a wide-eyed look.

    “Alex!”

    “You could ask Alex for advice!”

    Sam huffed. “You two can be so smart and yet so slow.”

    Lena suddenly recalled something. “Alex hates Mike.”

    “Uh? Why does she hate him?”

    “I don’t know.” She shrugged. “Kara wrote it off as some odd personality quirk of Alex’s, but—”

    “Hey. My woman’s personality is perfect.”

    “Maybe there is something else?”

    Andrea nodded energetically. “Sam, where can Lena find Alex right this minute?”

    “I don’t know. But”—Sam tapped her phone with a smug smile—“I happen to have her number right here.”

    —

    Alex (or Alex Bubblebutt, as Sam had saved her in her contacts) was just finishing up her talk. Lena caught the last five minutes of it—something about crystallography she neither understood nor wanted to—and was totally unsurprised by how smooth and charismatic a speaker Alex was.

    Lena approached Alex on the podium once she was done answering questions, and Alex smiled when she noticed her walk up the stairs, seeming genuinely happy to see her.

    “Lena. My new roommate-in-law!”

    “Right. Yes. Um, great talk.” Lena ordered herself to stop wringing her hands. “I wanted to ask you a question . . .”

    “Is it about the nucleic acids in the fourth slide? Because I totally BS’d my way through them. My Ph.D. student made the figure, and she’s way smarter than me. Don’t tell my mum that though, Eliza Danvers would not be too happy.”

    “I wouldn’t. The question is about Kara—” Alex’s expression brightened. “Well, actually, it’s about Mike Matthew.” It darkened just as quickly.

    “What about Mike?”

    Right. What about Mike, precisely? Lena wasn’t quite sure how to approach the topic. She wasn’t even sure what she meant to ask. Sure, she could have barfed up her entire life story for Alex and begged her to fix this mess for her, but somehow it didn’t seem like a good idea.

    She racked her brain for a moment, and then landed on: “Did you know that Kara is thinking about moving to Boston?”

    “Yeah.” Alex rolled her eyes and pointed at the tall windows. There were large, ominous clouds threatening to explode with torrential rain. “Who wouldn’t want to move here from California?” she scoffed.

    Lena liked the idea of seasons, but she kept the thought to herself. “Do you think . . . Do you think she’d be happy here?”

    Alex studied her intensely for a minute. “You know, you were already my favourite person that Kara had dated—not that there were many; you’re the only woman who could compete with computational modelling in about a decade—but that question wins you a lifelong number-one plaque.” Alex pondered the matter for a minute. “I think Kara could be happy here—in her own way, of course. Broodingly, unenthusiastically happy. But yes, happy. Provided that you are here, too.” Lena had to stop herself from snorting. “Provided that Mike behaves.”

    “Why do you say that? About Mike? I . . . I don’t mean to pry, but you told me to watch my back with him in Stanford. You . . . don’t like him?”

    Alex sighed. “It’s not that I don’t like him—even though I don’t. It’s more that I don’t trust him.”

    “Why, though? Kara told me about the things Mike did for her when your adviser was abusive.”

    “See, this is where a big part of my mistrust comes in.” Alex worried at her lower lip, as if deciding whether and how to continue. “Did Mike intercede to save Kara’s ass on numerous occasions? Sure. It’s undeniable. But how did those occasions come about to begin with? Our adviser was a piece of work, but he was not a micromanager. By the time we joined his lab, he was too busy being a famous asshole to know what was going on in day-to-day lab business. Which is why he had postdocs like Mike mentor grad students like Kara and me and de facto run the lab. And yet, he knew about every single minor screwup of Kara’s. Every few weeks he’d come in, tell Kara that she was a failure for minor stuff like switching reagents or dropping a beaker, and then Mike, our adviser’s most-trusted postdoc, would publicly intervene on behalf of Kara and save the day. The pattern was eerily specific, and only for Kara— who was by far the most promising student in our program. Destined for greatness, youngest member of the science guild on Krypton and all that. Initially, it made me a bit suspicious that Mike was purposefully sabotaging Kara. But in recent years I’ve been wondering if what he wanted was something else altogether…”

    “Did you tell Kara?”

    “Yes. But I had no proof, and Kara…well, you know her. She is stubbornly, unwaveringly loyal, and she was more than a little grateful to Mike. At one point I thought they might be into each other but thank God I was wrong because…ew. They ended up becoming close friends though.”

    “Did it bother you?”

    “Not per se, no. I realize I might sound jealous of their friendship, but the truth is that Kara has always been too focused and single-minded to have many friends. I’d have been happy for her, truly. But Mike . . .”

    Lena nodded. Yeah. Mike. “Why would he do this? This . . . weird vendetta against Kara?”

    Alex sighed. “This is why Kara dismissed my concerns. There really isn’t an obvious reason. The truth is, I don’t think Mike hates Kara. Or at least, I don’t think it’s that simple. But I do believe that Mike is smart, and very, very cunning. That there probably is some jealousy involved, some desire to take advantage of Kara, to maybe control or have power over her. Kara tends to downplay her accomplishments, but she’s one of the best scientists of our generation. Having influence over her…that’s a privilege, and no small feat.”

    “Yeah.” Lena nodded again. The question, the one she’d come here to ask, was starting to take shape in her mind. “Knowing all of this. Knowing how important Mike is to Kara, if you had proof of…of how Mike really is, would you show Kara?”

    To Alex’s credit, she didn’t ask what the proof was, or proof of what. She scanned Lena’s face with an intent, thoughtful expression, and when she spoke, her words were careful. “I can’t answer that for you. I don’t think I should.” Alex drummed her fingers on the podium, as if deep in thought. “But I do want to tell you three things. The first you probably already know: Kara is first and foremost a scientist. So am I, and so are you. And good science only happens when we draw conclusions based on all available evidence—not just the ones that are easy, or that confirm our hypotheses. Wouldn’t you agree?” Lena nodded, and Alex continued. “The second is something you may or may not be aware of, because it has to do with politics and academia, which are not easy to fully grasp until you find yourself sitting through five-hour-long faculty meetings every other week. But here’s the deal: the collaboration between Kara and Mike benefits Mike more than it does Kara. Which is why Kara is the main investigator of the grant they were awarded. Mike is . . . well, replaceable. Don’t get me wrong, he’s a very good scientist, but most of his fame is due to him having been our former adviser’s best and brightest. He inherited a lab that was an already well-oiled machine and kept it going. Kara created her own research line from the ground up, and . . . I think she tends to forget how good she is. Which is probably for the best, because she’s already pretty insufferable.” Alex huffed. “Can you imagine if she had a big ego, too?”

    Lena laughed at that, and the sound came out oddly wet. When she raised her hands to her cheeks, she was not surprised to find them glistening. Apparently, weeping silently was her new baseline state.

    “The last thing,” Alex continued, unbothered by the waterworks, “is something you probably do not know.” She paused. “Kara has been recruited by a lot of institutions in the past. A lot. She’s been offered money, prestigious positions, unlimited access to facilities and equipment. That includes Harvard—this year was not their first attempt at bringing her in. But it’s the first time she’s agreed to interview. And she only agreed after you decided to go work in Mike’s lab.” Alex gave Lena a gentle smile, and then looked away, beginning to collect her things and slide them inside her backpack. “Make of that what you will, Lena.”

    Chapter End Notes

    The Exposing

    Chapter Notes

    HYPOTHESIS: People who cross me will come to regret it.

    Lena had to lie.

    Again.

    It was becoming a bit of a habit, and while she spun an elaborate tale for the secretary of Harvard’s biology department, one in which she was a grad student of Dr. Zor-El’s who needed to track her down immediately to relay a crucial message in person, she swore to herself that this would be the last time. It was too stressful. Too difficult. Not worth the strain on her cardiovascular and psychophysical health. Plus, she sucked at it.

    The department secretary didn’t look like she believed a word of what Lena said, but she must have decided that there was no harm in telling her where the biology faculty had taken Kara out for dinner—according to Yelp, a fancy restaurant that was less than a ten-minute Uber ride away.

    Lena looked down at her ripped jeans and lilac Converse and wondered if they’d let her in. Then she wondered if Kara would be mad. Then she wondered if she was making a mistake and screwing up her own life, Kara’s life, her Uber driver’s life. She was very tempted to change her destination to the conference hotel when the car pulled up to the curb, and the driver—Nyxly, according to the app— turned around with a smile. “Here we are.”

    “Thank you.” Lena started getting out of the passenger seat and found that she couldn’t move her legs.

    “Are you okay?” Nyxly asked.

    “Yeah. Just, un . . .”

    “Are you gonna puke in my car?”

    Lena shook her head. No. Yes. “Maybe?”

    “Don’t, or I’ll destroy your rating.” Lena nodded and tried to slide out of the seat. Her limbs were still nonresponsive. Nyxly frowned. “Hey, what’s wrong?”

    “I just . . .” There was a lump in her throat. “I need to do a thing. That I don’t want to do.”

    Nyxly hummed. “Is it a work thing, or a love thing?”

    “Uh . . . both.”

    “Yikes.” Nyxly scrunched up her nose. “Double threat. Can you put it off?”

    “No, not really.”

    “Can you ask someone else to do it for you?”

    “No.”

    “Can you change your name, cauterize your fingertips, enter the witness protection program, and disappear?”

    “Um, not sure. I’m not an American citizen, though.”

    “Probably no, then. Can you say ‘fuck it’ and deal with the consequences?”

    Lena closed her eyes and thought about it. What, exactly, would the consequences be if she didn’t do what she was planning to? Mike would be free to keep on being an absolute piece of shit, for one. And Kara would never know that she was being taken advantage of. Kara would move to Boston and Lena would never have a chance to talk to her again, and all that she’d meant to her would end . . . In a lie. A lie, after a lot of lies. So many lies she’d told, so many true things she could have said but never did, all because she’d been too scared of the truth, of driving the people she loved away from her. All because she’d been afraid to lose them. All because she hadn’t wanted to be alone again. Well, the lying hadn’t worked out too well. In fact, it had downright sucked lately. Time for plan B, then. Time for some truth.

    “No. I don’t want to deal with the consequences.”

    Nyxly smiled. “Then, my friend, you better go do your thing.” She pressed a button, and the passenger door unlocked with a clunk. “And you better give me a perfect rating. For the free psychotherapy.”

    This time, Lena managed to get out of the car. She tipped Nyxly 150 percent, took a deep breath, and made her way into the restaurant.

    She found Kara immediately. Kara was big, after all, and the restaurant was not, which made for a pretty quick search. Not to mention that Kara was sitting with about ten people who looked a lot like very serious Harvard professors. And, of course, Mike. Fuck my life, she thought, slipping past the busy hostess and walking toward Kara.

    She figured that her bright red duffle coat would attract Kara’s attention, then she’d gesticulate for her to check her phone, and text her to please, please, please give her five minutes of her time when dinner was over. She figured that telling Kara tonight was the best option—her interview would be over tomorrow, and Kara would be able to make her decision with the truth at her disposal.

    She figured her plan might work. She had not figured that Kara would notice her while in conversation with a young, beautiful faculty member. She had not figured that Kara would suddenly stop speaking, eyes widening and lips parting; that she’d mutter “Excuse me” while staring at Lena and stand from the table, ignoring the curious looks in her direction; that she’d march to the entrance, where Lena was, with quick, long strides and a concerned expression.

    “Lena are you okay?” Kara asked her, and— Oh. Her voice. And her eyes. And the way her hands came up, as if to touch Lena, to make sure that she was intact and really there—though right before her fingers could close around Lena’s biceps she hesitated and let them fall back to her sides. It broke Lena’s heart a little.

    “I’m fine.” Lena attempted a smile. “I . . . I’m sorry to interrupt this. I know it’s important, that you want to move to Boston, and—this is inappropriate. But it’s now or never, and I wasn’t sure if I’d have the courage to . . .” She was rambling. So she took a deep breath and started again. “I need to tell you something. Something that happened. With—”

    “Hey, Lena.”

    Mike. But of course. “Hi, Mike.” Lena held Kara’s gaze and didn’t look at him. He did not deserve to be looked at. “Can you give us a minute of privacy?” She could see his oily, fake smile with the corner of her eye.

    “Lena, I know you’re young and don’t know how these things work, but Kara’s here to interview for a very important position, and she can’t just—”

    “Leave,” Kara ordered, voice low and cold.

    Lena closed her eyes and nodded, taking a step back. Fine. It was fine. It was Kara’s right not to talk to her.

    “Okay. I’m sorry, I—”

    “Not you. Mike, leave us.”

    Oh. Oh. Well, then.

    “Kar,” Mike said, sounding amused, “you can’t just get up from the table in the middle of an interview dinner and—”

    “Leave,” Kara repeated.

    Mike laughed, brazen. “No. Not unless you’re coming with me. We’re collaborators, and if you act like an asshole during a dinner with my department because of some student you’re screwing, it will reflect poorly on me. You need to come back to the table and—”

    “A pretty girl like you should know the score by now. Don’t lie to me and say you didn’t pick out a dress that short for my benefit. Nice legs, by the way. I can see why Kara’s wasting her time with you.”

    Neither Kara nor Mike had seen Lena take out her phone, or press Play. They both struggled for a second, confused— they’d clearly heard the words but were unsure where they came from. Until the recording restarted.

    “Lena, you don’t think I accepted you into my lab because you are good, do you? A girl like you. Who figured out so early in her academic career that fucking well-known, successful scholars is how to get ahead. You fucked Kara, didn’t you? We both know you’re going to fuck me for the same reason.”

    “What the—” Mike took a step forward, hand extended to grab the phone from Lena. He didn’t get far, because Kara pushed him away with a palm on his chest, making him stumble several steps back.

    Kara still wasn’t looking at Mike. And not at Lena, either. She was staring down at Lena’s phone, something dark and dangerous and frighteningly still in her expression. Lena should have probably been scared. Maybe she was, a little.

    “—you’re telling me you thought your pitiful abstract was selected for a talk because of its quality and scientific importance? Someone here has a very high opinion of herself, considering that her research is useless and derivative and that she can barely put together two words without stuttering like an idiot—”

    “It was him,” Kara whispered. Her voice was low, barely a whisper, deceptively calm. Her eyes, unreadable. “It was Mike. The reason you were crying.”

    Lena could only nod. In the background, Mike’s recorded voice droned on and on. Talking about how mediocre she was. How Kara would never believe her. Calling her names.

    “This is ridiculous.” Mike was coming closer again, reattempting to take the phone away. “I’m not sure what this bitch’s problem is, but she’s clearly—”

    Kara exploded so fast, Lena didn’t even see her move. One moment Kara stood in front of her, and the next Kara was pinning Mike against the wall with her left hand and her right fist connecting with Mike’s jaw.

    “I’m going to kill you,” Kara gritted out, little more than a growl. “If you say another word about the woman I love, if you look at her, if you even think about her—I’m going to take this bracelet off my hand and fucking kill you then throw your useless body into the sun.”

    “Kara—” Mike choked out.

    “Actually, I will kill you anyway.”

    People were running toward them. The hostess, a waiter, a few faculty members from Kara’s table. They were forming a crowd, yelling in confusion and trying to pull Kara off Mike —with no success.

    Lena’s mind went to Kara pushing Cherie’s truck, and she almost laughed in a moment of hysteria. Almost.

    “Kara,” Lena called. Her voice was barely audible in the chaos going on around them, but it was what got through to Kara. Kara turned to look at her, and there were entire worlds in her eyes. “Kara, don’t,” she whispered. “He’s not worth it.”

    Kara took a step back and let Mike go. An elderly gentleman—probably a Harvard dean—began laying into Kara, asking for explanations, telling her how unacceptable her behaviour was. Kara ignored him, and everyone else. She headed straight for Lena, and— She cradled Lena’s head with both hands, fingers sliding through Lena’s hair and holding her tight as she lowered her forehead to Lena’s.

    Kara was warm, and smelled like herself, like safe and home. Her thumbs swept through the mess of tears on Lena’s cheeks. “I’m sorry. I’m so sorry. I didn’t know, and I’m sorry, I’m sorry, I’m sorry—”

    “It’s not your fault,” Lena managed to mumble, but Kara didn’t seem to hear her.

    “I’m sorry. I’m—”

    “Dr. Zor-El,” a male voice boomed loudly from behind them, and Lena felt Kara’s body stiffen against hers. “I demand an explanation.” Kara paid no heed to the man, and kept holding Lena “Dr. Zor-El,” he repeated, “this is unacceptable—”

    “Darling,” Lena whispered. “You have to answer him.”

    Kara exhaled. Then she pressed a long, lingering kiss to Lena’s forehead before reluctantly disentangling herself.

    When Lena was finally able to get a good look at Kara, she seemed more like her usual self. Calm. Angry at the entire world. In charge.

    “Send me that recording immediately,” Kara murmured at Lena. Lena nodded, and Kara turned to the elderly man who’d just approached them. “We need to talk. Privately. Your office?” The other man looked shocked and offended, but he nodded stiffly. Behind him, Mike was holding his face and making a fuss. Kara clenched her jaw, “Keep him away from me.” Kara turned to Lena before leaving, bending closer to her and lowering her voice. Her palm was warm against Lena’s elbow. “I am going to take care of this,” she told Lena. There was something determined, earnest in her eyes. Lena had never felt safer, or more loved. “And then I’ll come find you, and I’ll take care of you.”

    Chapter End Notes

    The Double-Date

    Chapter Notes

    Okay, so I guess the new chapter 23 was not a good idea. People wanted the first version and thought that the second version was too fast so here is the slow version where Lena and Kara continue being oblivious idiots. Yes, it's just the second half of the other chapter 23 with minor changes and no smut, but this is the version y'all wanted so enjoy.

    HYPOTHESIS: Wearing expired contact lenses will cause bacterial and/or fungal infections that will have repercussions for years to come.

    “Alex sent a message for you.”

    Lena looked away from the window and to Sam, who’d turned off airplane mode the second they’d landed in Charlotte for their layover.

    “Alex?”

    “Yeah. Well, it’s technically from Zor-El.” Lena’s heart skipped a beat. “She lost her phone charger and can’t text you, but she and Alex are on their way back to SFO.”

    “Ah.” Lena nodded, feeling a small rush of relief. That explained Kara’s silence. Kara hadn’t been in touch since last night. Lena had worried that Kara had been arrested and was pondering emptying her savings account to help cover her bail. All twelve dollars and sixteen cents. “Where’s their layover?”

    “No layover.” Sam rolled her eyes. “Direct flight. They’ll be at SFO ten minutes after us, even though they’re only now leaving Boston. Eat the rich.”

    “Did Alex say anything about . . .”

    Sam shook her head. “Their plane is about to leave, but we can wait for them at SFO. I’m sure Kara will have some updates for you.”

    “You just want to make out with Alex, don’t you?”

    Sam smiled and leaned her head against Lena’s shoulder. “You know me well.”

    It seemed impossible that Lena had been gone for less than a week. That all the chaos had unfolded in the span of a few days. She felt dazed, shell-shocked, as though her brain was winded from running a marathon. She was tired and wanted to sleep. She was hungry and wanted to eat. She was angry and wanted to see Mike get what he deserved. She was anxious, as twitchy as a damaged nerve, and she wanted a hug. Preferably from Kara.

    In San Francisco, she folded her now-useless coat inside her suitcase and then sat on it. She checked her phone for new messages while Sam went to buy a bottle of Diet Coke. There were several from Andrea, just checking in from Boston, and one from her landlord about the elevator being out of commission. She rolled her eyes, switched to her academic email, and found several unread messages flagged as important. She tapped on the red exclamation point and opened one.

    Today, 5:15 p.m.
    FROM: Felicity-Smoak@berkeley.edu
    TO: Rhea-Daxam@stanford.edu
    CC: Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu
    SUBJECT: Re: Pancreatic Cancer Project

    Rhea,
    Thank you for reaching out to me. I had the privilege of seeing Lena Luthor’s talk at SBD—we were on the same panel—and I was very impressed with her work on early detection tools for pancreatic cancer. I’d love to have her in my lab next year! Maybe the three of us can chat more on the phone soon?

    Best,
    Felicity

    Lena gasped. She covered her mouth with her hand, and immediately opened another email.

    Today, 3:19 p.m.
    FROM: Raymond-Palmer@umn.edu
    TO: Rhea-Daxam@stanford.edu, Lena-Luthor@stanford.edu
    SUBJECT: Pancreatic Cancer Project

    Dr. Daxam, Ms. Luthor, Your work on pancreatic cancer is fascinating, and I would welcome the opportunity for a collaboration. We should set up a Zoom meeting.

    -R.P

    There were two more emails. Four total from cancer researchers, all following up on Dr. Daxam’s introductory message and saying they’d love having Lena in their labs. She felt a surge of happiness so violent, it almost made her dizzy.

    “Lee, look who I ran into.”

    Lena shot up to her feet. Sam was there, holding Alex’s hand, and barely a step behind them— Kara. Looking tired, and beautiful, and as large in real life as she’d been in Lena’s mind for the past twenty-four hours. Looking straight at her. Lena recalled the words Kara had said last night in the restaurant and felt her cheeks heat, her chest constrict, her heart beat out of her skin.

    “Hear me out,” Alex started without even saying hi, “the four of us: double date. Tonight.”

    Kara ignored her and came to stand next Lena. “How are you?” she asked in a low tone.

    “Good.” For the first time in days, it wasn’t even a lie. Kara was here. And all those emails were in her inbox. “You?”

    “Good,” Kara replied with a half smile, and Lena had a weird feeling that much like her, Kara wasn’t lying.

    “What about Chinese?” Alex interjected. “Everyone like Chinese here?”

    “I’m cool with Chinese,” Sam muttered, though she didn’t seem enthusiastic at the idea of a double date. Likely because she didn’t want to sit across from Kara for an entire meal and relive the trauma of her graduate advisory committee meetings.

    “Lena?”

    “Um . . . I like Chinese.”

    “Perfect. So does Kara, so—”

    “I’m not having dinner out,” Kara said.

    Alex frowned. “Why?”

    “I have better things to do.”

    “Like what? Lena’s coming, too.”

    “Leave Lena alone. She’s tired, and we’re busy.”

    “I have access to your Google Calendar, asshole. You’re not busy. If you don’t want to hang out with me, you can just be honest.”

    “I don’t want to hang out with you.”

    “You little shit. After the week we just had. And on my birthday.”

    Kara recoiled slightly. “What? It’s not your birthday.”

    “Yes, it is.”

    “Your birthday is October fifteenth.”

    “Is it, though?”

    Kara closed her eyes, scratching her forehead. “Alex, we’ve talked daily for the past two decades, and I have been to at least five Terminator–themed birthday parties of yours. The last one was when you turned thirty.” Sam attempted to cover her laugh with a cough. “I know when your birthday is.”

    “You always had it wrong, I was just too nice to tell you.” Alex clasped Kara’s shoulder. “So, Chinese to celebrate the blessing of my birth?”

    “Why not Thai?” Sam interjected, addressing Alex and ignoring Kara.

    Alex made a whiny noise and started saying something about the lack of good larb in Stanford, something Lena would have normally been interested in hearing, except that— Kara was looking at her again. From several inches above Alex’s and Sam’s heads, Kara was looking at her with an expression that was half apologetic, half annoyed, and . . . all intimate, really. Something familiar they’d shared before. Lena felt something inside her melt and suppressed a smile. Suddenly, dinner seemed like a great idea. It will be fun, she mouthed at Kara while Sam and Alex were busy arguing about whether they should just try that new burger place. It will be excruciating, Kara mouthed back barely parting her lips, looking resigned and put-upon and just so amazingly Kara that Lena couldn’t help but burst into laughter.

    Alex and Sam stopped arguing and turned to her. “What?”

    “Nothing,” Lena said. The corner of Kara’s mouth was curling up, too.

    “Why are you laughing, Lee?” She opened her mouth to deflect, but Kara beat her to it.

    “Fine. We’ll go.” Kara said ‘we’ like she and Lena were a ‘we,’ like it had never been fake after all, and Lena’s breath caught in her throat. “But I’m excused from any birthday-related outings for the next year. Actually, make it the next two.”

    Alex fist-pumped the air.

    —

    “We should start by addressing the obvious,” Alex said, chewing on the complimentary appetizers, and Lena tensed in her seat. She wasn’t sure she wanted to discuss the Mike situation with Sam and Alex before talking about it with Kara alone. As it turned out, she shouldn’t have worried. “Which is that Sam and Kara hate each other.”

    Next to her in the booth, Kara frowned in confusion. Sam, who was sitting across from Lena, covered her face with her palms and groaned.

    “I am reliably informed,” Alex continued, undeterred, “that Kara called Sam’s experiments ‘sloppy’ and ‘a misuse of research funds’ during a committee meeting, and that Sam took offense to that. Now, Kara, I’ve been telling Sam that you were probably just having a bad day —maybe one of your grads had split an infinitive in an email, or your arugula salad wasn’t organic enough. Do you have anything to say for yourself?”

    “Uh . . .” Kara’s frown deepened, and so did Sam’s facepalm. Alex waited pointedly for an answer, and Lena watched it all unfold, wondering if she should take out her phone and film this car crash. “I have no recollection of that committee meeting. Though it does sound like something I would say.”

    “Great. Now tell Sam it wasn’t personal, so we can move on and have fried rice.”

    “Oh my God,” Sam muttered. “Alex, please.”

    “I’m not having fried rice,” Kara said.

    “You can have raw bamboo while the normal people have fried rice. But as of right now, my girlfriend thinks that her BFF’s girlfriend and my own BFF has it out for her, and it’s cramping my double-dating style, so please.”

    Kara blinked slowly. “BFF?”

    “Kara.” Alex pointed at a grimacing Sam with her thumb. “Now, please.”

    Kara sighed heavily, but she turned to Sam. “Whatever I said or did, it was not personal. I’ve been told that I can be needlessly antagonistic. And unapproachable.”

    Lena didn’t get to see Sam’s reaction because she was busy studying Kara and the slight curl on Kara’s lips, the one that became an almost smile when she looked at her and met her eyes. For a second, the brief second she held Kara’s gaze before she looked away, it was just the two of them. And this sort-of-past they shared, their stupid inside jokes, the way they’d teased each other in the late-summer sunlight.

    “Perfect.” Alex clapped her hands, intrusively loud. “Egg rolls for appetizer, yes?”

    It was a good idea, this dinner. This night, this table, this moment. Sitting next to Kara, smelling the petrichor, watching the dark splotches on the grey cotton of her cardigan from the storm that had started just as they’d slipped inside the restaurant. They would have to talk, later, have a serious conversation about Mike and many other things. But for now it was the way it had always been between Kara and her: like slipping into a favourite dress, one she’d thought lost inside her closet, and finding that it fit as comfortably as it used to.

    “I want egg rolls.” Lena glanced at Kara. “I’m going to take a wild guess and assume that you hate egg rolls, just like everything else that’s good in the world.”

    Kara mouthed smart-ass right as the waiter brought their waters and set the menus on the table. Three menus, to be precise. Alex and Sam each took one, and Lena and Kara exchanged a loaded, amused look and grabbed the remaining one to share. It worked perfectly: Kara angled it so that the veggie section was on her side and all manner of fried entrées were on Lena’s. It was serendipitous enough that Lena let out a laugh. Kara tapped her index finger on the drink section.

    “Look at this abomination,” Kara murmured. Her lips were close to Lena’s ear—a chuff of hot air, intimate and pleasant in the blasting AC.

     

    Lena grinned. “No way.”

    “Appalling.”

    “Amazing, you mean.”

    “I do not.”

    “This is my new favourite restaurant.”

    “You haven’t even tried it yet.”

    “It will be spectacular.”

    “It will be horrific—”

    A throat cleared, reminding them that they were not alone. Sam and Alex were both staring—Sam with a shrewd, suspicious expression, and Alex with a knowing smile.

    “What’s all that about?”

    “Oh.” Lena’s cheeks warmed a little. “Nothing. They just have pumpkin spice bubble tea.”

    Sam pretended to gag. “Ugh, Lee. Gross.”

    “Shut up.”

    “It sounds great.” Alex smiled and leaned into Sam. “We should get one to split.”

    “Excuse me?”

    Lena tried not to laugh at Sam’s horrified expression. “Don’t get Sam started on pumpkin spice,” she told Alex in an exaggerated whisper.

    “Oh, shit.” Alex clutched her chest in mock terror. “This is a serious matter.”

    Sam let her menu fall on the table. “Pumpkin spice is Satan’s dandruff, harbinger of the apocalypse, and it tastes like ass—not in the good way.” Next to Lena Kara nodded slowly, highly impressed Sam’s rant. “One pumpkin spice latte contains the same amount of sugar you’d find in fifty Skittles—and no pumpkin whatsoever. Look it up.”

    Kara stared at Sam with something very similar to admiration.

    Alex met Lena’s eyes and told her conspiratorially, “Our girlfriends have so much in common.”

    “They do. They think hating entire harmless families of food is a personality trait.”

    “Pumpkin spice is not harmless. It’s a radioactive, overpowering sugar bomb that worms its way into every sort of product and is single-handedly responsible for the extinction of the Caribbean monk seal. And you”—Sam pointed her finger at Alex—“are on thin ice.”

    “What—why?”

    “I can’t date someone who doesn’t respect my stance on pumpkin spice.”

    “To be fair it’s not a very respectable stance—” Alex noticed Sam’s glare and lifted her hands defensively. “I had no idea, babe.”

    “You should have.”

    Kara clucked her tongue, amused. “Yes, Alex. Do better.” She leaned back in her seat, and her shoulder brushed against Lena’s.

    Alex gave Kara the finger.

    “Kara knows and respects Lena’s stance on it, and they’re not even—” Whatever Sam had been about to say, she had the sense to stop herself. “Well, if Kara knows, you should know.”

    “Wasn’t Kara a dick until, like, twelve seconds ago?”

    “How the turntables,” Kara murmured.

    Lena reached out to pinch her on the side, but Kara stopped her with a hand around her wrist. Evil, she mouthed at Kara. Kara just smiled, evilly, studying Sam and Alex a little too gleefully.

    “Come on. It’s not even comparable,” Alex was saying. “Kara and Lena have been together for years. We met less than a week ago.”

    “They have not,” Sam corrected her, wagging a finger. Kara’s hand was still curled around Lena’s wrist. “They started dating, like, a month before we did.”

    “No,” Alex insisted. “Kara was into her for ages. She probably secretly studied her eating habits and compiled seventeen databases and built machine-learning algorithms to predict her culinary preferences—”

    Lena burst into laughter. “She did not.” She took a sip of water, still smiling. “We only just started hanging out. At the beginning of the fall semester.”

    “Yes, but you knew each other from earlier.” Alex was frowning. “You two met the year before you started your Ph.D. here, when you came for your interview, and Kara has been pining after you ever since.”

    Lena shook her head and laughed, turning to Kara to share her amusement. Except that Kara was staring at her already, and she did not look amused. Kara looked . . . something else. Worried maybe, or apologetic, or resigned. Panicky? And just like that, the restaurant was silent. The pitter-patter of rain on the windows, people’s chatter, the clinking of silverware— it all receded; the floor tilted, shook a little, and the AC was just this side of too cold. At some point, Kara’s fingers had let go of her wrist. Lena thought back to the bathroom incident. To burning eyes and wet cheeks, the smell of reagent and clean skin. The blur of a large, dark figure standing in front of her with a deep, reassuring, amused voice. The panic of being twenty-three and alone and having no idea what she should be doing, where she should be going, what the right choice was.

    Is mine a good enough reason to go to grad school?

    It’s the best one.

    All of a sudden, things had seemed simple enough. It had been Kara, after all. She had been right. What she hadn’t been right about was whether Kara remembered her.

    “Yes,” she said. She wasn’t smiling anymore. Kara was still holding her gaze. “I guess she has."

    Chapter End Notes
