    The Truth pt2

    Chapter Notes

    I decided to post this early because I want to be done with this story.

    HYPOTHESIS: When given a choice between A (telling a lie) and B (telling the truth), I will inevitably end up selecting . . . No. Not this time.

    Lena had no doubt that Alex’s tales were highly embellished and the result of years of comedy workshopping, but she still couldn’t help laughing harder than ever before.

    “And I’m awakened by this waterfall pouring down on me —”

    Kara rolled her eyes. “It was a drop.”

    “And I’m asking myself why it’s raining inside the cabin, when I realize that it’s coming from the top bunk and that Kara, who was, like, fifteen at the time had pissed the bed, and the piss was seeping through the mattress and onto me.”

    Lena’s hands flew up to cover her mouth, not quite succeeding at hiding her amusement—just like she’d failed when Alex had recounted that a dalmatian puppy had once bitten Kara’s ass through her jeans, or that Kara had been voted “Most likely to make people cry” in her senior yearbook. At least Kara didn’t act embarrassed, and not nearly as upset as she’d seemed after Alex had talked about her pining for years. Which explained . . . so many things. Everything, maybe.

    “I did not pee in the bed. My water bottle was not closed properly,” Kara defended herself.

    “It was totally pee,” Alex insisted to which Kara just rolled her eyes.

    There was a large bowl of fortune cookies by the register. Lena noticed it on her way out of the restaurant, let out a delighted squeal, and dipped her hand in to fish out four plastic packages. She handed one each to Sam and Alex, and held out another for Kara with a mischievous smile.

    “You hate these, don’t you?”

    “I don’t.” Kara accepted the cookie. “I just think they taste like Styrofoam.”

    “Probably have similar nutritional values, too,” Sam muttered as they slipped out into the chilly humidity of the early night. Surprisingly, Sam and Kara were finding lots of common ground.

    It wasn’t raining anymore, but the street was shiny in the light under a lamppost; a soft breeze made the leaves rustle and stray drops of water scatter to the ground. The air was fresh in Lena’s lungs, pleasantly so after the hours spent in the restaurant. She unrolled her sleeves, accidentally brushing her hand against Kara’s abs. She smiled up at Kara, playfully apologetic; Kara flushed and averted her eyes.

    “ ‘He who laughs at himself never runs out of things to laugh at.’ ” Alex popped a bit of fortune cookie in her mouth, blinking at the message inside. “Is that shade?” She looked around, indignant. “Did this fortune cookie just throw shade at me?”

    “Sounds like it,” Sam answered. “Mine says ‘Why not treat yourself to a good time instead of waiting for somebody else to do it?’ I think my cookie just shaded you, too, babe.”

    “What’s wrong with this batch?” Alex pointed at Kara and Lena. “What do yours say?”

    Lena was already opening hers, nibbling on a corner as she pulled the paper out. It was very banal, and yet her heart skipped beat. “Mine’s normal,” she informed Alex.

    “You’re lying.”

    “Nope.”

    “What does it say?”

    “ ‘It’s never too late to tell the truth.’ ” Lena shrugged, and turned to throw away the plastic wrapper. At the last moment, she decided to keep the strip of paper and slip it inside her jeans’ back pocket.

    “Kara, open yours.”

    “Nah.”

    “Come on.”

    “I’m not going to eat a piece of cardboard because it hurt your feelings.”

    “You’re a shit friend.”

    “According to the fortune cookie industry, you’re a shit girlfriend, so—”

    “Give it here,” Lena interjected, plucking the cookie out of Kara’s hand. “I’ll eat it. And read it.”

    The parking lot was completely empty, save for Kara’s and Sam’s cars. Alex had ridden from the airport with Kara, but she and Sam were planning to spend the night at Alex’s apartment.

    “Kara’s giving you a ride, right, Lee?”

    “No need. It’s less than a ten-minute walk home.”

    “But what about your suitcase?”

    “It’s not heavy, and I—” She stopped abruptly, worried her lip for a second while she contemplated the possibilities, and then felt herself smile, at once tentative and purposeful. “Actually, Kara will walk me home. Right?”

    Kara was silent and inscrutable for a moment. Then she calmly said, “Of course,” slipped her keys in the pocket of her jeans, and slid the strap of Lena’s duffel bag over her shoulder. “Where do you live?” she asked when Alex was not within earshot anymore.

    Lena pointed silently. “You sure you want to carry my bag? I heard it’s easy to throw out your back, once you reach a certain age.”

    Kara glared at her, and Lena laughed, falling into step with her as they headed out of the parking lot. The street was silent, except for the soles of Lena’s Converse catching on the wet concrete and Sam’s car passing them by a few seconds later.

    “Hey,” Alex asked from the passenger window. “What did Kara’s fortune cookie say?”

    “Mmm.” Lena made a show to look at the strip. “Not much. Just ‘Alex Danvers, Ph.D., is a loser.’ ”Sam sped up just as Alex flipped her off, making her burst into laughter.

    “What does it really say?” Kara asked when they were finally alone. Lena handed her the crumpled paper and remained silent as she angled it to read it in the lamplight.

    Lena wasn’t surprised when she saw a muscle jump in Kara’s jaw, or when she slid the fortune into the pocket of her jeans. She knew what it said, after all. You can fall in love: someone will catch you.

    “Can we talk about Mike?” Lena asked, sidestepping a puddle. “We don’t have to, but if we can . . .”

    “We can. We should.” Lena saw Kara’s throat work. “Harvard’s going to fire him, of course. Other disciplinary measures are still being decided—there were meetings until very late last night.” Kara gave her a quick glance. “That’s why I didn’t call you earlier. Harvard’s Title IX coordinator should be in touch with you soon.”

    Good. “What about your grant?”

    Kara’s jaw clenched. “I’m not sure. I’ll figure something out —or not. I don’t particularly care at the moment.”

    It surprised Lena. And then it didn’t, not when she considered that the professional implications of Mike’s betrayal couldn’t have cut as deeply as the personal ones. “I’m sorry, Kara. I know he was your friend—”

    “He wasn’t.” Kara abruptly stopped in the middle of the street. She turned to Lena, her eyes a clear, bright blue. “I had no idea, Lena. I thought I knew him, but…I should never have trusted him with you. I’m sorry.” Kara said it—“with you”—like Lena was something special, uniquely precious to her. Her most beloved treasure. It made Lena want to shiver, and laugh, and weep at the same time. It made her happy and confused.

    “I was . . . I was afraid you might be mad at me. For ruining things. Your relationship with Mike, and maybe . . . maybe you won’t be able to move to Boston anymore.”

    Kara shook her head. “I don’t care. I couldn’t care less about any of it.” She held Lena’s eyes for a long moment, her mouth working as though she was swallowing the rest of her words. But she never continued, so Lena nodded and turned around, starting to walk again.

    “I think I’ve found another lab. To finish my study. Closer, so I won’t have to move next year.” She pushed her hair behind her ear and smiled at Kara. There was something intrinsically enjoyable in having Kara next to her, so physical and undeniable. She felt it on some primal, visceral level, the giddy happiness that always came with his presence. Suddenly, Mike was the last thing she wanted to discuss with Kara. “Dinner was nice. And you were right, by the way.”

    “About the pumpkin sludge?”

    “No, that was amazing. About Alex. She really is insufferable.”

    “She grows on you, after a decade or so.”

    “Does she?”

    “Nah. Not really.”

    “Poor Alex.” Lena huffed out a small laugh. “You weren’t the only one who remembered, by the way.”

    Kara glanced at her. “Remembered what?”

    “Our meeting. The one in the bathroom, when I came to interview.” Lena thought that maybe Kara’s step faltered for a split second. Or maybe it didn’t. Still, there was a tinge of uncertainty in the deep breath Kara took.

    “Did you really?”

    “Yup. It just took me a while to realize that it was you. Why didn’t you say anything?” She was so curious about what had been going on in Kara’s head in the past few days, weeks, years. She was starting to imagine quite a bit, but some things . . . some things Kara would have to clear up for her.

    “Because you introduced yourself like we’d never met before. And I’d been . . . I’d been thinking about you. For years. And I didn’t want to . . .”

    Lena could only imagine. They’d passed each other in the hallways, been at countless department research symposiums and seminars together. She hadn’t thought anything of it, but now . . . now she wondered what Kara had thought. Kara had been going on and on about this amazing girl for years, but she was concerned about being in the same department, Alex had said. And Lena had assumed so much. She had been so wrong.

    “You didn’t need to lie, you know,” Lena said, not accusing.

    Kara adjusted the strap of Lena’s suitcase on her shoulder. “I didn’t.”

    “You sort of did. By omission.”

    “True. Are you . . .” Kara pressed her lips together. “Are you upset?”

    “No, not really. It’s really not that bad a lie.”

    “It’s not?”

    “I’ve said much worse, myself. And I didn’t bring up our meeting, either, even after I made the connection.”

    “Still, if you feel—”

    “I’m not upset,” Lena said, gentle but final. She looked up at Kara, willing Kara to understand. Trying to figure out how to tell her. How to show her. “I am . . . other things.” She smiled. “Glad, for instance. That you remembered me, from that day.”

    “You . . .” A pause. “You are very memorable.”

    “Ha. I’m not, really. I was no one—part of a huge incoming cohort.” Lena snorted and looked down to her feet. Her steps had to be much quicker than Kara’s to keep up with Kara’s longer legs. “I hated my first year. It was so stressful.”

    Kara glanced at her, surprised. “Do you remember your first seminar talk?”

    “I do. Why?”

    “Your elevator pitch—you called it a turbolift pitch. You put a picture from The Next Generation on your slides.”

    “Oh, yes. I did.” Lena let out a low laugh. “I didn’t know you were a Trekkie.”

    “I had a phase. And that year’s picnic, when we got rained on. You were playing freeze tag with someone’s kids for hours. They loved you—they had to physically peel the youngest off you to get her inside the car.”

    “Dr. J’onn’s kids.” Lena looked at Kara curiously. “I didn’t think you liked kids. The opposite, actually.”

    Kara lifted one eyebrow. “I don’t like twenty-five-year-olds who act like toddlers. I don’t mind them if they’re actually three.”

    Lena smiled. “Kara, the fact that you knew who I was . . . Did it have anything to do with your decision to pretend to date me?”

    About a dozen expressions crossed Kara’s face as she looked for an answer, and Lena couldn’t pick apart a single one. “I wanted to help you, Lena.”

    “I know. I believe that. But was that all?”

    Kara pressed her lips together. Exhaled. Closed her eyes, and for a split second looked like she was having her teeth and her soul pulled out. Then she said, resigned, “No.”

    “No,” Lena repeated, pensive. “This is my place, by the way.” She pointed at the tall brick building on the corner.

    “Right.” Kara looked around, studying her street. “Should I carry your bag upstairs?”

    “I . . . Maybe later. There is something I need to tell you. Before.”

    “Of course.” Kara stopped in front of her.

    Lena took a deep breath. “The thing is, Kara…I was stupid. And wrong.” She played nervously with a lock of her hair, then let her hand drift down to her stomach, and—okay. Okay. She was going to tell Kara. She would do this. Now. “It’s like—it’s like statistical hypothesis testing. Type I error. It’s scary, isn’t it?”

    Kara frowned. She could tell Kara had no idea where she was going with this.

    “Type I error?”

    “A false positive. Thinking that something is happening when it’s not.”

    “I know what type I error is—”

    “Yes, of course. It’s just . . . in the past few weeks, what terrified me was the idea that I could misread a situation. That I could convince myself of something that wasn’t true. See something that wasn’t there just because I wanted to see it. A scientist’s worst nightmare, right?”

    “Right.” Kara’s brows furrowed. “That is why in your analyses you set a level of significance that is—”

    “But the thing is, type II error is bad, too.” Lena’s eyes bore into Kara’s, hesitant and urgent all at once. She was frightened—so frightened by what she was about to say. But also exhilarated for Kara to finally know. Determined to get it out.

    “Yes,” Kara agreed slowly, confused. “False negatives are bad, too.”

    “That’s the thing with science. We’re drilled to believe that false positives are bad, but false negatives are just as terrifying.” She swallowed. “Not being able to see something, even if it’s in front of your eyes. Purposefully making yourself blind, just because you’re afraid of seeing too much.”

    “Are you saying that statistics graduate education is inadequate?”

    Lena exhaled a laugh, suddenly flushed, even in the dark cool of the night. Her eyes were starting to sting. “Maybe. But also . . . I think that I have been inadequate. And I don’t want to be, not anymore.”

    “Lena.” Kara took one step closer, just a few inches. Not enough to crowd, but plenty for Lena to feel her warmth. “Are you okay?”

    “There have been . . . so many things that have happened, before I even met you, and I think they messed me up a little. I’ve mostly lived in fear of being alone, and . . . I’ll tell you about them, if you want. First, I have to figure it out on my own, why shielding myself with a bunch of lies seemed like a better idea than admitting even one ounce of truth. But I think . . .” She took a deep, shuddering breath. There was a tear, one single tear that she could feel sliding down her cheek. Kara saw it and mouthed her name. “I think that somewhere along the way I forgot that I was something. I forgot myself.” She was the one who stepped closer. The one who put her hand on the hem of Kara’s cardigan, who tugged gently and held on to it, who started touching Kara and crying and smiling at the same time. “There are two things I want to tell you, Kara.”

    “What can I—”

    “Please. Just let me tell you.” Kara wasn’t very good at it. At standing there and doing nothing while her eyes welled fuller and fuller. She could tell that Kara felt useless, Kara’s hands dangling in fists at her sides, and she . . . she loved Kara even more for it. For looking at her like she was the beginning and end of her every thought. “The first thing is that I lied to you. And my lie was not just by omission.”

    “Lena—”

    “It was a real lie. A bad one. A stupid one. I let you—no, I made you think that I had feelings for someone else, when in truth . . . I didn’t. I never did.”

    Kara’s hand came up to cup the side of Lena’s face. “What do you —”

    “But that’s not very important.”

    “Lena.” Kara pulled her closer, pressing her lips against her forehead. “It doesn’t matter. Whatever it is that you’re crying about, I will fix it. I will make it right. I—”

    “Kara,” Lena interrupted her with a wet smile. “It’s not important, because the second thing, that’s what really matters.” They were so close, now. She could smell Kara’s scent and her warmth, and her hands were cradling her face, thumbs swiping back and forth to dry her cheeks.

    “Sweetheart,” Kara murmured. “What is the second thing?”

    Lena was still crying, but she’d never been happier. So she said it, probably in the worst accent Kara had ever heard. “Khap zhao ripp, Kara.”

    The End

    Chapter Notes

    RESULTS: Careful analyses of the data collected, accounting for potential confounds, statistical error, and experimenter’s bias, show that when I fall in love . . . things don’t actually turn out to be that bad.

    Ten months later

    “Stand there. You were standing right there.”

    “Was I?” Kara was humouring her. A little. That deliciously put-upon expression had become Lena’s favourite over the past year.

    “A bit closer to the water fountain. Perfect.” Lena took a step back to admire her handiwork and then winked at Kara as she took out her phone to snap a quick picture. She briefly considered swapping it for her current screensaver—a selfie of the two of them in National City last week at Lex’s wedding, Kara squinting in the sun and Lena pressing her lips to her cheek—but then thought better of it. This summer had been full of hiking trips, and delicious ice cream, spending time with her stepmother who welcomed her with open arms and a loving heart, learning kryptonese with help from Aunt Astra and Kara, helping Lex with wedding planning, late-night kisses on Kara’s balcony, laughing and sharing untold stories and looking up at the stars, so much brighter than the ones Lena had once climbed on a ladder to stick to the ceiling of her bedroom. She was going to start working at a cancer lab at Berkeley in less than a week, which would mean a busier, more stressful schedule and a bit of a commute. And yet, she couldn’t wait. “Just stand there,” she ordered. “Look antagonistic and unapproachable. And say ‘pumpkin spice.’ ”

    Kara rolled her eyes. “What’s your plan if someone comes in?”

    Lena glanced around the biology building. The hallway was silent and deserted, and the dim after-hours lights made Kara’s hair looked almost brown. It was late, and summer, and the weekend to boot: no one was going to come in. Even if they did, Lena Luthor and Kara Zor-El were old news by now. “Like who?”

    “Andrea might show up. To help you re-create the magic.”

    “Pretty sure she’s out with Jack.”

    “Jack? The guy you’re in love with?”

    Lena stuck her tongue out at Kara and glanced down at her phone. Happy. She was so happy, and she didn’t even know why. Except that she did know. “Okay. In one minute.”

    “You can’t know the exact time.” Kara’s tone was patient and indulgent. “Not to the minute.”

    “Wrong. I ran a Western blot that night. I looked at my lab logs, and I reconstructed both the when and the where down to the error bars. I am a thorough scientist.”

    “Hm.” Kara folded her arms across her chest. “How did that blotting turn out?”

    “Not the point.” Lena grinned. “What were you doing here, by the way?”

    “What do you mean?”

    “A year ago. Why were you walking around the department at night?”

    “I can’t remember. Maybe I had a deadline. Or maybe I was going home.” Kara shrugged, and scanned the hallway until her eyes fell on the water fountain. “Maybe I was thirsty.”

    “Maybe.” Lena took a step closer. “Maybe you were secretly hoping for a kiss.”

    Kara gave her a long, amused look. “Maybe.”

    Lena took another step, and another, and another. And then her alarm beeped, once, right as she came to stand in front of Kara. Another intrusion of Kara’s personal space. But this time, when she pushed up on her toes, when she wrapped her arms around Karas neck, Kara’s hands pulled her deeper into herself. It had been one year. Exactly one year. And by now Kara’s body was so familiar to her. She sank into Kara, let Kara support her weight, and then moved until her mouth was almost level with Kara’s ear. She pressed her lips against its shell and whispered softly into Kara’s skin. “May I kiss you, Dr. Zor-El?”
